The purpose of this chapter is to state conditions on $\mathfrak{X}$ and $\mathfrak{X}$ which ensure the existence of an isometry $\tau$ that maps suitable subsets of the character ring of $\mathfrak{L}$ into the character ring of $\mathfrak{X}$ and has certain additional properties. If $\alpha$ is in the character ring of $\mathfrak{L}$ and $\alpha^{\tau}$ is defined then these additional properties will yield information concerning $\alpha^{\tau}(L)$ for some elements $L$ of $\mathfrak{L}$. Once the existence of $\tau$ is established it will enable us to derive information about certain generalized characters of $\mathfrak{X}$ provided we know something about the character ring of $\mathfrak{L}$. In this way it is possible to get global information about $\mathfrak{X}$ from local information about $\mathfrak{L}$.

There are two stages in establishing the existence of $\tau$. First we will require that $\mathfrak{L}$ is in some sense "nicely" imbedded in $\mathfrak{X}$. When this requirement is fulfilled it is possible to define $\alpha^{\tau}$ for certain generalized characters $\alpha$ of $\mathfrak{X}$ with $\alpha(1)=0$. In this situation $\alpha^{\tau}$ is explicitly defined in terms of induced characters of various subgroups of $\mathfrak{X}$. Secondly it is necessary that the character ring of $\mathfrak{X}$ have certain special properties. These properties make it possible to extend the definition of $\tau$ to a wider domain. In particular it is then possible to define $\alpha^{\tau}$ for some generalized characters $\alpha$ of $\mathfrak{X}$ with $\alpha(1)\neq0$. The precise conditions that the character ring of $\mathfrak{X}$ needs to satisfy will be stated later. In this section we are concerned with the imbedding of $\mathfrak{X}$ in $\mathfrak{X}$. The following definition is appropriate.

DEFINITION 9.1. Let $\hat{\mathfrak{X}}$ be a subset of the group $\mathfrak{X}$ such that

$$
\langle 1 \rangle \subseteq \widehat {\mathfrak {L}} \subseteq N (\widehat {\mathfrak {L}}) = \mathfrak {L}.\tag{9.1}
$$

Let $\mathfrak{L}_0$ be the set of elements $L$ in $\hat{\mathfrak{L}}$ such that $C(L) \subseteq \mathfrak{L}$, and let $\mathfrak{D} = \hat{\mathfrak{L}}^* - \mathfrak{L}_0$.

We say that $\hat{\mathfrak{L}}$ is tamely imbedded in $\mathfrak{X}$ if the following conditions are satisfied:

(i) If two elements of $\mathfrak{L}$ are conjugate in $\mathfrak{X}$, they are conjugate in $\mathfrak{L}$.

(ii) If $\mathfrak{D}$ is non empty, then there are non identity subgroups $\mathfrak{Q}_1, \cdots, \mathfrak{Q}_n$ of $\mathfrak{X}, n \geq 1$, with the following properties:

(a) $(|\mathfrak{H}_i|, |\mathfrak{H}_j|) = 1$ for $i \neq j$;

(b) $\mathfrak{H}_i$ is a $S$-subgroup of $\mathfrak{N}_i = N(\mathfrak{H}_i)$;

(c) $\mathfrak{N}_i = \mathfrak{H}_i(\mathfrak{L} \cap \mathfrak{N}_i)$ and $\mathfrak{H}_i \cap \mathfrak{L} = 1$;

(d) (| $\mathfrak{Q}_i$, $|\mathbf{C}_{\mathfrak{L}}(L)|$ = 1 for $L \in \widehat{\mathfrak{L}}^*$;

(e) For $1 \leq i \leq n$, define

$$
\hat {\mathfrak {N}} _ {i} = \left\{\bigcup_ {H \in \mathfrak {H} _ {i} ^ {\sharp}} C _ {\mathfrak {N} _ {i}} (H) \right\} - \mathfrak {H} _ {i} ^ {\sharp}.
$$

Then $\hat{\mathfrak{N}}_i^*$ is a non empty T. I. set in $\mathfrak{X}$ and $\mathfrak{N}_i = N(\hat{\mathfrak{N}}_i)$.

(iii) If $L_0 \in \mathfrak{D}$, then there is a conjugate $L$ of $L_0$ in $\widehat{\mathfrak{D}}$ and an index $i$ such that

$$
\boldsymbol {C} (L) = \boldsymbol {C} _ {\mathfrak {H} _ {i}} (L) \cdot \boldsymbol {C} _ {\mathfrak {L}} (L) \subseteq \mathfrak {N} _ {i}.
$$

If $\hat{\mathfrak{L}}$ is a tamely imbedded subset of $\mathfrak{X}$ then for $1 \leq i \leq n$, each of the groups $\mathfrak{H}_i$ is called a supporting subgroup of $\hat{\mathfrak{L}}$. The collection $\{\mathfrak{H}_i | 1 \leq i \leq n\}$ is called a system of supporting subgroups of $\hat{\mathfrak{L}}$.

In one important special case, the definition of tamely imbedded subset of $\mathfrak{X}$ is fairly easy to master. Namely, if $\mathfrak{D}$ is empty, the reader can check that $\hat{\mathfrak{L}}$ is a T. I. set.

If $\hat{\mathfrak{X}}$ is a tamely imbedded subset of $\mathfrak{X}$ with $\mathfrak{L}=N(\hat{\mathfrak{L}})$ then in this section $\mathcal{I}(\hat{\mathfrak{L}})$ denotes the set of generalized characters of $\mathfrak{L}$ which vanish outside $\hat{\mathfrak{L}}$ and $\mathcal{C}(\hat{\mathfrak{L}})$ denotes the complex valued class functions of $\mathfrak{L}$ which vanish outside $\hat{\mathfrak{L}}$. Similarly, $\mathcal{I}_{0}(\hat{\mathfrak{L}})(\mathcal{C}_{0}(\hat{\mathfrak{L}}))$ is the subset of $\mathcal{I}(\hat{\mathfrak{L}})(\mathcal{C}(\hat{\mathfrak{L}}))$ vanishing at 1. R. Brauer and M. Suzuki noted that if $\hat{\mathfrak{L}}$ is a T. I. set in $\mathfrak{X}$ then the mapping $\tau$ from $\mathcal{C}_{0}(\hat{\mathfrak{L}})$ into the ring of class functions of $\mathfrak{X}$ defined by

$$
\alpha^ {\tau} = \alpha^ {*}
$$

is an isometry ([24], p. 662). They were then able to extend this isometry to certain subsets of $\mathcal{C}(\widehat{\mathfrak{Q}})$. Several authors have since then used this technique and it has played an important role in recent work in group theory.

In this chapter these results will be generalized in two ways. First we will consider tamely imbedded subsets of $\mathfrak{X}$ rather than T. I. sets in $\mathfrak{X}$. Secondly we will show that under a variety of conditions $\tau$ can be extended to various large subsets of $\mathcal{C}(\hat{\mathfrak{X}})$. The results proved in this chapter are important for the proof of the main theorem of this paper. However it is unnecessary in general to assume that $\mathfrak{X}$ has odd order or that $\mathfrak{X}$ is a minimal simple group.

The following notation will be used throughout this section.

For a tamely imbedded subset $\hat{\mathfrak{L}}$ of $\mathfrak{X}$ let $\mathfrak{L} = N(\hat{\mathfrak{L}})$ and for

$1 \leq i \leq n$ let $\mathfrak{H}_i$ and $\mathfrak{N}_i$ have the same meaning as in Definition 9.1. Define $\mathfrak{H}_0 = 1$ and

$$
\mathfrak {L} _ {i} = \{L \mid L \in \mathfrak {D}, C (L) \subseteq \mathfrak {N} _ {i} \} \quad \text {   for   } 1 \leq i \leq n.
$$

For $L \in \mathfrak{L}_i, 0 \leq i \leq n$ let

$$
\mathfrak {A} _ {L} = \{L H | L H = H L, H \in \mathfrak {H} _ {i} \} = L \{\mathfrak {H} _ {i} \cap C (L) \}.\tag{9.2}
$$

Since $\hat{\mathfrak{L}}$ is tamely imbedded in $\mathfrak{X}$ it follows from (9.2) and Definition 9.1 that for $L\in\mathfrak{L}_{i},0\leq i\leq n$

$$
\mid \boldsymbol {C} (L) \mid = \mid \boldsymbol {C} (L) \cap \mathfrak {L} \mid \mid \mathfrak {A} _ {L} \mid .\tag{9.3}
$$

For $\alpha \in \mathcal{C}_0(\widehat{\mathfrak{X}})$ and $1 \leq i \leq n$ define

$$
\alpha_ {i} = \alpha | _ {\mathfrak {L} \cap \mathfrak {R} _ {i}}.
$$

Let $\alpha_{i1}$ be the class function of $\mathfrak{N}_i / \mathfrak{H}_i$ which satisfies

$$
\alpha_ {i 1} | _ {\mathfrak {L} \cap \mathfrak {R} _ {i}} = \alpha_ {i}.
$$

Let $\alpha_{i2}$ be the class function of $\mathfrak{N}_i$ induced by $\alpha_i$. Define

$$
\alpha^ {\tau} = \alpha^ {*} + \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} (\alpha_ {i 1} - \alpha_ {i 2}) ^ {*}\tag{9.4}
$$

If $\alpha \in \mathcal{S}_0(\hat{\mathfrak{X}})$ then (9.4) implies that $\alpha^{\tau}$ is a generalized character of $\mathfrak{X}$. It is an immediate consequence of the definition of induced characters that for $1 \leq i \leq n$

$$
\begin{array}{l l} \alpha_ {i 1} (A) = \alpha (L) & \text { for } L \in \mathfrak {L} _ {i}, A \in \mathfrak {A} _ {L} \\ \alpha_ {i 2} (A) = 0 & \text { for } L \in \mathfrak {L} _ {i}, A \in \mathfrak {A} _ {L}, A \neq L \\ \alpha_ {i 2} (L) = | C (L) \cap \mathfrak {H} _ {i} | \alpha (L) & \text { for } L \in \mathfrak {L} _ {i}. \end{array}\tag{9.5}
$$

LEMMA 9.1. Suppose that $\hat{\mathfrak{L}}$ is a tamely imbedded subset of $\mathfrak{X}$. If $\alpha \in \mathscr{C}_0(\hat{\mathfrak{L}})$ let $\alpha^r$ be defined by (9.4). Then $\alpha^r(X) = 0$ if $X$ is not conjugate to an element of $\mathfrak{A}_L$ for any $L \in \bigcup_{i=0}^{n} \mathfrak{L}_i$, while

$$
\alpha^ {\tau} (A) = \alpha (L) \quad \text { for } \quad A \in \mathfrak {A} _ {L}, L \in \bigcup_ {i = 0} ^ {n} \mathfrak {L} _ {i} .
$$

Proof. If  $N \in R_{i}$  then a complement of  $H_{i}$  in  $H_{i} \langle N \rangle$  is solvable. Thus ([28] p. 162) for  $1 \leq i \leq n$  every element of  $R_{i}$  is conjugate to an element of the form HL = LH with  $L \in L \cap R_{i}, H \in H_{i}$ . Suppose

that $L$ is not conjugate to an element of $\hat{\mathfrak{X}}^{\sharp}$; then since $\alpha \in \mathcal{C}_{0}(\hat{\mathfrak{X}})$, (9.4) implies that $\alpha^{\tau}(HL) = 0$. This implies that $\alpha^{\tau}(X) = 0$ unless $X$ is conjugate to an element of $\mathfrak{A}_L$ for some $L \in \bigcup_{i=0}^{n} \mathfrak{X}_i$.

Let $A \in \mathfrak{A}_L, L \in \mathfrak{L}_i$ for some $i$ with $0 \leq i \leq n$. Suppose that $X^{-1}LX \in \widehat{\mathfrak{N}}_j$ for some $X \in \mathfrak{X}$ and some $j$ with $1 \leq j \leq n, i \neq j$. Then $(|\mathfrak{H}_j|, |C(L)|) \neq 1$. Thus $i \neq 0$ and $L \in \widehat{\mathfrak{N}}_i$. Furthermore $C(L) = C_{\mathfrak{L}}(L)C_{\mathfrak{H}_i}(L)$. By assumption $(|\mathfrak{H}_i|, |\mathfrak{H}_j|) = 1$ and $(|\mathfrak{H}_j|, |C_{\mathfrak{L}}(L)|) = 1$. Thus $(|C(L)|, |\mathfrak{H}_j|) = 1$ contrary to the choice of $L$. Since $\alpha \in \mathscr{C}_0(\widehat{\mathfrak{V}}), \alpha_{j_1} - \alpha_{j_2}$ vanishes on $\mathfrak{N}_j - \widehat{\mathfrak{N}}_j^*$. Consequently (9.4) implies that

$$
\begin{array}{l l} \alpha^ {\tau} (A) = \alpha^ {*} (A) & \text { for } i = 0 \\ \alpha^ {\tau} (A) = \alpha^ {*} (A) + (\alpha_ {i _ {1}} - \alpha_ {i _ {2}}) ^ {*} (A) & \text { for } 1 \leq i \leq n. \end{array}\tag{9.6}
$$

Since $\hat{\mathfrak{N}}_i$ is a T. I. set in $\mathfrak{X}$ with $N(\hat{\mathfrak{N}}_i) = \mathfrak{N}_i$ we get that

$$
(\alpha_ {i 1} - \alpha_ {i 2}) ^ {*} (A) = (\alpha_ {i 1} - \alpha_ {i 2}) (A).
$$

Thus (9.6) yields that

$$
\alpha^ {\tau} (A) = \alpha^ {*} (A) + (\alpha_ {i 1} - \alpha_ {i 2}) (A) \quad \text { for } 1 \leq i \leq n.\tag{9.7}
$$

Assume first that $A = L$. Then $\alpha^{*}(L) = |C(L) \cap \mathfrak{H}_{i}| \alpha(L)$. Hence (9.5), (9.6) and (9.7) yield that $\alpha^{\tau}(A) = \alpha(L)$. If $A \neq L$, then $\alpha^{*}(A) = 0$ and $1 \leq i \leq n$. Thus (9.5) and (9.7) yield that also in this case $\alpha^{\tau}(A) = \alpha(L)$. The proof is complete in all cases.

LEMMA 9.2. Suppose that $\hat{\mathfrak{L}}$ is a tamely imbedded subset of $\mathfrak{X}$. If $\alpha \in \mathcal{C}_0(\hat{\mathfrak{L}})$ let $\alpha^r$ be defined by (9.4). Then for $1 \leq i \leq n$

$$
\alpha^ {\tau} (N) = \alpha_ {i 1} (N) \quad f o r N \in \hat {\mathfrak {N}} _ {i} \cup \mathfrak {Q} _ {i}.
$$

Furthermore $\alpha^{\tau}|_{\mathfrak{N}_{i}}$ is a linear combination of characters of $\mathfrak{N}_i / \mathfrak{H}_i$.

Proof. If $N \in \mathfrak{Q}_i$ then by Lemma 9.1 and the definition of $\alpha_{i1}$

$$
\alpha^ {\tau} (N) = 0 = \alpha_ {i 1} (1) = \alpha_ {i 1} (N).
$$

If $N \in \mathfrak{R}_i$, and $\alpha^{\tau}(N) \neq 0$, then $N$ is conjugate to an element $A$ of $\mathfrak{A}_L$ for some $L \in \mathfrak{B}_i$. Thus by (9.5) and Lemma 9.1 $\alpha^{\tau}(N) = \alpha_{i1}(N)$ as required.

Let $\theta$ be an irreducible character of $\mathfrak{N}_i$ which does not have $\mathfrak{Q}_i$ in its kernel. Then

$$
\left(\alpha^ {\tau} \mid_ {\mathfrak {N} _ {i}}, \theta\right) = \frac {1}{\mid \mathfrak {N} _ {i} \mid} \Sigma_ {\mathfrak {N} _ {i}} \alpha^ {\tau} (N) \overline {{{{\theta (N)}}}}.\tag{9.8}
$$

By Lemma 4.3 $\theta$ vanishes on $\mathfrak{N}_i - \hat{\mathfrak{N}}_i - \mathfrak{S}_i$ hence (9.8) and the first part of the lemma yield that

$$
\begin{array}{r l} (\alpha^ {\tau} | _ {\mathfrak {N} _ {i}}, \theta) & = \frac {1}{| \mathfrak {N} _ {i} |} \Sigma_ {\mathfrak {N} _ {i}} \alpha^ {\tau} (N) \overline {{\theta (N)}} \\ & = \frac {1}{| \mathfrak {N} _ {i} |} \Sigma_ {\mathfrak {N} _ {i}} \alpha_ {i 1} (N) \overline {{\theta (N)}} = (\alpha_ {i 1}, \theta). \end{array}
$$

Since $\alpha_{i1}$ is a linear combination of characters of $\mathfrak{N}_i / \mathfrak{Q}_i$ this yields that $(\alpha^{\tau}|_{\mathfrak{N}_i},\theta) = 0$. The lemma is proved.

LEMMA 9.3. Suppose that $\hat{\mathfrak{L}}$ is a tamely imbedded subset of $\mathfrak{X}$. If $\alpha \in \mathcal{C}_0(\hat{\mathfrak{L}})$ and $\alpha^r$ is defined by (9.4) then

$$
(\alpha^ {\tau}, 1 _ {\mathfrak {X}}) _ {\mathfrak {X}} = (\alpha , 1 _ {\mathfrak {L}}) _ {\mathfrak {L}}.
$$

Proof. Let  $C_{1}, C_{2}, \cdots$  be all the conjugate classes of X which contain elements of  $\hat{X}^{\sharp}$ . Let  $L_{1}, L_{2}, \cdots$  be elements in  $\bigcup_{i=0}^{n} X_{i}$  such that  $L_{j} \in C_{j} \cap \hat{X}^{\sharp}$ . The number of elements in X which are conjugate to an element of  $A_{L_{j}}$  is easily seen to be

$$
\left| \mathfrak {C} _ {j} \right| \left| \mathfrak {A} _ {L _ {j}} \right| = \frac {\left| \mathfrak {X} \right|}{\left| C (L _ {j}) \right|} \left| \mathfrak {A} _ {L _ {j}} \right|.
$$

Thus by Lemma 9.1 and (9.3)

$$
\begin{array}{r l} (\alpha^ {r}, 1 _ {\mathfrak {x}}) _ {\mathfrak {x}} & = \frac {1}{| \mathfrak {x} |} \Sigma_ {j} \frac {| \mathfrak {x} |}{| C (L _ {j}) |} | \mathfrak {A} _ {L _ {j}} | \alpha (L _ {j}) \\ & = \frac {1}{| \mathfrak {x} |} \Sigma_ {j} \frac {| \mathfrak {x} |}{| C (L _ {j}) \cap \mathfrak {x} |} \alpha (L _ {j}). \end{array}\tag{9.9}
$$

By assumption $\mathfrak{C}_1 \cap \hat{\mathfrak{V}}^{\sharp}, \mathfrak{C}_2 \cap \hat{\mathfrak{V}}^{\sharp}, \cdots$ are the conjugate classes of $\mathfrak{V}$ which contain elements of $\hat{\mathfrak{V}}^{\sharp}$. Since $\alpha \in \mathcal{C}_0(\hat{\mathfrak{V}})$ this yields that

$$
(\alpha , 1 _ {\mathfrak {L}}) _ {\mathfrak {L}} = \frac {1}{| \mathfrak {L} |} \Sigma_ {j} \frac {| \mathfrak {L} |}{| C (L _ {j}) \cap \mathfrak {L} |} \alpha (L _ {j}).
$$

Therefore (9.9) implies the desired equality.

LEMMA 9.4. Suppose that $\hat{\mathfrak{L}}$ is a tamely imbedded subset of $\mathfrak{X}$.

Let $\Theta$ be a generalized character of $\mathfrak{X}$ such that for $L\in\bigcup_{i=0}^{n}\mathfrak{L}_{i}$, $\Theta$ is constant on $\mathfrak{A}_{L}$. If $\alpha,\beta\in\mathcal{S}_{0}(\hat{\mathfrak{Q}})$ and if $\alpha^{\tau},\beta^{\tau}$ are defined by (9.4) then

$$
\begin{array}{c} (\alpha^ {\tau}, \theta) _ {\mathfrak {X}} = (\alpha , \theta_ {| \mathfrak {X}}) _ {\mathfrak {X}} \\ (\alpha^ {\tau}, \beta^ {\tau}) _ {\mathfrak {X}} = (\alpha , \beta) _ {\mathfrak {X}}. \end{array}
$$

Proof. Since $\theta$ is constant on $\mathfrak{A}_L$ for $L \in \bigcup_{i=0}^{*} \mathfrak{L}_i$ it follows from Lemma 9.1 that

$$
\{\alpha \bar {\Theta} _ {| \mathfrak {L}} \} ^ {\tau} = \alpha^ {\tau} \bar {\Theta}.
$$

Thus by Lemma 9.3

$$
(\alpha^ {\tau}, \Theta) _ {\mathfrak {x}} = (\alpha^ {\tau} \bar {\Theta}, 1 _ {\mathfrak {x}}) _ {\mathfrak {x}} = (\alpha \bar {\Theta} _ {| \mathfrak {L}}, 1 _ {\mathfrak {L}}) _ {\mathfrak {L}} = (\alpha , \Theta_ {| \mathfrak {L}}) _ {\mathfrak {L}}.
$$

By Lemma 9.1 $\beta^{\tau}$ is a generalized character of $\mathfrak{X}$ which is constant on $\mathfrak{A}_L$ for $L\in \bigcup_{i = 0}^{n}\mathfrak{L}_i$. If now $\theta$ is replaced by $\beta^{\tau}$ in the first equation of the lemma the second equation follows.

LEMMA 9.5. Suppose that $\hat{\mathfrak{X}}$ is a tamely imbedded subset of $\mathfrak{X}$. Let $\Theta$ be a class function of $\mathfrak{X}$ which is constant on $\mathfrak{A}_L$ for $L \in \bigcup_{i=0}^{n} \mathfrak{X}_i$. Let $\mathfrak{X}_0$ be the set of all elements in $\mathfrak{X}$ which are conjugate to some element of $\mathfrak{A}_L$ with $L \in \bigcup_{i=0}^{n} \mathfrak{X}_i$. Then

$$
\frac {1}{| \mathfrak {X} |} \Sigma_ {\mathfrak {x} _ {0}} \Theta (X) = \frac {1}{| \mathfrak {L} |} \Sigma_ {\hat {\mathfrak {L}}} \Theta (L).
$$

Proof. Define $\alpha \in \mathcal{C}_0(\widehat{\mathfrak{Q}})$ by

$$
\begin{array}{l l} \alpha (L) = \theta (L) & \text {if} L \in \hat {\mathfrak {X}} ^ {\sharp} \\ \alpha (L) = 0 & \text {if} L \in \hat {\mathfrak {X}} - \hat {\mathfrak {X}} ^ {\sharp}. \end{array}
$$

By Lemma 9.1

$$
\begin{array}{l l} \alpha^ {\tau} (X) = \theta (X) & \text { if } X \in \mathfrak {X} _ {0} \\ \alpha^ {\tau} (X) = 0 & \text { if } X \in \mathfrak {X} - \mathfrak {X} _ {0}. \end{array}
$$

Consequently Lemma 9.3 implies that

$$
\begin{array}{r l} \frac {1}{| \mathfrak {X} |} \sum_ {\tilde {x} _ {0}} \theta (X) & = (\alpha^ {\tau}, 1 _ {\tilde {x}}) _ {\tilde {x}} = (\alpha , 1 _ {\mathfrak {Q}}) _ {\mathfrak {Q}} = \frac {1}{| \mathfrak {Q} |} \sum_ {\hat {\mathfrak {Q}}} \alpha (L) \\ & = \frac {1}{| \mathfrak {Q} |} \sum_ {\hat {\mathfrak {Q}}} \theta (L). \end{array}
$$

Lemma 9.5 is of great importance. Even the special case in which $\Theta = 1_{\mathfrak{X}}$ is of considerable interest and plays a role in section 26. In this special case, Lemma 9.5 asserts simply that $|\mathfrak{X}_0| / |\mathfrak{X}| = |\hat{\mathfrak{L}}^{\sharp}| / |\mathfrak{L}|$.

## 10. Coherent Sets of Characters

Throughout this section let $\hat{\mathfrak{L}}$ be a tamely imbedded subset of the group $\mathfrak{X}$. Let $\mathfrak{L} = N(\hat{\mathfrak{L}})$ and let $\mathcal{I}(\hat{\mathfrak{L}})$ be the set of generalized characters of $\mathfrak{X}$ which vanish outside $\hat{\mathfrak{L}}$. Let $\tau$ be defined by (9.4).

DEFINITION 10.1. A set S of generalized characters of R is coherent if and only if

(i) $\mathcal{I}_0(\mathcal{S})\neq 0.$

(ii) It is possible to extend $\tau$ from $\mathcal{I}_0(\mathcal{S})$ to a linear isometry mapping $\mathcal{I}(\mathcal{S})$ into the set of generalized characters of $\mathfrak{X}$.

(iii) $\mathcal{I}_0(\mathcal{S})\subseteq \mathcal{I}_0(\hat{\mathfrak{L}}).$

It is easily seen that if $\mathcal{S}$ is a coherent set and $\mathcal{T} \subseteq \mathcal{S}$ with $\mathcal{I}_0(\mathcal{T}) \neq 0$ then also $\mathcal{T}$ is a coherent set. It is more difficult to decide whether the union of two coherent subsets of $\mathcal{I}(\hat{\mathfrak{X}})$ is coherent. Examples are known in which $\mathcal{S}$ consists of irreducible characters of $\mathfrak{X}$ and is not coherent though $\mathcal{I}_0(\mathcal{S}) \neq 0$ [25]. In these examples $\hat{\mathfrak{X}}$ is even a T. I. set in $\mathfrak{X}$. The main purpose of this section is to give some sufficient conditions which ensure that a subset $\mathcal{S}$ of $\mathcal{I}(\hat{\mathfrak{X}})$ is coherent.

LEMMA 10.1. Suppose that $\hat{\mathfrak{S}}$ is a tamely imbedded subset of $\mathfrak{X}$. Let $\mathcal{S} = \{\lambda_i | 1 \leq i \leq n\}$ with $n \geq 2$. Assume that for $1 \leq i \leq n$, $\lambda_i$ is an irreducible character of $\mathfrak{X}$. Furthermore $\lambda_i(L) = \lambda_1(L)$ for $L \in \mathfrak{X} - \hat{\mathfrak{X}}^*$. Then $\mathcal{S}$ is coherent. Furthermore, if $\tau_1$ and $\tau_2$ are extensions of $\tau$ to $\mathcal{S}$ then either $\tau_1 = \tau_2$ or $|\mathcal{S}| = 2$ and $\lambda_i^{\tau_1} = -\lambda_{\overline{i}}^{\tau_2}$, $i = 1, 2$.

Proof. For $1 \leq i, j \leq n$ let $\alpha_{ij} = \lambda_i - \lambda_j$, then $\alpha_{ij} \in \mathcal{I}_0(\mathcal{S})$. Thus $\mathcal{I}_0(\mathcal{S}) \neq 0$ since $n \geq 2$. Furthermore $\alpha_{ij}^\tau$ is defined. Since $\tau$ is an isometry this yields that

$$
\left(\alpha_ {i j} ^ {\tau}, \alpha_ {i ^ {\prime} j ^ {\prime}} ^ {\tau}\right) = \left(\alpha_ {i j}, \alpha_ {i ^ {\prime} j ^ {\prime}}\right) = \delta_ {i i ^ {\prime}} - \delta_ {j i ^ {\prime}} - \delta_ {i j ^ {\prime}} + \delta_ {j j ^ {\prime}}.\tag{10.1}
$$

In particular (10.1) implies that if $i \neq j$ then $\| \alpha_{ij}^{\tau} \|^{2} = 2$. By Lemma 9.1 $\alpha_{ij}^{\tau}(1) = 0$, therefore $\alpha_{ij}^{\tau}$ is the difference of two irreducible characters of $\mathfrak{X}$.

If n > 2, then it follows from equation (10.1) that  $(\alpha_{i i}^{\tau}, \alpha_{i j}^{\tau}) = 1$  if 1 < i, j and  $i \neq j$ . It is now a simple consequence of (10.1) that there exists a unique irreducible character of x which is not orthogonal to any  $\alpha_{i i}^{\tau}$  for  $2 \leq i \leq n$ . Furthermore if  $A_{1}$  is chosen to be plus or minus this character then it may be assumed that

$$
\left(\alpha_ {1 i} ^ {\tau}, \Delta_ {1}\right) = 1 \quad \text { for } 2 \leq i \leq n.
$$

Now define $\Delta_{i}$ by

$$
\alpha_ {1 i} ^ {\tau} = \Delta_ {1} - \Delta_ {i}, \quad 2 \leq i \leq n.
$$

This implies that

$$
\alpha_ {i j} ^ {\tau} = \Delta_ {i} - \Delta_ {j}.
$$

Hence (10.1) yields that the generalized characters $\Lambda_{i}, 1 \leq i \leq n$ are pairwise orthogonal and that they each have weight one. It is easily shown that a rational integral linear combination of the characters $\lambda_{i}$ of degree zero is a rational integral linear combination of the generalized characters $\alpha_{1i}$. Hence if $\mathcal{S}^{\tau}$ is the set of generalized characters $\Lambda_{i}, 1 \leq i \leq n$, then the linear mapping sending $\lambda_{i}$ into $\Lambda_{i}$ is an isometry. Thus, $\mathcal{S}$ is coherent and the extension of $\tau$ to $\mathcal{S}$ is unique in this case.

If $n = 2$, define $\Lambda_i$ for $i = 1, 2$ by $\alpha_{13}^{\tau} = \Lambda_1 - \Lambda_2$, where $\Lambda_i$ has weight one. Any rational integral linear combination of $\lambda_1$ and $\lambda_2$ of degree zero is a multiple of $\alpha_{12}$. Thus, if $\tau_1$ is any extension of $\tau$ to $\mathcal{S}$, $\lambda_i^{\tau_1} = \Lambda_i$ or $\lambda_i^{\tau_1} = -\Lambda_{3-i}$ for $i = 1, 2$. The proof is complete.

Before proving the main result of this section, another definition is needed. The following notation is introduced temporarily.

Let $\mathcal{S}$ be a subset of $\mathcal{I}(\hat{\mathfrak{Q}})$ which consists of pairwise orthogonal characters. If $\mathcal{S}_1 \subseteq \mathcal{S}$, let $x(\mathcal{S}_1)$ denote the smallest weight of any character in $\mathcal{S}_1$ of minimum degree. If $\mathcal{S}_1$ and $\mathcal{T}$ are coherent subsets of $\mathcal{S}$ and $\tau_1$ and $\tau_2$ are extensions of $\tau$ to $\mathcal{S}_1$ and $\mathcal{T}$ respectively, define

$$
\mathscr {A} \left(\mathscr {S} _ {1}, \tau_ {1}; \mathscr {T}, \tau_ {2}\right) = \{\alpha \mid
$$

(i) $\alpha \in \mathcal{F}_0(\mathcal{S})$.

(ii) $\alpha^{\tau} = \Delta_{1} + \Delta_{2}$, where

(a) $\Delta_{2} \in \mathcal{C}(\mathcal{T}^{\tau_{2}})$,

(b) $\Delta_{1}$ is not orthogonal to $\mathcal{I}_{0}(\mathcal{S}_{1})^{r}$,

(c)  $\|\Delta_{1}\|^{2} \leq x(\mathcal{S}_{1})\}.$

DEFINITION 10.2. Let $\mathcal{S}_1$ be a coherent subset of $\mathcal{S}$ and let $\tau^*$ be an extension of $\tau$ to $\mathcal{S}_1$. The pair $(\mathcal{S}_1, \tau^*)$ is subcoherent in $\mathcal{S}$ if the following conditions are satisfied: If $\mathcal{T}$ is any coherent subset of $\mathcal{S}$ which is orthogonal to $\mathcal{S}_1$ and if $\tau_1$ and $\tau_2$ are extensions of $\tau$ to $\mathcal{S}_1$ and $\mathcal{T}$ respectively, then

(i)  $S_{1}^{\tau_{1}}$  is orthogonal to  $T^{\tau_{2}}$ .

(ii) If $\alpha \in \mathcal{A}(\mathcal{S}_1, \tau_1; \mathcal{T}, \tau_2)$, then $\alpha^r$ is a sum of two generalized characters, one of which is orthogonal to $\mathcal{S}_1^{r*}$ and the other is in $\pm \mathcal{S}_1^{r*}$.

If  $(\mathcal{S}_{1},\tau^{*})$  is subcoherent in S, we also say that  $S_{1}$  is subcoherent in S, which causes no confusion in case  $\tau^{*}$  has been designated.

Hypothesis 10.1.

(i) $\hat{\mathfrak{L}}$ is a tamely imbedded subset of the group $\mathfrak{X}$.

(ii) For $1 \leq i \leq k$, $\mathcal{S}_i = \{\lambda_{i_s} | 1 \leq s \leq n_i\}$ is a subset of $\mathcal{F}(\hat{\mathfrak{Q}})$.

(iii) $\mathcal{S} = \bigcup_{i=1}^{k} \mathcal{S}_i$ consists of pairwise orthogonal characters.

(iv) For any $i$ with $1 \leq i \leq k$, $\mathcal{S}_i$ is coherent with isometry $\tau_i$. $\mathcal{S}_i$ is partitioned into sets $\mathcal{S}_{ij}$ such that each $\mathcal{S}_{ij}$ either consists of irreducible characters of the same degree and $|\mathcal{S}_{ij}| \geq 2$ or $(\mathcal{S}_{ij}, \tau_{ij})$ is subcoherent in $\mathcal{S}$ where $\tau_{ij} = \tau_i$ on $\mathcal{S}_{ij}$.

(v) For $1 \leq i \leq k, 1 \leq s \leq n_i$, there exist integers $\angle_{i_s}$ such that

$$
1 = \ell_ {1 1} \leq \ell_ {2 1} \leq \dots \leq \ell_ {k 1},
$$

$$
\lambda_ {i s} (1) = \ell_ {i s} \lambda_ {1 1} (1), \ell_ {i 1} | \ell_ {i s}.
$$

(vi) $\lambda_{11}$ is an irreducible character of $\mathfrak{L}$.

(vii) For any integer $m$ with $1 < m \leq k$,

$$
\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {m - 1} \sum_ {s = 1} ^ {n _ {i}} \frac {\ell_ {i s} ^ {2}}{\left| \left| \lambda_ {i s} \right| \right| ^ {2}} > 2 \ell_ {m 1}.\tag{10.2}
$$

THEOREM 10.1. Suppose that Hypothesis 10.1 is satisfied. Then $\mathcal{S}$ is coherent. There is an extension $\tau^{*}$ of $\tau$ to $\mathcal{I}(\mathcal{S})$ such that either $\tau^{*}$ agrees with $\tau_{i}$ on $\mathcal{S}_{i}$ or $\mathcal{S}_{i} = \{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\}$ and $\lambda_{j}^{\tau_{i}} = -\lambda_{3-j}^{\tau_{i}^{*}}$ for $j = 1,2$.

Proof. The proof is by induction on k. If k = 1 the theorem follows by assumption.

It is easily seen that $\bigcup_{i=1}^{k-1} \mathcal{S}_i$ satisfies the assumption of the theorem. Hence by induction it may be assumed that $\bigcup_{i=1}^{k-1} \mathcal{S}_i$ is coherent. Let $\tau^*$ denote an extension of $\tau$ to $\bigcup_{i=1}^{k-1} \mathcal{S}_i$, with the property that for

$1 \leq i \leq k - 1, \tau^{*}$ agrees with $\tau_{i}$ on $\mathcal{S}_{i}$, or $\mathcal{S}_{i} = \{\lambda_{1}, \lambda_{2}\}$ and $\lambda_{j}^{\tau^{*}} = -\lambda_{3}^{\tau_{i}}_{-j}$, $j = 1, 2$.

Choose the notation so that $\lambda_{k1}$ has minimum weight among the characters in $\mathcal{S}_k$ of degree $\ell_{k1}\lambda_{11}(1)$. Let $\mathcal{S}_{k1}$ be the subset $\mathcal{S}_{ki}$ which contains $\lambda_{k1}$. For $1 \leq s \leq n_k$ define

$$
\beta_ {s} = \iota_ {k s} \lambda_ {1 1} - \lambda_ {k s}.
$$

Thus $\beta_{s} \in \mathcal{I}_{0}(\mathcal{S})$ and $\beta_{s}^{r}$ is defined. Define the integer $y$ by

$$
\left(\lambda_ {1 1} ^ {\tau^ {*}}, \beta_ {1} ^ {\tau}\right) = \iota_ {k 1} - y.\tag{10.3}
$$

If $(i, t) \neq (1, 1)$ and $1 \leq i \leq k - 1, 1 \leq t \leq n_i$, then by (10.3)

$$
\begin{array}{r l} (\lambda_ {i t} ^ {\tau^ {*}}, \beta_ {1} ^ {\tau}) & = (\ell_ {i t} \lambda_ {1 1} ^ {\tau^ {*}}, \beta_ {1} ^ {\tau}) - (\ell_ {i t} \lambda_ {1 1} ^ {\tau^ {*}} - \lambda_ {i t} ^ {\tau^ {*}}, \beta_ {1} ^ {\tau}) \\ & = \ell_ {i t} (\ell_ {k 1} - y) - \ell_ {i t} \ell_ {k 1} = - y \ell_ {i t}. \end{array}\tag{10.4}
$$

Since $\lambda_{11}$ is irreducible and $\tau$ is an isometry on $\mathcal{I}_0(\mathcal{S})$

(10.5)

$$
\left\| \beta_ {s} ^ {r} \right\| ^ {2} = \mathscr {L} _ {k s} ^ {2} + \left\| \lambda_ {k s} \right\| ^ {2} \quad \text { for } 1 \leq s \leq n _ {k}.\tag{By (10.4}
$$

$$
\beta_ {1} ^ {\tau} = \angle_ {k 1} \lambda_ {1 1} ^ {\tau^ {*}} - y \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {k - 1} \sum_ {s = 1} ^ {n _ {i}} \frac {\angle_ {i s}}{\| \lambda_ {i s} \| ^ {2}} \lambda_ {i s} ^ {\tau^ {*}} + \Delta\tag{10.6}
$$

where $(\Delta, \lambda_{i,s}^{*}) = 0$ for $1 \leq i \leq k - 1, 1 \leq s \leq n_i$. Equations (10.5) and (10.6) now yield that

$$
\mathcal {L} _ {k 1} ^ {2} - 2 \mathcal {L} _ {k 1} y + y ^ {2} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {k - 1} \sum_ {s = 1} ^ {n _ {i}} \frac {\mathcal {L} _ {i s} ^ {2}}{\| \lambda_ {i s} \| ^ {2}} + \| \Delta \| ^ {2} = \mathcal {L} _ {k 1} ^ {2} + \| \lambda_ {k 1} \| ^ {2}.\tag{10.7}
$$

If $y \neq 0$ then since $y$ is an integer (10.2) and (10.7) imply that

$$
0 \leq 2 / _ {k 1} (y ^ {3} - y) <   \| \lambda_ {k 1} \| ^ {2} - \| \Delta \| ^ {2}.
$$

Therefore

$$
\| \Delta \| ^ {2} <   \| \lambda_ {k 1} \| ^ {2} \quad \text { if } y \neq 0.\tag{10.8}
$$

We will show that $y = 0$. By Hypothesis 10.1 (iv), $\tau$ can be extended from $\mathcal{S}_0(\mathcal{S}_k)$ to a linear isometry $\tau_k$ on $\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{S}_k)$. For $1 \leq s \leq n_k$ let $\Lambda_s$ be the image of $\lambda_{k,s}$ under this extension. If $(\mathcal{S}_{kj}, \tau_{kj})$ is subcoherent in $\mathcal{S}$, then $\mathcal{S}_{kj}^{\tau_{kj}}$ is orthogonal to $\bigcup_{i=1}^{k-1} \mathcal{S}_i^{\tau^*}$. Suppose that $\mathcal{S}_{kj}$ consists of irreducible characters of the same degree. If $\mathcal{S}_{kj}^{\tau_{kj}}$ is not orthogonal to $\bigcup_{i=1}^{k-1} \mathcal{S}_i^{\tau^*}$, then there exists $\lambda \in \mathcal{S}_{kj}$ and $\lambda_1 \in \mathcal{S}_{im}$ for some $i$ and $m$ with $1 \leq i \leq k-1$, such that $(\lambda^{\tau_{kj}}, \lambda_1^{\tau^*}) \neq 0$. Assume first that $\mathcal{S}_{im}$ consists of irreducible characters of the same degree.

Then it may be assumed that $\lambda = \lambda_{kt}, \lambda_{kt'} \in \mathcal{S}_{kj}, \lambda_{kt} \neq \lambda_{kt'}$ and $\lambda_1 = \lambda_{is}, \lambda_{is'} \in \mathcal{S}_{im}, \lambda_{is} \neq \lambda_{is'}$. Thus $\lambda_{kt}^{rkj} = \varepsilon \lambda_{is}^{r*}$ for suitable $\varepsilon = \pm 1$. Hence

$$
0 = \left(\lambda_ {i s} ^ {\tau *} - \lambda_ {i s ^ {\prime}} ^ {\tau *}, \lambda_ {k t} ^ {\tau k} - \lambda_ {k t ^ {\prime}} ^ {\tau k}\right) = \varepsilon + \left(\lambda_ {i s ^ {\prime}} ^ {\tau *}, \lambda_ {k t ^ {\prime}} ^ {\tau k}\right).
$$

Hence $\lambda_{is'}^{\tau *} = -\varepsilon \lambda_{kt'}^{\tau_k}$. Therefore

$$
0 = \left(\lambda_ {i s} ^ {\tau *} - \lambda_ {i s ^ {\prime}} ^ {\tau *}\right) (1) = \varepsilon \left(\lambda_ {k t} ^ {\tau k j} + \lambda_ {k t ^ {\prime}} ^ {\tau k j}\right) (1) = 2 \varepsilon \lambda_ {k t} ^ {\tau k j} (1),
$$

which is not the case as $||\lambda^{\tau_{kj}}||^2 = 1$. Suppose now that $\mathcal{S}_{im}$ is subcoherent in $\mathcal{S}$. Then $\mathcal{S}_{im}^{\tau^*}$ is orthogonal to $\mathcal{S}_k^{\tau_k}$ by definition. Therefore, for $2 \leq s \leq n_k$,

$$
\left(\Delta , \frac {\mathcal {L} _ {k s}}{\mathcal {L} _ {k 1}} \Delta_ {1} - \Delta_ {s}\right) = \left(\beta_ {1} ^ {r}, \frac {\mathcal {L} _ {k s}}{\mathcal {L} _ {k 1}} \Delta_ {1} - \Delta_ {s}\right) = - \frac {\mathcal {L} _ {k s}}{\mathcal {L} _ {k 1}} \| \lambda_ {k 1} \| ^ {2}.\tag{10.9}
$$

Thus, $\Delta$ is not orthogonal to $\mathcal{I}_0(\mathcal{S}_{k1})^\tau$. If $\mathcal{S}_{k1}$ consists of irreducible characters this yields that $\| \Delta \|^2 \geq 1$. Hence, $y = 0$ by (10.8). Suppose that $(\mathcal{S}_{k1}, \tau_{k1})$ is subcoherent in $\mathcal{S}$. If $y \neq 0$, (10.8) implies that

$$
\beta_ {1} ^ {\tau} = \Delta_ {1} + \Delta\tag{10.10}
$$

where $\Lambda\in\pm\mathcal{S}_{k1}^{\tau_{k1}}$ and $\Delta_{1}$ is orthogonal to $\mathcal{S}_{k1}^{\tau_{k1}}$. By changing notation if necessary it may be assumed that

$$
\varLambda = \pm \varLambda_ {1}\tag{10.11}
$$

by (10.9). Now (10.9), (10.10) and (10.11) yield that

$$
\left| \left| \lambda_ {k 1} \right| \right| ^ {4} = \left| (\Delta , \Delta) \right| ^ {2} \leq \left| \left| \Delta \right| \right| ^ {2} \cdot \left| \left| \lambda_ {k 1} \right| \right| ^ {2}.\tag{10.12}
$$

Hence, (10.8) and (10.12) imply that $y = 0$ in all cases. Thus, (10.3) becomes

$$
\left(\lambda_ {1 1} ^ {\tau^ {*}}, \beta_ {1} ^ {\tau}\right) = \angle_ {k 1}.\tag{10.13}
$$

For $1 \leq s \leq n_k$,

$$
\beta_ {s} = \frac {\ell_ {k s}}{\ell_ {k 1}} \beta_ {1} + \left(\frac {\ell_ {k s}}{\ell_ {k 1}} \lambda_ {k 1} - \lambda_ {k s}\right).
$$

Therefore, (10.13) implies that

$$
\left(\lambda_ {1 1} ^ {\tau^ {*}}, \beta_ {s} ^ {\tau}\right) = \angle_ {k s}, \quad 1 \leq s \leq n _ {k}.\tag{10.14}
$$

For $1 \leq s \leq n_k$, define $\lambda_{k_s}^{\tau^*}$ by

$$
\beta_ {s} ^ {\tau} = \angle_ {k s} \lambda_ {1 1} ^ {\tau^ {*}} - \lambda_ {k s} ^ {\tau^ {*}},\tag{10.15}
$$

and extend the definition to $\mathcal{I}(\mathcal{S})$ by linearity. This implies that $\lambda_{k s}^{\tau *} = \lambda_{k s}^{\tau k}$ or $\mathcal{S}_k = \{\lambda_1, \lambda_2\}$ and $\lambda_i^* = -\lambda_{s - i}^{\tau k}$ for $i = 1, 2$. Hence, $\mathcal{S}_k^{\tau *}$ is

orthogonal to $\bigcup_{i=1}^{k-1} \mathcal{S}_i^{\tau^*}$ and thus $\tau^*$ is an isometry on $\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{S})$. The proof is complete.

If $\mathcal{S}$ is a coherent subset of $\mathcal{I}(\hat{\mathfrak{V}})$, then $\tau$ will be used to denote an extension of $\tau$ to $\mathcal{I}(\mathcal{S})$.

Hypothesis 10.2.

(i) $\hat{\mathfrak{X}}$ is a tamely imbedded subset of $\mathfrak{X}$ and $\mathfrak{Q}_i$ is a supporting subgroup of $\hat{\mathfrak{X}}$. $\mathfrak{N}_i = N(\mathfrak{Q}_i)$.

(ii) If $\theta$ is any non-principal irreducible character of $\mathfrak{S}_i$ and $\tilde{\theta}$ is the character of $\mathfrak{R}_i$ induced by $\theta$, then $\tilde{\theta}$ is a sum of irreducible characters of $\mathfrak{R}_i$, all of which have the same degree and occur with the same multiplicity in $\tilde{\theta}$.

LEMMA 10.2. Suppose that Hypothesis 10.2 is satisfied. For any character $\alpha$ of $\mathfrak{S}_i$ let $\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}$ be the set of irreducible characters of $\mathfrak{N}_i$ whose restriction to $\mathfrak{S}_i$ coincides with $\alpha$. If $\Theta$ is a generalized character of $\mathfrak{X}$ which is orthogonal to $\mathcal{S}_0(\mathcal{S}_{\alpha})^*$ for all $\alpha$ with $(\alpha, 1_{\mathfrak{X}_i}) = 0$ then $\Theta$ is constant on the cosets of $\mathfrak{S}_i$ which lie in $\mathfrak{N}_i - \mathfrak{S}_i$.

Proof. We first remark that by Lemma 4.3 characters in $\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}$ vanish on $\mathfrak{N}_i - \hat{\mathfrak{N}}_i - \mathfrak{H}_i$, and so generalized characters in $\mathcal{S}_0(\mathcal{S}_{\alpha})$ vanish on $\mathfrak{N}_i - \hat{\mathfrak{N}}_i$. Suppose that $\theta_1, \theta_2$ are distinct characters in $\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}$ with $(\alpha, 1_{\mathfrak{H}_i}) = 0$. By assumption $(\theta, (\theta_1 - \theta_2)^*) = 0$. Thus by the Frobenius reciprocity theorem $(\theta_{|\mathfrak{N}_i}, \theta_1 - \theta_2) = 0$. Hence by Hypothesis 10.2 $\theta_{|\mathfrak{N}_i} = \tilde{\gamma} + \beta$, where $\tilde{\gamma}$ is a class function of $\mathfrak{N}_i$ induced by a class function $\gamma$ of $\mathfrak{H}_i$ and $\beta$ is a generalized character of $\mathfrak{N}_i / \mathfrak{H}_i$. Thus $\theta(N) = \beta(N)$ for $N \in \mathfrak{N}_i - \mathfrak{H}_i$. The proof is complete.

LEMMA 10.3. Suppose that Hypothesis 10.2 is satisfied. Let $\mathcal{S}$ be a coherent subset of $\mathcal{I}(\hat{\mathfrak{Q}})$ which consists of pairwise orthogonal characters of $\mathfrak{L}$. Assume further that $\mathcal{S}$ contains at least two irreducible characters. Then if $\lambda \in \mathcal{S}$, $\lambda^{\tau}$ is constant on the cosets of $\mathfrak{H}_i$ which lie in $\mathfrak{R}_i - \mathfrak{H}_i$.

Proof. Suppose that $\theta_{1},\theta_{2}$ are distinct irreducible characters of $\mathfrak{N}_{i}$ which do not contain $\mathfrak{D}_{i}$ in their kernel such that $\theta_{1| \mathfrak{D}_{i}} = \theta_{2| \mathfrak{D}_{i}}$. We will show that

$$
\left(\lambda_ {| \mathfrak {M} _ {4}} ^ {\tau}, \theta_ {1} - \theta_ {2}\right) = 0.\tag{10.16}
$$

By Lemma 4.3 $\theta_{1}$ and $\theta_{2}$ vanish on $\mathfrak{N}_i - \hat{\mathfrak{N}}_i - \mathfrak{H}_i$. Since $\hat{\mathfrak{N}}_i$ is a T. I. set in $\mathfrak{X}$ and $\mathfrak{N}_i = N(\hat{\mathfrak{N}}_i)$ the mapping sending $\theta_{1} - \theta_{2}$ into

$(\theta_{1}-\theta_{2})^{*}$ defines an isometry on $\mathcal{S}_{0}(\{\theta_{1},\theta_{2}\})$. By Lemma 10.1 this can be extended to an isometry of $\mathcal{S}(\{\theta_{1},\theta_{2}\})$. Let $\Theta_{1},\Theta_{2}$ be the respective images of $\theta_{1},\theta_{2}$ under this isometry. By assumption S contains two irreducible characters $\lambda_{1}$ and $\lambda_{2}$. Since

$$
\lambda_ {j} (\mathbf {1}) \lambda - \lambda (\mathbf {1}) \lambda_ {j} \in \mathcal {S} _ {0} (\mathcal {S})
$$

for $j = 1,2$, Lemma 9.2 implies that if (10.16) is violated then

$$
\left(\lambda_ {j _ {| \mathfrak {N} _ {i}}} ^ {\tau}, \theta_ {1} - \theta_ {2}\right) \neq 0 \quad \text { for } j = 1, 2.
$$

Thus by the Frobenius reciprocity theorem

$$
(\lambda_ {j} ^ {\tau}, \theta_ {1} - \theta_ {2}) = (\lambda_ {j} ^ {\tau}, (\theta_ {1} - \theta_ {2}) ^ {*}) \neq 0 \quad \text { for } j = 1, 2.
$$

Thus by changing notation if necessary it may be assumed that  $\lambda_{j}^{r} = \pm\theta_{j}$  for j = 1, 2, where the sign is independent of j. Hence

$$
(\lambda_ {1} (1) \lambda_ {2} ^ {r} - \lambda_ {2} (1) \lambda_ {1} ^ {r}, \theta_ {1} - \theta_ {2}) = \pm (\lambda_ {1} (1) + \lambda_ {2} (1)) \neq 0.\tag{10.17}
$$

Since $\lambda_1(1)\lambda_2 - \lambda_2(1)\lambda_1\in \mathcal{I}_0(\mathcal{S})$ Lemma 9.2 implies that

$$
((\lambda_ {1} (1) \lambda_ {2} ^ {\tau} - \lambda_ {2} (1) \lambda_ {1} ^ {\tau}) _ {| \mathfrak {N} _ {i}}, \theta_ {1} - \theta_ {2}) = 0.
$$

Thus by the Frobenius reciprocity theorem

$$
\left(\lambda_ {1} (1) \lambda_ {2} ^ {\tau} - \lambda_ {2} (1) \lambda_ {1} ^ {\tau}, \theta_ {1} - \theta_ {2}\right) = \left(\lambda_ {1} (1) \lambda_ {2} ^ {\tau} - \lambda_ {2} (1) \lambda_ {1} ^ {\tau}, \left(\theta_ {1} - \theta_ {2}\right) ^ {*}\right) = 0
$$

contrary to (10.17). Therefore (10.16) must hold. The result now follows from Lemma 10.2.

LEMMA 10.4. Suppose that the assumptions of Lemma 10.3 are satisfied. Let $a$ be the least common multiple of all the orders of elements in $\hat{\mathfrak{Q}}$. If $\lambda$ is an irreducible character in $\mathcal{S}$, then $\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha}$ contains all the values assumed by $\lambda^{\tau}$.

Proof. By assumption $\mathcal{S}$ contains another irreducible character $\lambda_{1}$. Let $\sigma$ be any automorphism of $\mathcal{Q}_{|\mathfrak{X}|}$ whose fixed field contains $\mathcal{Q}_{a}$. Then since $\lambda_{1}(1)\lambda - \lambda(1)\lambda_{1} \in \mathcal{I}_{0}(\mathcal{S})$ it follows directly from (9.4) that

$$
\begin{array}{r l} \sigma [ \{\lambda_ {1} (1) \lambda - \lambda (1) \lambda_ {1} \} ^ {\tau} ] & = \{\lambda_ {1} (1) \sigma (\lambda) - \lambda (1) \sigma (\lambda_ {1}) \} ^ {\tau} \\ & = \{\lambda_ {1} (1) \lambda - \lambda (1) \lambda_ {1} \} ^ {\tau}. \end{array}
$$

Therefore

$$
\lambda_ {1} (1) \sigma (\lambda^ {\tau}) - \lambda (1) \sigma (\lambda_ {1} ^ {\tau}) = \lambda_ {1} (1) \lambda^ {\tau} - \lambda (1) \lambda_ {1} ^ {\tau}.
$$

As $||\lambda^{\tau}||^{2}=||\lambda_{1}^{\tau}||^{2}=1$, this implies that $\sigma(\lambda^{\tau})=\lambda^{\tau}$. As $\sigma$ may be an arbitrary automorphism of $\mathcal{Q}_{|\mathfrak{X}|}$ whose fixed field contains $\mathcal{Q}_{a}$ the result is proved.

LEMMA 10.5. Suppose that $\hat{\mathfrak{L}}$ is a tamely imbedded subset of $\mathfrak{X}$. Let $\mathfrak{A}_L$ have the same meaning as in (9.2) and let $\Theta$ be a generalized character of $\mathfrak{X}$ which is constant on $\mathfrak{A}_L$ for $L \in \bigcup_{i=0}^{n} \mathfrak{X}_i$. Let $\mathcal{S}$ be a coherent subset of $\mathcal{I}(\hat{\mathfrak{X}})$ consisting of irreducible characters. Then there exist rational numbers $b, c$, and generalized characters $\beta, \gamma$ of $\mathfrak{X}$ which are orthogonal to $\mathcal{S}$ such that if $L \in \hat{\mathfrak{X}}^{\sharp}$ then $\Theta(L) = b\beta(L)$ if $\Theta$ is orthogonal to $\mathcal{S}^{\tau}$, and $\lambda^{\tau}(L) = \lambda(L) + c\gamma(L)$ if $\Theta = \lambda^{\tau} \in \mathcal{S}^{\tau}$.

Proof. It is an immediate consequence of Lemma 9.4 that if $\theta$ is orthogonal to $\mathcal{S}^{\tau}$ and if $\xi = \Sigma_{i}\lambda_{i}(1)\lambda_{i}$, where $\lambda_{i}$ ranges over $\mathcal{S}$, then

$$
\theta (L) = b _ {1} \xi (L) + b _ {2} \beta_ {1} (L) \quad \text { for } L \in \widehat {\mathfrak {L}} ^ {\sharp}\tag{10.18}
$$

where  $b_{1}, b_{2}$  are rational numbers and  $\beta_{1}$  is a generalized character of  $\mathfrak{L}$  which is orthogonal to S. If  $\theta = \lambda^{\tau}$ , then Lemma 9.4 yields that

$$
\lambda^ {\tau} (L) = \lambda (L) + c _ {1} \xi (L) + c _ {2} \gamma_ {1} (L) \quad \text { for } L \in \widehat {\mathfrak {X}} ^ {\sharp}\tag{19.19}
$$

where  $c_{1}, c_{2}$  are rational numbers and  $\gamma_{1}$  is a generalized character of L which is orthogonal to S. There exists a generalized character  $\xi'$  of L which is orthogonal to S such that

$$
\xi + \xi^ {\prime} = \rho_ {\mathfrak {L}}.
$$

Since $\rho_{\mathfrak{z}}(L) = 0$ for $L \in \mathfrak{L}^{\sharp}$ (10.18) and (10.19) imply respectively that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \Theta (L) = - b _ {1} \xi^ {\prime} (L) + b _ {2} \beta_ {1} (L) \\ \lambda^ {\tau} (L) = \lambda (L) - c _ {1} \xi^ {\prime} (L) + c _ {2} \gamma_ {1} (L). \end{array}
$$

The lemma follows by a suitable change in notation.

It is worth noting that if the hypotheses of Lemma 10.3 are satisfied for every subgroup in a system of supporting subgroups of $\hat{\mathfrak{L}}$, then that lemma implies that $\lambda^r$ satisfies the hypotheses of Lemma 10.5. This fact will be used later in this paper.

## 11. Some Applications of Theorem 10.1

In this section we are concerned with the problem of finding conditions under which it is possible to apply Theorem 10.1. That theorem will then allow us to conclude that certain sets of characters are coherent. To clarify matters the main Hypothesis is stated separately. This also serves to introduce the notation.

Hypothesis 11.1.

(i) $\hat{\mathfrak{L}}_0$ is a tamely imbedded subset of the group $\mathfrak{X}$ and $\mathfrak{L}_0 = N(\hat{\mathfrak{L}}_0)$ has odd order. $\mathfrak{L}_0 \triangleleft \mathfrak{L}_0$ and $\hat{\mathfrak{L}}_0$ is a union of cosets of $\mathfrak{L}_0$. Let $\mathfrak{L} = \mathfrak{L}_0 / \mathfrak{L}_0$ and let $\hat{\mathfrak{L}}$ be the image of $\hat{\mathfrak{L}}_0$ in $\mathfrak{L}$.

(ii) $\mathfrak{Q}$ and $\mathfrak{R}$ are normal subgroups of $\mathfrak{L}$ such that $\mathfrak{Q}$ is nilpotent and

$$
\mathfrak {H} \subseteq \bigcup_ {\boldsymbol {H} \in \mathfrak {H} ^ {\sharp}} C (H) \cap \mathfrak {R} \subseteq \widehat {\mathfrak {L}} \subseteq \mathfrak {R} \subseteq \mathfrak {L}.\tag{11.1}
$$

(iii) $\mathcal{S}$ is the set of all characters of $\mathfrak{S}$ which are induced by non principal irreducible characters of $\mathfrak{R}$, each of which vanishes outside $\hat{\mathfrak{R}}$. Then $\mathcal{S}$ consists of pairwise orthogonal characters.

(iv) There exists an integer $d$ such that $d|\mathfrak{L}:\mathfrak{R}||\lambda(1)$ for $\lambda\in\mathcal{S}$. Furthermore $\mathcal{S}$ contains an irreducible character of degree $d|\mathfrak{L}:\mathfrak{R}|$.

(v) Define an equivalence relation on S by the condition that two characters in S are equivalent if and only if they have the same degree and the same weight. Then each equivalence class of S is either subcoherent in S or consists of irreducible characters.

(vi) For any subgroup $\mathfrak{A}$ of $\mathfrak{S}$ which is normal in $\mathfrak{L}$ let $\mathcal{S}(\mathfrak{A})$ be the subset of $\mathcal{S}$ consisting of those characters which are equivalent to some character in $\mathcal{S}$ that has $\mathfrak{A}$ in its kernel.

In the application to the main theorem of this paper (11.1) will always be augmented by one of the following conditions.

(11.2)

$$
\mathfrak {H} = \widehat {\mathfrak {L}} = \mathfrak {R} \subset \mathfrak {L}.\tag{11.3}
$$

$$
\mathfrak {H} \subset \widehat {\mathfrak {L}} = \mathfrak {R} \subset \mathfrak {L}.\tag{11.4}
$$

$$
\mathfrak {H} \subseteq \bigcup_ {H \in \mathfrak {H} ^ {\sharp}} C (H) \cap \mathfrak {R} = \widehat {\mathfrak {L}} \subseteq \mathfrak {R} \subseteq \mathfrak {L}.
$$

THEOREM 11.1. Suppose that Hypothesis 11.1 is satisfied. Let $\mathfrak{H}_1$ be a normal subgroup of $\mathfrak{L}$ which is contained in $\mathfrak{H}$ such that

$$
\left| \mathfrak {H}: \mathfrak {H} _ {1} \right| > 4 d ^ {2} \left| \mathfrak {L}: \mathfrak {R} \right| ^ {2} + 1.\tag{11.5}
$$

If $\mathcal{S}(\mathfrak{H}_{1})$ is coherent and contains an irreducible character of degree $d|\mathfrak{L}:\mathfrak{R}|$ then $\mathcal{S}$ is coherent.

Proof. Let $\mathfrak{H}_2$ be a normal subgroup of $\mathfrak{L}$ which is contained in $\mathfrak{H}_1$ and is minimal with the property that $\mathcal{S}(\mathfrak{H}_2)$ is coherent. Suppose that $\mathfrak{H}_2 \neq \langle 1 \rangle$. Choose $\mathfrak{H}_3 \subset \mathfrak{H}_2$ such that $\mathfrak{H}_2 / \mathfrak{H}_3$ is a chief factor of $\mathfrak{L}$. Let $\mathcal{S}(\mathfrak{H}_2) = \mathcal{S}_1 = \{\lambda_{1s} | 1 \leq s \leq n_1\}$, where $\lambda_{11}$ is irreducible and $\lambda_{11}(1) = d | \mathfrak{L}: \mathfrak{R}|$. Let $\mathcal{S}_1, \cdots, \mathcal{S}_k$ be the subsets of $\mathcal{S}(\mathfrak{H}_3) - \mathcal{S}(\mathfrak{H}_2)$ consisting of all characters of a given weight and a given degree. For $2 \leq i \leq k$ let $\ell_i \lambda_{11}(1)$ be the common degree of the characters in $\mathcal{S}_i$.

By Hypothesis 11.1 all the assumptions of Theorem 10.1, except possibly inequality (10.2), are satisfied for $\mathcal{S}(\mathfrak{P}_3)$. We will now verify that also inequality (10.2) is satisfied.

Let $\theta_{1},\theta_{2},\cdots$ be all the irreducible characters of $\mathfrak{R}$ which do not have $\mathfrak{S}$ in their kernel. Let $\tilde{\theta}_{j}$ denote the character of $\mathfrak{S}$ induced by $\theta_{j}$. Then each $\tilde{\theta}_{j}$ is in $\mathcal{S}$ by Lemma 4.3. Furthermore if $\theta_{j}$ ranges only over characters of $\mathfrak{R}/\mathfrak{S}_{2}$ then

$$
\Sigma \theta_ {j} (1) \theta_ {j} = \rho_ {\mathfrak {R} / \mathfrak {H} _ {2}} - \rho_ {\mathfrak {R} / \mathfrak {H}}.
$$

Therefore

$$
\Sigma \theta_ {j} (1) \tilde {\theta} _ {j} = \rho_ {\mathfrak {L} / \mathfrak {H} _ {2}} - \rho_ {\mathfrak {L} / \mathfrak {H}}.\tag{11.6}
$$

If $\tilde{\theta}_i \neq \tilde{\theta}_j$ then $(\tilde{\theta}_i, \tilde{\theta}_j) = 0$. Suppose that for a given $j$ there are $a_j$ values of $i$ such that $\tilde{\theta}_j = \tilde{\theta}_i$. Then (11.6) implies that

$$
\Sigma \{\theta_ {j} (1) a _ {j} \} ^ {2} | | \tilde {\theta} _ {j} | | ^ {2} = | \mathfrak {L}: \mathfrak {H} _ {2} | - | \mathfrak {L}: \mathfrak {H} |\tag{11.7}
$$

where the summation in (11.7) ranges over the distinct ones among the  $\tilde{\theta}_{j}$ . Since

$$
\{\theta_ {j} (1) a _ {j} \} ^ {2} | | \tilde {\theta} _ {j} | | ^ {2} = \theta_ {j} (1) ^ {2} | \mathfrak {L}: \Re | a _ {j} = \tilde {\theta} _ {j} (1) \theta_ {j} (1) a _ {j} = \frac {\tilde {\theta} _ {j} (1) ^ {2}}{| | \tilde {\theta} _ {j} | | ^ {2}}
$$

(11.7) yields that

$$
\sum_ {s} \frac {\lambda_ {1 s} (1) ^ {2}}{\| \lambda_ {1 s} \| ^ {2}} \geq | \mathfrak {L}: \mathfrak {H} _ {2} | - | \mathfrak {L}: \mathfrak {H} |,
$$

where $\mathcal{S}_1 = \{\lambda_{1s}\}$ or equivalently

$$
\sum_ {s} \frac {\mathbb {1} _ {1 s} ^ {2}}{\| \lambda_ {1 s} \| ^ {2}} \geq \frac {| \mathfrak {L} : \mathfrak {H} _ {2} | - | \mathfrak {L} : \mathfrak {H} |}{d ^ {2} | \mathfrak {L} : \mathfrak {R} | ^ {2}}.\tag{11.8}
$$

Since $\mathfrak{H}$ is nilpotent $\mathfrak{H}_2 / \mathfrak{H}_3$ is in the center of $\mathfrak{H} / \mathfrak{H}_3$. Every irreducible character of $\mathfrak{R}$ is a constituent of a character induced by an irreducible character of $\mathfrak{H}$. Thus for $2 \leq m \leq k$, Lemma 4.1 implies that

$$
\angle_ {m 1} d \mid \mathfrak {L}: \mathfrak {R} \mid \leq \sqrt {\mid \mathfrak {H} : \mathfrak {H} _ {2} \mid} \mid \mathfrak {L}: \mathfrak {H} \mid ,
$$

or equivalently

$$
\angle_ {m 1} \leq \frac {| \mathfrak {R} : \mathfrak {H} | \sqrt {| \mathfrak {H} : \mathfrak {H} _ {2} |}}{d}.\tag{11.9}
$$

Suppose now that inequality (10.2) is violated for some value of m. Then (11.8) and (11.9) yield that

$$
\frac {| \mathfrak {L} : \mathfrak {H} _ {2} | - | \mathfrak {L} : \mathfrak {H} |}{d ^ {2} | \mathfrak {L} : \mathfrak {R} | ^ {2}} \leq \frac {2 | \mathfrak {R} : \mathfrak {H} | \sqrt {| \mathfrak {H} : \mathfrak {H} _ {2} |}}{d}.
$$

Thus

$$
\left\{\left| \mathfrak {H}: \mathfrak {H} _ {2} \right| - 1 \right\} \leq 2 d | \mathfrak {L}: \mathfrak {R} | \sqrt {| \mathfrak {H} : \mathfrak {H} _ {2} |},
$$

or

$$
\left| \mathfrak {H}: \mathfrak {H} _ {2} \right| ^ {2} - 2 \left| \mathfrak {H}: \mathfrak {H} _ {2} \right| + 1 \leq 4 d ^ {2} \left| \mathfrak {L}: \mathfrak {R} \right| ^ {2} \left| \mathfrak {H}: \mathfrak {H} _ {2} \right|.
$$

Since every term is an integer this implies that

$$
\left| \mathfrak {H}: \mathfrak {H} _ {2} \right| - 1 \leq 4 d ^ {2} \left| \mathfrak {L}: \mathfrak {R} \right| ^ {2}.\tag{11.10}
$$

However $\mathfrak{S}_2 \subseteq \mathfrak{S}_1$, thus $|\mathfrak{S} : \mathfrak{S}_2| \geq |\mathfrak{S} : \mathfrak{S}_1|$. Now (11.5) and (11.10) are incompatible. Therefore inequality (10.2), and thus all the assumptions of Theorem 10.1, are satisfied. Hence by that theorem $\mathcal{S}(\mathfrak{S}_3)$ is coherent contrary to the minimal nature of $\mathfrak{S}_2$. This finally implies that $\mathfrak{S}_2 = \langle 1 \rangle$. Therefore $\mathcal{S} = \mathcal{S}(\mathfrak{S}_2)$ is coherent. The proof is complete.

The remainder of this section consists of applications of Theorem 11.1. Lemmas 11.1 and 11.2 are closely related to Theorem 2 of [8]. By using the argument of that theorem the assumption that $|\mathfrak{B}|$ is odd in the following lemmas can be replaced by suitable weaker assumptions. However the stronger results are not relevant to this paper and will not be proved here.

Hypothesis 11.2.

(i) Hypothesis 11.1 and equation (11.2) are satisfied. Thus $d = 1$.

(ii) $|\mathfrak{L}|$ is odd and $\mathfrak{L}/\mathfrak{S}'$ is a Frobenius group with Frobenius kernel $\mathfrak{S}/\mathfrak{S}'$.

LEMMA 11.1. Suppose that Hypothesis 11.2 is satisfied. If

$$
\left| \mathfrak {H}: \mathfrak {H} ^ {\prime} \right| > 4 \left| \mathfrak {L}: \mathfrak {H} \right| ^ {2} + 1
$$

then $\mathcal{S}$ is coherent.

Proof. By Lemma 10.1 and 3.16 (iii) $\mathcal{S}(\mathfrak{S}')$ is coherent. The result now follows from Theorem 11.1.

LEMMA 11.2. Suppose that Hypothesis 11.2 is satisfied. Then $\mathcal{S}$ is coherent except possibly if $\mathfrak{S}$ is a non abelian $p$-group for some prime $p$ and

$$
\left| \mathfrak {H}: \mathfrak {H} ^ {\prime} \right| \leq 4 \left| \mathfrak {L}: \mathfrak {H} \right| ^ {2} + 1.
$$

Proof. If $\mathfrak{H} = \mathfrak{H}_1 \times \mathfrak{H}_2$, where $\mathfrak{H}_1$ and $\mathfrak{H}_2$ are proper normal subgroups of $\mathfrak{L}$, then

$$
\left| \mathfrak {H} _ {i}: \mathfrak {H} _ {i} ^ {\prime} \right| \equiv 1 (\mathrm{mod} | \mathfrak {L}: \mathfrak {H} |) \quad \text { for } i = 1, 2.
$$

Since $|\mathfrak{L}|$ is odd, this implies that

$$
\left| \mathfrak {H} _ {i}: \mathfrak {H} _ {i} ^ {\prime} \right| \geq 2 \left| \mathfrak {L}: \mathfrak {H} \right| + 1 \quad \text { for } i = 1, 2.
$$

Hence $|\mathfrak{X}:\mathfrak{X}'| > 4|\mathfrak{X}:\mathfrak{X}|^2 + 1$ and $\mathcal{S}$ is coherent by Lemma 11.1. As $\mathfrak{X}$ is nilpotent this implies that $\mathcal{S}$ is coherent if $\mathfrak{X}$ is not a $p$-group for any prime $p$. Since $|\mathfrak{X}|$ is odd

$$
| \mathcal {S} | \geq \frac {| \mathfrak {H} : \mathfrak {H} ^ {\prime} | - 1}{| \mathfrak {L} : \mathfrak {H} |} \geq 2.
$$

Thus by Lemma 10.1 S is coherent if $\mathfrak{H}$ is abelian. The result now follows directly from Lemma 11.1.

LEMMA 11.3. Suppose that Hypothesis 11.2 is satisfied and $\mathfrak{L}$ is a Frobenius group with Frobenius kernel $\mathfrak{S}$. Assume that $\mathfrak{S}$ is a $p$-group for some prime $p$ and $|\mathfrak{S}:D(\mathfrak{S})| = p^2$. Then $\mathcal{S}$ is coherent.

Proof. If $\mathfrak{S}$ is abelian Lemma 11.2 implies that $\mathcal{S}$ is coherent. If $\mathfrak{S}$ is not abelian then the second term of the descending central series modulo the third is cyclic. Thus

$$
p \equiv 1 (\mathrm{mod} | \mathfrak {L}: \mathfrak {H} |).
$$

Therefore $(p - 1) \geq 2|\mathfrak{L}:\mathfrak{H}|$ as $|\mathfrak{L}|$ is odd. Hence

$$
\left| \mathfrak {H}: \mathfrak {H} ^ {\prime} \right| \geq p ^ {2} > 4 \left| \mathfrak {L}: \mathfrak {H} \right| ^ {2} + 1
$$

and the result follows from Lemma 11.1.

LEMMA 11.4. Suppose that Hypothesis 11.2 is satisfied and $\mathfrak{L}$ is a Frobenius group with Frobenius kernel $\mathfrak{S}$. Assume that $\mathfrak{S}$ is a $p$-group for some prime $p$ and $|\mathfrak{S}:D(\mathfrak{S})| = p^3$. If

$$
p ^ {3} - 1 > 2 p \mid \mathfrak {L}: \mathfrak {H} \mid\tag{11.11}
$$

then $\mathcal{S}$ is coherent.

Proof. If $\mathfrak{H}$ is abelian Lemma 11.2 implies that $\mathcal{S}$ is coherent. If $\mathfrak{H}$ is non-abelian let $\mathfrak{H}_1$ be a subgroup of $D(\mathfrak{H})$ such that $D(\mathfrak{H}) / \mathfrak{H}_1$ is a chief factor of $\mathfrak{L}$. As $\mathfrak{H}$ is nilpotent $D(\mathfrak{H}) / \mathfrak{H}_1$ is in the center of $\mathfrak{H} / \mathfrak{H}_1$. Thus by Lemma 4.1 the degree of any irreducible character of $\mathfrak{H} / \mathfrak{H}_1$ is either 1 or $p$. Hence the degree of any character in $\mathcal{S}(\mathfrak{H}_1)$

is either $|\mathfrak{L}:\mathfrak{H}|$ or $|\mathfrak{L}:\mathfrak{H}|p$. Let $\mathcal{S}_1, \mathcal{S}_2$ be the subsets of $\mathcal{S}(\mathfrak{H}_1)$ consisting of all the characters of degree $|\mathfrak{L}:\mathfrak{H}|, |\mathfrak{L}:\mathfrak{H}|p$ respectively. Let $\angle_1 = 1, \angle_2 = p$. By (11.11)

$$
\left| \mathcal {S} _ {1} \right| \geq \frac {p ^ {8} - 1}{\left| \mathfrak {L} : \mathfrak {H} \right|} > 2 p = 2 / _ {2}.
$$

Thus by Theorem 10.1 $\mathcal{S}(\mathfrak{H}_i)$ is coherent.

If $|D(\mathfrak{Q}) : \mathfrak{Q}_1| = p$ or $p^2$, then $p \equiv 1 \pmod{| \mathfrak{Q} : \mathfrak{Q}|}$ or

$$
p ^ {2} - 1 \equiv 0 (\mathrm{mod} | \mathfrak {L}: \mathfrak {H} |).
$$

As $(p^3 - 1, p^2 - 1) = p - 1$ this yields that in either case

$$
\boldsymbol {p} \equiv 1 (\mathrm{mod} | \mathfrak {L}: \mathfrak {H} |).
$$

Therefore $p - 1 \geq 2|\mathfrak{L}:\mathfrak{H}|$. Hence

$$
\left| \mathfrak {H}: \mathfrak {H} ^ {\prime} \right| \geq \left| \mathfrak {H}: D (\mathfrak {H}) \right| = p ^ {3} > 4 \left| \mathfrak {L}: \mathfrak {H} \right| ^ {2} + 1
$$

and $\mathcal{S}$ is coherent by Lemma 11.1. Suppose that $|D(\mathfrak{H}):\mathfrak{H}_1|\geq p^3$. Then by (11.11)

$$
\left| \mathfrak {H}: \mathfrak {H} _ {1} \right| \geq p ^ {6} > 4 \left| \mathfrak {L}: \mathfrak {H} \right| ^ {2} + 1.
$$

Since $\mathcal{S}(\mathfrak{H}_1)$ is coherent the result now follows from Theorem 11.1.

The next two lemmas involve the following situation:

Hypothesis 11.3.

(i) Hypothesis 11.2 is satisfied.

(ii) There exist primes $p, q$ and positive integers $a, b$ such that $|\mathfrak{L}:\mathfrak{H}| = p^b, |\mathfrak{H}:\mathfrak{H}'| = |\mathfrak{H}:D(\mathfrak{H})| = q^a$. Thus $|\mathfrak{H}|$ is a power of $q$.

LEMMA 11.5. Suppose that Hypothesis 11.3 is satisfied and $a = 2c$ is even. Then $\mathcal{S}$ is coherent except possibly if $q^c + 1 = 2p^b$, $q^c$ is the smallest degree of any non linear irreducible character of $\mathfrak{Q}$ whose kernel contains $[\mathfrak{Q}, \mathfrak{Q}']$ and for no subgroup $\mathfrak{Q}_1$ of $\mathfrak{Q}'$ with $\mathfrak{Q}_1 \neq \mathfrak{Q}'$, $\mathfrak{Q}_1 \triangleleft \mathfrak{Q}$ is $\mathfrak{L}/\mathfrak{Q}_1$ a Frobenius group.

Proof. Suppose that $\mathcal{S}$ is not coherent. Then by Lemma 11.1 $4p^{2b} + 1 \geq q^a$. As $(q^c + 1, q^c - 1) = 2$ it follows that $2p^b | q^c + 1$ or $2p^b | q^c - 1$. If $2p^b \neq q^c + 1$ this implies that $4p^{2b} + 1 < q^a$ contrary to what has been proved above. Therefore $q^c + 1 = 2p^b$.

Let $\mathcal{T}_i = \{\theta_{ij}\}$ be the set of non principal irreducible characters of $\mathfrak{Q} / [\mathfrak{Q},\mathfrak{Q}']$ of degree $q^i$. Lemma 4.1 implies that $\mathcal{T}_i$ is empty for $i > c$. Let $\mathcal{S}_i = \{\lambda_{ij}\}$ be the set of characters in $\mathcal{S}$ of degree $q^i p^b$.

Since $|\mathcal{S}_0| = 2(q^c - 1) > 2q^{c-1}$, it follows from Hypothesis 11.1 and Theorem 10.1 that $\bigcup_{i=0}^{c-1} \mathcal{S}_i$ is coherent. Suppose that $\bigcup_{i=1}^{c-1} \mathcal{T}_i$ is non empty. Then 3.15 implies that

$$
\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {c - 1} \sum_ {j} \theta_ {i j} (1) ^ {2} \geq q ^ {2 c}.
$$

Therefore

$$
\sum_ {i = 0} ^ {c - 1} \sum_ {j} \frac {q ^ {2 i}}{\| \lambda_ {i j} \| ^ {2}} = \frac {1}{p ^ {2 b}} \sum_ {i = 0} ^ {c - 1} \sum_ {j} \frac {\lambda_ {i j} (1) ^ {2}}{\| \lambda_ {i j} \| ^ {2}} = | \mathcal {S} _ {0} | + \frac {1}{p ^ {b}} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {c - 1} \sum_ {j} \theta_ {i j} (1) ^ {2} > 2 q ^ {c}.
$$

Thus by Theorem 10.1

$$
\bigcup_ {i = 0} ^ {c} \mathcal {S} _ {i} \supseteq \mathcal {S} ([ \mathfrak {H}, \mathfrak {H} ^ {\prime} ])
$$

is coherent. Since

$$
4 \left| \mathfrak {L}: \mathfrak {H} \right| ^ {2} + 1 = 4 p ^ {2 b} + 1 <   q ^ {a + 1} \leq \left| \mathfrak {H}: [ \mathfrak {H}, \mathfrak {H} ^ {\prime} ] \right|,
$$

Theorem 11.1 implies that $\mathcal{S}$ is coherent. Thus it may be assumed that $q^{c}$ is the smallest degree of any non linear irreducible character of $\mathfrak{H}/[\mathfrak{H},\mathfrak{H}^{\prime}]$.

Suppose now that $\mathfrak{Q}'$ contains a subgroup $\mathfrak{Q}_1 \neq \mathfrak{Q}'$ such that $\mathfrak{L}/\mathfrak{Q}_1$ is a Frobenius group. Then $\mathfrak{Q}_1$ may be chosen so that $\mathfrak{Q}'/\mathfrak{Q}_1$ is a chief factor of $\mathfrak{L}$. Thus $[\mathfrak{Q}, \mathfrak{Q}'] \subseteq \mathfrak{Q}_1$ and by the earlier part of the lemma every irreducible character of $\mathfrak{Q}/\mathfrak{Q}_1$ has degree either 1 or $q^c$. As $q^c + 1 = 2p^b$, $q^{2c}$ is the smallest power of $q$ which satisfies $q^{2c} \equiv 1 (\text{mod } p^b)$. Since $\mathfrak{Q}'/\mathfrak{Q}_1$ is a chief factor of $\mathfrak{L}$ this implies that $\mathfrak{Q}'/\mathfrak{Q}_1$ is in the center of $\mathfrak{Q}/\mathfrak{Q}_1$ and $|\mathfrak{Q}': \mathfrak{Q}_1| = q^{2c}$. If $\theta$ is an irreducible character of $\mathfrak{Q}/\mathfrak{Q}_1$ of degree $q^c$, then the orthogonality relations yield that $\theta(H) = 0$ for $H \in \mathfrak{Q}/\mathfrak{Q}_1 - \mathfrak{Q}'/\mathfrak{Q}_1$. As every non linear character of $\mathfrak{Q}/\mathfrak{Q}_1$ has degree $q^c$ the orthogonality relations may once again be used. They imply that

$$
\mid C (H) \mid = q ^ {2 c} \quad \text { for } H \in \mathfrak {H} / \mathfrak {H} _ {1} - \mathfrak {H} ^ {\prime} / \mathfrak {H} _ {1}.\tag{11.13}
$$

However

$$
\langle H, \mathfrak {H} ^ {\prime} / \mathfrak {H} _ {1} \rangle \subseteq C (H)
$$

which contradicts (11.13). Thus $\mathfrak{Q}'$ contains no subgroup $\mathfrak{Q}_1 \neq \mathfrak{Q}'$ such that $\mathfrak{Q} / \mathfrak{Q}_1$ is a Frobenius group. All statements in the lemma are proved.

LEMMA 11.6. Suppose that Hypothesis 11.3 is satisfied. Assume further that $a$ is odd and $p = 3$. Then $\mathcal{S}$ is coherent.

Proof. As $a$ is odd and $q^a \equiv 1 \pmod{3}$, it follows that $q \equiv 1 \pmod{3}$. Define the integer $c \geq 1$ by

$$
q \equiv 1 (\mathrm{mod} 3 ^ {c}), \quad q \not \equiv 1 (\mathrm{mod} 3 ^ {c + 1}).
$$

If $b \leq c$, then $q \geq 2.3^b + 1$. Thus if $a \neq 1, 4 \cdot 3^{2b} + 1 < q^a$ and $\mathcal{S}$ is coherent by Lemma 11.1. If $a = 1$, then $\mathfrak{S}$ is cyclic. Therefore $\mathcal{S}$ is coherent by Lemma 10.1.

Suppose now that $b > c$. Then since $q^a \equiv 1 \pmod{3^b}$ we must have $a = 3^{b - c}x$ for some integer $x$. Therefore

$$
q ^ {a} \geq (q ^ {3 b - c - 1}) ^ {3}.
$$

Since $q^{3^b - c - 1} \equiv 1 \pmod{3^{b - 1}}$, this yields that

$$
q ^ {a} \geq (1 + 2 \cdot 3 ^ {b - 1}) ^ {3}.\tag{11.14}
$$

If $4.3^{2b} + 1 < q^a$ then $\mathcal{S}$ is coherent by Lemma 11.1. Thus if $\mathcal{S}$ is not coherent (11.14) implies that

$$
4. 3 ^ {2 b} + 1 \geq q ^ {a} \geq (1 + 2. 3 ^ {b - 1}) ^ {3} > 8. 3 ^ {3 (b - 1)} + 1.
$$

Therefore $3^3 > 2.3^b$. Hence $b = 1$ or $b = 2$. In either case this implies that $q^a \leq 4.3^4 + 1 < 7^3$. As $a \equiv 0 \pmod{3}$ we get that $q < 7$. However $q \equiv 1 \pmod{3}$. This contradiction arose from assuming that $\mathcal{S}$ is not coherent. The proof is complete.

## 12. Further Results about Tamely Imbedded Subsets

In this section a fairly special situation is studied. Our purpose here is to get some information about certain sets of characters which may not be coherent.

Hypothesis 12.1.

(i) Let $q$ be a prime and let $\mathfrak{Q}$ be a $S_{q}$-subgroup of the group $\mathfrak{X}$. Assume that $\mathfrak{Q} = \widehat{\mathfrak{X}}$ is tamely imbedded in $\mathfrak{X}$ and $\mathfrak{L} = N(\mathfrak{Q}) \neq \mathfrak{Q}$ has odd order. Let $\mathfrak{Q}_1 \triangleleft \mathfrak{L}, \mathfrak{Q}_1 \subset \mathfrak{Q}$ and let $\overline{\mathfrak{Q}} = \mathfrak{Q}/\mathfrak{Q}_1, \overline{\mathfrak{L}} = \mathfrak{L}/\mathfrak{Q}_1$.

(ii) $\mathcal{L}$ is the set of all characters of $\mathfrak{L}$ which are induced by non-principal irreducible characters of $\bar{\mathfrak{D}}$. Define an equivalence relation on $\mathcal{L}$ by the condition that two characters are equivalent if and only if they have the same degree and the same weight. Then each equivalence class of $\mathcal{L}$ is either subcoherent in $\mathcal{L}$ or consists of irreducible characters.

(iii) Let $1 = q^{f_0} < q^{f_1} \cdots$ be all the integers which are degrees of irreducible characters of $\mathfrak{D}$. Let $n > 0$ be a fixed integer. For $0 \leq i \leq n - 1$ let $\mathcal{S}_i$ be the set of all characters in $\mathscr{L}$ of degree

$q^{f_{i}}|\mathfrak{L}:\mathfrak{Q}|$ . Assume that each  $S_{i}$  consists of irreducible characters. Let  $S_{n}$  be an equivalence class in L consisting of characters of degree  $q^{f_{n}}|\mathfrak{L}:\mathfrak{Q}|$ . Let  $S=\bigcup_{i=0}^{n}S_{i}$ .

In case Hypothesis 12.1 is satisfied the following notation will be used.

$$
\mid \bar {\mathfrak {Q}}: \bar {\mathfrak {Q}} ^ {\prime} \mid = q ^ {a}, \quad \mid \bar {\mathfrak {Q}}: \bar {\mathfrak {Q}} \mid = e > 1.\tag{12.1}
$$

Since $|\mathfrak{L}|$ is odd, $|\mathcal{S}_i| \geq 2$ and $\mathcal{I}_0(\mathcal{S}_i) \neq 0$ for $0 \leq i \leq n$. Thus by Lemma 10.1 $\mathcal{S}_i$ is coherent for $0 \leq i \leq n - 1$.

For $0 \leq i < n$ let $a_i$ be the number of non principal irreducible characters of $\bar{\Omega}$ of degree $q^{f_i}$. By Hypothesis 12.1 $\bar{\mathfrak{L}}/\bar{\mathfrak{L}}$ acts regularly as a permutation group on the non principal irreducible characters of degree $q^{f_i}$ for $0 \leq i < n$. Since $|\mathfrak{L}|$ is odd, no non principal irreducible character of $\mathfrak{L}$ is real. Thus $a_i$ is even. Therefore

$$
a _ {i} \equiv 0 (\mathrm{mod} 2 e), | \mathcal {S} _ {i} | = \frac {a _ {i}}{e} \quad \text { for } 0 \leq i \leq n - 1.\tag{12.2}
$$

Let $j_0 = 0$. Define $j_s$ inductively to be the largest integer not exceeding $n + 1$ such that $\bigcup_{i=j_{s-1}}^{j_{s}-1} S_i$ is coherent. Suppose that

$$
0 = j _ {0} <   \dots <   j _ {t} <   j _ {t + 1} = n + 1.
$$

For $0 \leq s \leq t$, define

$$
\mathcal {T} _ {i} = \bigcup_ {i = j _ {s}} ^ {j _ {s + 1} - 1} \mathcal {S} _ {i}\tag{12.3}
$$

and let $m_{s} = f_{j_{s}}$. Define

$$
c _ {s} = \sum_ {i} a _ {i} q ^ {2 (f _ {i} - m _ {s})} \quad \text { for } 0 \leq s \leq t ,\tag{12.4}
$$

where i ranges from  $j_{s}$  to  $j_{s+1}-1$ . Define

$$
d _ {s} = q ^ {m _ {s + 1} - m _ {s}} \quad \text { for } 0 \leq s <   t.\tag{12.5}
$$

Then by Theorem 10.1 applied to $\mathcal{T}_{s} \cup \mathcal{S}_{j_{s+1}}$

(12.6)

$$
c _ {s} \leq 2 e d _ {s} \quad \text { for } 0 \leq s <   t.\tag{By (12.2}
$$

(12.7)

$$
c _ {s} \equiv 0 (\mathrm{mod} 2 e) \quad \text { for } 0 \leq s <   t.\tag{By 3.15}
$$

$$
1 + \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {s} c _ {j} q ^ {2 m _ {j}} \equiv 0 (\mathrm{mod} q ^ {2 m _ {s + 1}}) \quad \text { for } 0 \leq s <   t.\tag{12.8}
$$

LEMMA 12.1. Suppose that Hypothesis 12.1 is satisfied. Assume that

$$
\mid \bar {\mathfrak {D}}: \bar {\mathfrak {D}} ^ {\prime} \mid = q ^ {a} \leq 4 e ^ {2} + 1.
$$

Then

$$
\frac {d _ {s} ^ {2} e}{c _ {s}} <   e + 1 \quad f o r 0 \leq s <   t.
$$

Furthermore if $a$ is odd, $c_{s} < e^{2}$ and $c_{s} \not\equiv 0 \pmod{q}$, then

$$
\frac {d _ {s} ^ {2} e}{c _ {s}} <   e - 1.
$$

Proof. We will first prove that

$$
1 + \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {s - 1} c _ {j} q ^ {2 m _ {j}} <   e q ^ {2 m _ {s}} \quad \text { for } 0 \leq s <   t.\tag{12.9}
$$

This is true if $s = 0$ since $1 < e$. Suppose that $s > 0$. Then by (12.5) and (12.6)

$$
\begin{array}{r l} 1 + \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {s - 1} c _ {j} q ^ {2 m _ {j}} & \leq 1 + 2 e \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {s - 1} q ^ {m _ {j} + m _ {j + 1}} \\ & \leq 1 + 2 e (1 + q + \dots + q ^ {2 m _ {s} - 1}) \\ & \leq 1 + 2 e \frac {(q ^ {2 m _ {s}} - 1)}{(q - 1)} \leq 1 + e (q ^ {2 m _ {s}} - 1) <   e q ^ {2 m _ {s}}. \end{array}
$$

Assume now that the lemma is false and choose s minimum to violate the result. Let  $c = c_{s}, d = d_{s}$ .

By (12.8) and (12.9)

$$
q ^ {2 m _ {s} + 1} <   e q ^ {2 m _ {s}} + c q ^ {2 m _ {s}}.
$$

Hence by (12.5)

$$
d ^ {2} <   e + c.\tag{12.10}
$$

Inequalities (12.6) and (12.10) yield that  $d^{2} < e + 2ed$  or  $d^{2} - 2ed - e < 0$ . This implies that

$$
e - \sqrt {e ^ {2} + e} \leq d \leq e + \sqrt {e ^ {2} + e}.
$$

Consequently

$$
d \leq e + \sqrt {e ^ {2} + e} <   3 e.\tag{12.11}
$$

Suppose that

$$
1 + \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {s} c _ {j} q ^ {2 m _ {j}} \geq 3 q ^ {2 m _ {s + 1}}.
$$

Then by (12.9)

$$
3 q ^ {2 m _ {s} + 1} <   (e + c) q ^ {2 m _ {s}}.
$$

Hence by (12.7) $3d_{\bullet}^{2}<e+c\leq 3c/2$ . Thus

$$
\frac {d ^ {2} e}{c} \leq \frac {e}{2} <   e - 1
$$

since e > 2. This contradicts the choice of s. Hence

$$
1 + \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {s} c _ {j} q ^ {2 m _ {j}} <   3 q ^ {2 m _ {s + 1}}.
$$

As $c_{j}$ is even for $0 \leq j \leq s$, (12.8) implies that

$$
1 + \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {s} c _ {j} q ^ {2 m _ {j}} = q ^ {2 m _ {s + 1}}.\tag{12.12}
$$

The group $\bar{\Omega}$ contains a normal subgroup $\bar{\Omega}_0$ of index $q^{2m_{s+1}}$. Every irreducible character of $\bar{\Omega}/\bar{\Omega}_0$ has degree strictly less than $q^{m_{s+1}}$ and the sum of the squares of the degrees of these characters is equal to $q^{2m_{s+1}}$. Hence (12.12) implies that every character of $\bar{\Omega}$ whose degree is strictly less than $q^{m_{s+1}}$ has $\bar{\Omega}_0$ in its kernel. Thus $\bar{\Omega}_0$ is a normal subgroup of $\bar{\Omega}$ and $\bar{\Omega}/\bar{\Omega}_0$ is a Frobenius group with Frobenius kernel $\bar{\Omega}/\bar{\Omega}_0$. Therefore

$$
q ^ {2 m _ {s + 1}} \equiv d ^ {2} q ^ {2 m _ {s}} \equiv 1 (\mathrm{mod} e),\tag{12.13}
$$

and the center of $\bar{\mathfrak{D}}/\bar{\mathfrak{D}}_{0}$ has order at least $q^{a}$. Thus by Lemma 4.1 $q^{2m_{s}} \leq q^{2m_{s+1}-a}$. This yields that

$$
q ^ {a} \leq d ^ {2}.\tag{12.14}
$$

Define the integer k by

$$
c + k = d ^ {2}.\tag{12.15}
$$

By (12.10) $k < e$ and by (12.12) $0 < k$. Thus

$$
0 <   k <   e.\tag{12.16}
$$

Define the integer $b$ by

$$
q ^ {2 m _ {s}} \equiv q ^ {a - b} (\mathrm{mod} e), \quad 0 \leq b \leq a - 1.\tag{12.17}
$$

Equations (12.7), (12.13), (12.15) and (12.17) imply that

$$
k \equiv d ^ {2} \equiv q ^ {b - a} \equiv q ^ {b} (\mathrm{mod} e).\tag{12.18}
$$

If $b = 0$, then by (12.16) and (12.18) $k = 1$. Thus by (12.15) $c = d^2 - 1$, hence by (12.7)

$$
\frac {d ^ {2} e}{c} = \frac {(c + 1) e}{c} = e + \frac {e}{c} <   e + 1.
$$

If $c < e^2$ and $a$ is odd, then

$$
d ^ {2} = c + 1 <   e ^ {2} + 1 <   q ^ {2 a}.
$$

Thus by (12.18) $d^2 = q^a$. However this is impossible as $a$ is odd. Assume now that $b \neq 0$. As $d^2$ is a power of $q$, (12.14) and (12.18) imply that either $d^2 = q^{a+b}$ or $d^2 \geq q^{2a+b}$. Since $b \neq 0$, the latter case leads to

$$
d ^ {2} \geq q ^ {2 a + b} = q ^ {2 a} q ^ {b} > 4 e ^ {2} q > 9 e ^ {2}.
$$

Hence $d > 3e$ contrary to (12.11). Thus

$$
d ^ {2} = q ^ {a + b}, \quad 2 \leq a - b.\tag{12.19}
$$

The inequality follows from (12.17) and the fact that  $a + b$  is even. Now (12.11) and (12.19) yield that

$$
q ^ {2 b} = \frac {q ^ {a + b}}{q ^ {a - b}} = \frac {d ^ {2}}{q ^ {a - b}} <   \frac {9 e ^ {2}}{q ^ {2}} \leq e ^ {2}.
$$

Thus $1 \leq q^{b} < e$. (12.16) and (12.18) imply that

$$
k = q ^ {b}, \quad b > 0.\tag{12.20}
$$

Equation (12.15) now becomes $d^2 = c + q^b$. Hence

$$
\boldsymbol {c} \equiv 0 (\mathrm{mod} q).
$$

Furthermore by (12.19)

$$
c = d ^ {2} - q ^ {b} = q ^ {b} (q ^ {a} - 1) .
$$

Consequently

$$
\frac {d ^ {2} e}{c} = \frac {q ^ {a + b} e}{q ^ {b} (q ^ {a} - 1)} = \frac {q ^ {a} e}{q ^ {a} - 1} = e + \frac {e}{q ^ {a} - 1} <   e + 1.
$$

THEOREM 12.1. Suppose that Hypothesis 12.1 is satisfied. Assume that for some $j$ with $0 \leq j \leq n - 1$, $\lambda_1 \in \mathcal{S}_j$ and $\lambda_2 \in \mathcal{S}_{j+1}$. Define

$$
\alpha = q ^ {f _ {j + 1} - f _ {j}} \lambda_ {1} - \lambda_ {2}.
$$

Suppose that $\mathcal{S}_j\subseteq \mathcal{T}$ and

$$
\alpha^ {\tau} = \Delta + \Delta_ {1}
$$

where $\Delta_{1}\in\mathcal{I}(\mathcal{T}_{\bullet}^{\tau})$ and $\Delta$ is orthogonal to $\mathcal{I}(\mathcal{T}_{\bullet}^{\tau})$. Then

$$
\left\| \Delta \right\| ^ {2} \leq e + \left\| \lambda_ {2} \right\| ^ {2}.
$$

Furthermore if $a$ is odd, $c = c_{*} < e^{2}$ and $c \not\equiv 0 \pmod{q}$ then

$$
\| \Delta \| ^ {2} \leq e + \| \lambda_ {2} \| ^ {2} - 2.
$$

Proof. Let $\mathcal{T} = \mathcal{T}_{s}$. If $\mathcal{S}_{j+1} \subseteq \mathcal{T}$ then $\alpha^{\tau} \in \mathcal{I}(\mathcal{T})^{\tau}$ and $A = 0$. Thus the result is trivial in this case. Hence it may be assumed that $\mathcal{S}_{j+1} \not\subseteq \mathcal{T}$. In particular, $\mathcal{S}$ is not coherent, hence $\mathcal{L}$ is not coherent, so by Lemma 11.1 $|\bar{\mathfrak{Q}}: \bar{\mathfrak{Q}}'| \leq 4e^{2} + 1$. Consequently Lemma 12.1 may be applied. Furthermore $f_{j+1} = m_{s+1}$ and $s < t$. Thus $\mathcal{T}$ consists of irreducible characters. Let $\mathcal{T} = \{\lambda_{s i} | 1 \leq i \leq n_s\}$, where the notation is chosen so that $\lambda_1 \neq \lambda_{s 1}$ and $\lambda_{s i}(1) | \lambda_{s i+1}(1)$ for $1 \leq i < n_s$. Suppose that $\lambda_1 = \lambda_{s k}$. Define the integer $x$ by $(\alpha^{\tau}, \lambda_{s 1}^{\tau}) = -x$. Then since $\alpha \in \mathcal{I}_0(\mathcal{S})$ Lemma 9.4 implies that

$$
\left(\alpha^ {r}, \lambda_ {s i} ^ {r}\right) = - x \frac {\lambda_ {s i} (1)}{\lambda_ {s 1} (1)} + \delta_ {i k} q ^ {m _ {s + 1} - f _ {j}} \quad \text { for } 1 \leq i \leq n _ {s}.
$$

Then

$$
\Delta_ {1} = q ^ {m _ {s + 1} - f _ {j}} \lambda_ {s k} ^ {\tau} - x \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \frac {\lambda_ {s i} (1)}{\lambda_ {s 1} (1)} \lambda_ {s i} ^ {\tau}.
$$

Therefore

$$
\begin{array}{r l} & {\| \mathcal {A} \| ^ {2} = \| \alpha^ {\tau} \| ^ {2} - \| \mathcal {A} _ {1} \| ^ {2} = q ^ {2 (m _ {s + 1} - f _ {j})}} \\ & {\quad + \| \lambda_ {2} \| ^ {2} - x ^ {2} \frac {c}{e} - q ^ {2 (m _ {s + 1} - f _ {j})} + 2 x \frac {\lambda_ {s k} (1)}{\lambda_ {s 1} (1)} q ^ {m _ {s + 1} - f _ {j}},} \end{array}\tag{12.21}
$$

where $c = c_s$ is defined by (12.4). Let $d = d_s$ be defined by (12.5). Since $\lambda_{s1}(1) = q^{m_s}$ and $\lambda_{sk}(1) = q^{f_j}$ (12.21) yields that

$$
\left| \left| \Delta \right| \right| ^ {2} = \left| \left| \lambda_ {2} \right| \right| ^ {2} + 2 x d - \frac {x ^ {2} c}{e}.\tag{12.22}
$$

As a function of $x, 2xd - (x^2c / e)$ assumes its maximum at $x = ed / c$. Thus (12.22) implies that

$$
\| \Delta \| ^ {2} \leq \| \lambda_ {2} \| ^ {2} + 2 \frac {e d ^ {2}}{c} - \frac {e d ^ {2}}{c} = \| \lambda_ {2} \| ^ {2} + \frac {e d ^ {2}}{c}.\tag{12.23}
$$

As $||\Delta||^2$ is an integer Lemma 12.1 and (12.23) imply that $||\Delta||^2 \leq ||\lambda_2||^2 + e$. Furthermore if $a$ is odd, $c < e^2$ and $c \not\equiv 0 \pmod{q}$, then

$$
\left\| \Delta \right\| ^ {2} \leq \left\| \lambda_ {2} \right\| ^ {2} + e - 2.
$$

The proof is complete.

## 13. Self Normalizing Cyclic Subgroups

Hypothesis 13.1.

(i) $\mathfrak{W}$ is a cyclic subgroup of the group $\mathfrak{X}$ with $|\mathfrak{W}| = w$ odd. Suppose that $\mathfrak{W} = \mathfrak{W}_1 \times \mathfrak{W}_2$, where $w_i = |\mathfrak{W}_i|$ and $w_i \neq 1$ for $i = 1, 2$. Let

$$
\hat {\mathfrak {W}} = \mathfrak {W} - \mathfrak {W} _ {1} - \mathfrak {W} _ {2}.
$$

For any non empty subset $\mathfrak{A}$ of $\hat{\mathfrak{W}}$

$$
\boldsymbol {C} (\mathfrak {A}) = \boldsymbol {N} (\mathfrak {A}) = \mathfrak {W}.\tag{13.1}
$$

(ii) Let $\omega_{10}, \omega_{01}$ be faithful irreducible characters of $\mathfrak{W}/\mathfrak{W}_2, \mathfrak{W}/\mathfrak{W}_1$ respectively. Define

$$
\omega_ {i j} \doteq \omega_ {1 0} ^ {i} \omega_ {0 1} ^ {j}
$$

for $0 \leq i \leq w_{1} - 1, 0 \leq j \leq w_{2} - 1$.

If  $w_{1}, w_{2}$  in Hypothesis 13.1 are both primes then (13.1) follows from the assumption that  $N(\mathfrak{W}) = \mathfrak{W}$ . Thus the situation described above is a generalization of this case.

LEMMA 13.1. Suppose that Hypothesis 13.1 is satisfied. Then $\hat{\mathfrak{W}}$ is a T.I. set in $\mathfrak{X}$. There exists an orthonormal set $\{\eta_{ij} | 0 \leq i \leq w_1 - 1, 0 \leq j \leq w_2 - 1\}$ of generalized characters of $\mathfrak{X}$ such that for $0 \leq i \leq w_1 - 1, 0 \leq j \leq w_2 - 1$, the values assumed by $\eta_{ij}, \eta_{i0}, \eta_{0j}$ lie in $\mathcal{Q}_w, \mathcal{Q}_{w_1}, \mathcal{Q}_{w_2}$ respectively. $\eta_{00} = 1_{\mathfrak{X}}$ and

$$
\eta_ {i j} (W) = \omega_ {i j} (W) \quad f o r \quad W \in \hat {\mathfrak {W}},
$$

$$
(1 - \omega_ {i 0} - \omega_ {0 j} + \omega_ {i j}) ^ {*} = 1 _ {\mathcal {X}} - \eta_ {i 0} - \eta_ {0 j} + \eta_ {i j}.
$$

Furthermore every irreducible character of $\mathfrak{X}$ distinct from all $\pm\eta_{ij}$ vanishes on $\hat{\mathfrak{W}}$.

Proof. It follows directly from Hypothesis 13.1 that $\hat{\mathfrak{W}}$ is a T. I. set in $\mathfrak{X}$. Define the generalized character $\alpha_{ij}$ of $\mathfrak{W}$ by

$$
\alpha_ {i j} = \left(\omega_ {0 0} - \omega_ {i 0}\right) \left(\omega_ {0 0} - \omega_ {0 j}\right).
$$

Clearly $\alpha_{ij}$ vanishes on $\mathfrak{W} - \hat{\mathfrak{W}}$. Thus

$$
\alpha_ {i j} ^ {*} (W) = \alpha_ {i j} (W) \quad \text { for } W \in \hat {\mathfrak {W}}  ,\tag{13.2}
$$

$$
\left(\alpha_ {i j} ^ {*}, \alpha_ {s t} ^ {*}\right) = 1 + \delta_ {i s} + \delta_ {j t} + \delta_ {i s} \delta_ {j t}
$$

for $1 \leq i, s \leq w_1 - 1, 1 \leq j, t \leq w_2 - 1$. Therefore $||\alpha_{ij}^*||^2 = 4$ and $(\alpha_{ij}^*, \alpha_{it}^*) = 2$ for $i, j, t \neq 0, j \neq t$. It follows directly from the definition of $\alpha_{ij}$ that the values of $\alpha_{ij}^*$ lie in $\mathcal{Q}_w$.

For any algebraic number field $\mathcal{F}$ and any generalized character $\alpha$ of a group let $\mathcal{F}(\alpha)$ denote the field generated by $\mathcal{F}$ and all the values assumed by $\alpha$. Since $\mathcal{Q}(\alpha_{ij}) = \mathcal{Q}(\alpha_{ij}^*)$ we see that $\mathcal{Q}(\alpha_{ij}^*) = \mathcal{Q}_v$ for some $v$ with $v|w$. If $i, j \neq 0$ then $v = v_1 v_2$, where $v_s | w_s$ and $v_s > 1$ for $s = 1, 2$. By (13.2)

$$
\alpha_ {i j} ^ {*} = 1 _ {\mathfrak {x}} \pm \theta_ {1} \pm \theta_ {2} \pm \theta_ {3},
$$

where $\Theta_{1},\Theta_{2},\Theta_{3}$ are distinct irreducible characters of $\mathfrak{X}$.

Suppose that $\mathcal{Q}(\theta_k) \not\subseteq \mathcal{Q}_{v_1}$ for $k = 1, 2, 3$. Let

$$
\mathcal {F} = \mathcal {Q} _ {v} (\theta_ {1}, \theta_ {2}, \theta_ {3}) = \mathcal {Q} (\theta_ {1}, \theta_ {2}, \theta_ {3}).
$$

Let $\mathfrak{G}$ be the Galois group of $\mathcal{F}$ over $\mathcal{Q}_{v_1}$. For $k = 1, 2, 3$ let $\mathfrak{G}_k$ be the subgroup of $\mathfrak{G}$ whose fixed field is $\mathcal{Q}_{v_1}(\theta_k)$.

Assume first that $\mathfrak{G} = \mathfrak{G}_1 \cup \mathfrak{G}_2 \cup \mathfrak{G}_3$. By (13.2) $\mathfrak{G}_s \cap \mathfrak{G}_t = 1$ for $1 \leq s < t \leq 3$. If $\mathfrak{G} = \mathfrak{G}_k$ for some $k$ then $\mathcal{Q}(\theta_k) \subseteq \mathcal{Q}_{v_1}$ contrary to assumption. Let $|\mathfrak{G}| = g$ and $|\mathfrak{G}_k| = g_k$ for $k = 1, 2, 3$. Then it may be assumed that $g > g_1 \geq g_2 \geq g_3$. Since $g = g_1 + g_2 + g_3 - 1 - 1 - 1 + 1$ we must have $g_1 = g/2$. Therefore

$$
\mathbf {1} = | \mathfrak {G} _ {1} \cap \mathfrak {G} _ {2} | \geq g _ {2} / 2, \quad \mathbf {1} = | \mathfrak {G} _ {1} \cap \mathfrak {G} _ {3} | \geq g _ {3} / 2.
$$

Hence

$$
g / 2 = g - g _ {1} = g _ {2} + g _ {3} - 2, \quad g _ {2}, g _ {3} \leq 2.
$$

Therefore $g \leq 4$. $\mathfrak{G}$ is not cyclic as it is the union of proper subgroups. Hence $\mathfrak{G}$ is the non cyclic group of order 4 and $|\mathfrak{G}_k| = 2$ for $k = 1, 2, 3$. As $v_2$ is odd this implies that $v_2 = 3$. For $k = 1, 2, 3$ let $\mathfrak{G}_k = \langle \sigma_k \rangle$, where the notation is chosen so that $\mathcal{Q}_v = \mathcal{Q}_{v_1}(\Theta_1)$. Therefore $\sigma_1(\alpha_{ij}^*) = \alpha_{ij}^*$. Hence $\sigma_1(\Theta_2) = \Theta_3$. Consequently $\mathcal{Q}_{v_1}(\Theta_2) = \mathcal{Q}_{v_1}(\Theta_3)$ as $\mathfrak{G}$ is abelian. This implies that $\sigma_2 = \sigma_3$ which is not the case. Thus $\mathfrak{G} \neq \mathfrak{G}_1 \cup \mathfrak{G}_2 \cup \mathfrak{G}_3$.

If $\sigma \in \mathfrak{G} - \mathfrak{G}_1 \cup \mathfrak{G}_2 \cup \mathfrak{G}_3$, then by (13.2) $(\alpha_{ij}^*, \sigma(\alpha_{ij}^*)) \geq 2$. Hence by choosing the notation suitably it may be assumed that $\sigma(\theta_2) = \theta_3$. If $(\alpha_{ij}^*, \theta_2) \neq (\alpha_{ij}^*, \sigma(\theta_2))$ then replacing $\sigma$ by $\sigma^{-1}$ and $\theta_2$ by $\theta_3$ if necessary we get that

$$
\alpha_ {i j} ^ {*} = 1 _ {\mathcal {X}} \pm \left\{\theta_ {1} + \theta_ {2} - \sigma (\theta_ {2}) \right\}.
$$

By (13.2) $\sigma(\theta_2) \neq \theta_1, \theta_2$. Hence also $\sigma(\theta_2) \neq \sigma^2(\theta_2)$. Therefore

$$
\begin{array}{r l} 2 \leq (\sigma (\alpha_ {i j} ^ {*}), \alpha_ {i j} ^ {*}) & = 1 - 1 + (\theta_ {1} + \theta_ {2}, \sigma (\theta_ {1}) - \sigma^ {2} (\theta_ {2})) \\ & = (\theta_ {1} + \theta_ {2}, \sigma (\theta_ {1})) - (\theta_ {1} + \theta_ {2}, \sigma^ {2} (\theta_ {2})) \\ & \leq (\theta_ {1} + \theta_ {2}, \sigma (\theta_ {1})) \leq 1 \end{array}
$$

since $\theta_{1},\theta_{2},\sigma (\theta_{1})$ and $\sigma^2 (\theta_2)$ are all characters. This contradiction establishes that $(\alpha_{ij}^{*},\theta_{2}) = (\alpha_{ij}^{*},\sigma (\theta_{2}))$ . Since $\alpha_{ij}^{*}(1) = 0$ we see that

$$
\alpha_ {i j} ^ {*} = 1 _ {\mathfrak {x}} \pm \left\{\theta_ {2} + \sigma (\theta_ {2}) - \theta_ {1} \right\}.\tag{13.3}
$$

Furthermore $\mathfrak{G}_2 = \mathfrak{G}_3$ and if $\gamma \in \mathfrak{G} - \mathfrak{G}_1 \cup \mathfrak{G}_2$ then $\theta_1 \neq \gamma(\theta_2)$. By definition $\theta_1 \neq \gamma(\theta_2)$ for $\gamma \in \mathfrak{G}_1 \cup \mathfrak{G}_2$. Therefore

$$
\theta_ {1} \neq \gamma (\theta_ {2}) \quad \text { for } \gamma \in \mathfrak {G}.
$$

Suppose that $\gamma(\theta_2) = \theta_2$ for some automorphism $\gamma$ of $\mathcal{F}$. Then $\gamma\sigma(\theta_2) = \sigma(\theta_2)$ and (13.3) implies that $(\alpha_{ij}^*, \gamma(\alpha_{ij}^*)) \geq 3$. Thus by (13.2) $\gamma(\alpha_{ij}^*) = \alpha_{ij}^*$. Consequently $\gamma(\theta_1) = \theta_1$ and so

$$
\mathcal {Q} _ {v} \subseteq \mathcal {F} = \mathcal {Q} (\theta_ {2}).\tag{13.4}
$$

If now $\gamma \in \mathfrak{G}^{\sharp},\gamma \neq \sigma ,\gamma \neq \sigma^{-1}$ , then (13.3) yields that

$$
2 \leq (\alpha_ {i j} ^ {*}, \gamma (\alpha_ {i j} ^ {*})) = 1 + (\theta_ {1}, \gamma (\theta_ {1})) .
$$
