Since $\mathfrak{D}_{12}$ centralizes both $O_{p_1'}(\mathfrak{R}_2)$ and $O_{p_1'}(\mathfrak{Q})$, it follows that $\mathfrak{L} = \langle \mathfrak{P}_1, O_{p_1'}(\mathfrak{R}_2), O_{p_1'}(\mathfrak{Q}) \rangle \subset \mathfrak{G}$. By Lemma 7.4, it follows that $\langle O_{p_1'}(\mathfrak{R}_2), O_{p_1'}(\mathfrak{Q}) \rangle \subseteq O_{p_1'}(\mathfrak{Q})$. By Theorem 17.1 and $C_{p_1, p_4}^*, O_{p_1'}(\mathfrak{R}_2)$ and $O_{p_1'}(\mathfrak{Q})$ are $S$-subgroups of $O_{p_1'}(\mathfrak{Q})$, so are conjugate in $\mathfrak{L}$, being of the same order. Since $O_{p_1'}(\mathfrak{Q}) \neq \langle 1 \rangle$, $C_\sigma$ follows immediately.

## 22. Linking Theorems

One of the purposes of this section is to clarify the relationship between  $\pi_{3}$  and  $\pi_{4}$ .

Hypothesis 22.1.

(i)  $p \in \pi_{3}, q \in \pi(\mathfrak{G})$ .

(ii) A $S_{p}$-subgroup $\mathfrak{P}$ of $\mathfrak{G}$ does not centralize every element of $\mathcal{N}(\mathfrak{P}; q)$.

THEOREM 22.1. Under Hypothesis 22.1, if $\mathfrak{D}_1$ is a maximal element of $\mathsf{V}(\mathfrak{P};q)$ and $Q$ is an element of $\mathfrak{D}_1$ of order $q$, then $C_{\mathfrak{D}_1}(Q)$ contains an elementary subgroup of order $q^3$. In particular, $q\in \pi_3\cup \pi_4$.

Proof. Choose $\mathfrak{C}$ char $\mathfrak{Q}_1$ in accordance with Lemma 8.2, and set $\mathfrak{C}_1 = \Omega_1(\mathfrak{C})$. From 3.6 and Lemma 8.2, it follows that $\mathfrak{P}$ does not centralize $\mathfrak{C}_1$. Since $cl(\mathfrak{C}) \leq 2$, $\mathfrak{C}_1$ is of exponent $q$.

Since $N(\mathfrak{C}_1) \supseteq N(\mathfrak{D}_1)$, Lemma 17.3 implies that $O^p(N(\mathfrak{C}_1)) = N(\mathfrak{C}_1)$. Since $N(\mathfrak{C}_1)$ has odd order, this in turn implies that $\mathfrak{C}_1$ is not generated by two elements. Consider the chain $\mathcal{C} \colon \mathfrak{C}_1 \supseteq \gamma \mathfrak{C}_1 \mathfrak{D}_1 \supseteq \gamma^2 \mathfrak{C}_1 \mathfrak{D}_1^2 \supseteq \cdots$. Since $\mathfrak{P}$ does not centralize $\mathfrak{C}_1$, $\mathfrak{P}$ does not stabilize $\mathcal{C}$, so we can find an integer $n$ and subgroups $\mathfrak{A}_1, \mathfrak{A}_2$ such that $\gamma^{n+1} \mathfrak{C}_1 \mathfrak{D}_1^{n+1} \subseteq \mathfrak{A}_1 \subseteq \mathfrak{A}_2 \subseteq \gamma^n \mathfrak{C}_1 \mathfrak{D}_1^n$ and such that $\mathfrak{B} = \mathfrak{A}_2 / \mathfrak{A}_1$ is a chief factor of $N(\mathfrak{D}_1)$ and with the additional property that $\mathfrak{P}$ does not centralize $\mathfrak{B}$. Since $N(\mathfrak{D}_1) = O^p(N(\mathfrak{D}_1))$, we also have $\bar{\mathfrak{N}} = O^p(\bar{\mathfrak{N}})$, where $\bar{\mathfrak{N}} = (N(\mathfrak{B}) \cap N(\mathfrak{D}_1)) / (C(\mathfrak{B}) \cap N(\mathfrak{D}_1))$. Since $|N(\mathfrak{D}_1)|$ is odd it follows that $|\mathfrak{B}| \geq q^3$. Since $\gamma \mathfrak{A}_2 \mathfrak{D}_1 \subseteq \mathfrak{A}_1$, it follows that $|C_{\mathfrak{A}_2}(Q)| \geq q^3$. If $C_{\mathfrak{D}_1}(Q)$ did not contain an elementary subgroup of order $q^3$, then we would necessarily have $Q \in \mathfrak{A}_2$ since $\mathfrak{A}_2$ is of exponent $q$. Since $|C_{\mathfrak{A}_2}(Q)| \geq q^3$, the only possibility is that $C_{\mathfrak{A}_2}(Q)$ is the non abelian group of order $q^3$ and exponent $q$. But in this case $Q \in C_{\mathfrak{A}_2}(Q)' \subseteq Z(\mathfrak{C}_1)$, and $C_{\mathfrak{D}_1}(Q)$ contains an elementary subgroup of order $q^3$ since $\mathfrak{D}_1$ does, by Lemma 8.13, Lemma 8.1, and the equation $N(\mathfrak{D}_1) = O^p(N(\mathfrak{D}_1))$.

Hypothesis 22.2.

(i) $\mathfrak{P}$ is a $S_{p}$-subgroup of $\mathfrak{G}$ and $p \in \pi_{3}$.

(ii) $q, r \in \pi_{3} \cup \pi_{4}$; $\mathfrak{P}$ does not centralize every element of $\mathsf{V}(\mathfrak{P}; q)$ and $\mathfrak{P}$ does not centralize every element of $\mathsf{V}(\mathfrak{P}; r)$.

## THEOREM 22.2. Under Hypothesis 22.2, $q \sim r$.

The proof of this theorem is by contradiction. The following lemmas assume that  $q \not\sim r$ .

Since Hypothesis 22.2 is symmetric in q and r we can assume that q > r, thereby destroying the symmetry.

Let $\mathfrak{A} \in \mathcal{SCN}_{3}(\mathfrak{P})$. Let $\mathfrak{D}_1, \mathfrak{R}_1$ be maximal elements in $\mathfrak{U}(\mathfrak{P}; q)$, $\mathfrak{U}(\mathfrak{P}; r)$ respectively.

LEMMA 22.1. If $\mathfrak{S}$ is an $\mathfrak{A}$-invariant $q$, $r$-subgroup of $\mathfrak{G}$, and if a $S_q$-subgroup $\mathfrak{G}_q$ of $\mathfrak{G}$ is non cyclic, then $\mathfrak{G}_q \triangleleft \mathfrak{G}$.

Proof. Let $\mathfrak{H}_r$ be a $S_r$-subgroup of $\mathfrak{H}$ normalized by $\mathfrak{A}$. Since $q \nmid r$, either $\mathcal{SCN}_3(\mathfrak{H}_r)$ or $\mathcal{SCN}_3(\mathfrak{H}_q)$ is empty. If $\mathcal{SCN}_3(\mathfrak{H}_r)$ is empty, application of Lemma 8.5 to $\mathfrak{H}$ yields this lemma.

Suppose $\mathcal{S}\mathcal{C}\mathcal{N}_{s}(\mathfrak{H}_{r})$ is non empty. Then $\mathcal{S}\mathcal{C}\mathcal{N}_{s}(\mathfrak{H}_{q})$ is empty, so $\mathfrak{H}$ has $q$-length one. Thus, it suffices to show that $\mathfrak{H}_{q}$ centralizes $O_{r}(\mathfrak{H})$. We suppose without loss of generality that $\mathfrak{A}$ normalizes $\mathfrak{H}_{q}$.

Then by Corollary 17.2 $\mathfrak{H}_q$ is contained in a conjugate of $\mathfrak{D}_1$, so $C(H)$ possesses an elementary subgroup of order $q^3$ for $H$ in $\mathfrak{H}_q$, $H$ of order $q$, by Theorem 22.1. We will show that $\Omega_1(\mathfrak{H}_q)$ centralizes $O_r(\mathfrak{H})$. Since $\mathfrak{H}_q$ is assumed non cyclic, $\Omega_1(\mathfrak{H}_q)$ is generated by its subgroups $\mathfrak{E}$ which are elementary of order $q^3$, so it suffices to show that each such $\mathfrak{E}$ centralizes $O_r(\mathfrak{H})$. If $\mathfrak{E}$ does not centralize $O_r(\mathfrak{H})$, then $\mathfrak{E}$ does not centralize $O_r(\mathfrak{H}) \cap C(E)$ for suitable $E$ in $\mathfrak{E}^*$. By Lemma 8.4, $\mathcal{S}\mathcal{C}\mathcal{N}_3(O_r(\mathfrak{H}) \cap C(E))$ is non empty for such an $E$, so $q \not\sim r$ is violated in $C(E)$.

Since $\Omega_{1}(\mathfrak{H}_{q})$ centralizes $O_{r}(\mathfrak{H})$, it follows that $\mathcal{S}\mathcal{E}\mathcal{N}_{3}(O_{r}(\mathfrak{H}))$ is empty, since $q \not\sim r$. Hence, $\mathfrak{H}_{q}$ centralizes $O_{r}(\mathfrak{H})$ by Lemma 8.4, as required.

We define $\mathcal{K}$ as the set of $q$, $r$-subgroups of $\mathcal{M}(\mathfrak{A})$ which have the additional property that no $S_q$- or $S_r$-subgroup is centralized by $\mathfrak{A}$,

LEMMA 22.2. $\mathcal{X}$ is non empty.

Proof. Suppose that $\gamma\mathfrak{D}_{1}\mathfrak{A}=\langle1\rangle$. If we also had $\gamma\mathfrak{R}_{1}\mathfrak{A}=\langle1\rangle$, then $q\not\sim r$ would be violated in $C(\mathfrak{A})$. Hence, $\gamma\mathfrak{R}_{1}\mathfrak{A}\neq\langle1\rangle$, and we can find $\mathfrak{R}_{2}\subseteq\mathfrak{R}_{1},\;\mathfrak{R}_{2}\neq\langle1\rangle$, such that $\mathfrak{R}_{2}=\gamma\mathfrak{R}_{2}\mathfrak{A}$ and such that $\mathfrak{A}_{1}=C_{\mathfrak{A}}(\mathfrak{R}_{2})\neq\langle1\rangle$. Consider $C(\mathfrak{A}_{1})\supseteq\langle\mathfrak{A},\;\mathfrak{D}_{1},\;\mathfrak{R}_{2}\rangle=\mathfrak{L}$. By Lemma 17.6, $\mathfrak{A}\supseteq O_{p^{\prime},p}(\mathfrak{L})$ and it follows readily that $\mathfrak{L}$ possesses a normal complement $\mathfrak{S}_{0}$ to $\mathfrak{A}$. We can then find $C$ in $C_{\mathfrak{L}}(\mathfrak{A})$ such that $\mathfrak{H}=\langle\mathfrak{D}_{1},\;\mathfrak{R}_{2}^{o}\rangle$ is a $q,r$-group. By Lemma 22.1 and the fact that $\mathfrak{D}_{1}$ is a maximal element of $U(\mathfrak{A};q)$, we have $\mathfrak{D}_{1}\triangleleft\mathfrak{H}$. But now $\mathfrak{R}_{2}^{o}\subseteq N(\mathfrak{D}_{1})=O^{p}(N(\mathfrak{D}_{1}))$. Since $q\not\sim r$, if $\mathfrak{S}_{r}$ is a $S_{r}$-subgroup of $N(\mathfrak{D}_{1})$, then $\mathcal{SCN}_{3}(\mathfrak{S}_{r})$ is empty. By Lemma 8.13 $N(\mathfrak{D}_{1})'$ centralizes every chief $r$-factor of $N(\mathfrak{D}_{1})$. It follows that $\mathfrak{A}$ centralizes $\mathfrak{R}_{2}^{o}$, contrary to construction, so we can assume that $\gamma\mathfrak{D}_{1}\mathfrak{A}\neq\langle1\rangle$.

Suppose $\gamma\mathfrak{R}_{1}\mathfrak{A}=\langle1\rangle$. Since $\mathfrak{A}$ possesses an elementary subgroup of order $p^{3}$, we can find $A$ in $\mathfrak{A}$ such that $C_{\mathfrak{Q}_{1}}(A)$ is non cyclic. Consider $C(A)\supseteq\langle\mathfrak{A},C_{\mathfrak{Q}_{1}}(A),\mathfrak{R}_{1}\rangle$. By Lemma 17.6 we can assume that $\mathfrak{Q}=\langle C_{\mathfrak{Q}_{1}}(A),\mathfrak{R}_{1}\rangle$ is a $q,r$-group. Then Lemma 22.1 implies that $\mathfrak{S}_{q}\triangleleft\mathfrak{H},\mathfrak{S}_{q}$ being a $S_{q}$-subgroup of $\mathfrak{Q}$. Enlarge $\mathfrak{Q}$ to $\mathfrak{K}$, a maximal $\mathfrak{A}$-invariant $q,r$-subgroup with Sylow system $\mathfrak{R}_{q},\mathfrak{R}_{1}$. Lemma 17.6, Lemma 22.1 and maximality of $\mathfrak{A}$ imply that $\mathfrak{R}_{q}$ is a maximal element of $\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{A};q)$, contrary to $q\not\sim r$.

We can now assume that $\gamma \mathfrak{Q}_1\mathfrak{A} \neq \langle 1\rangle$ and $\gamma \mathfrak{R}_1\mathfrak{A} \neq \langle 1\rangle$.

Let $\mathfrak{Q}_2$ be an $\mathfrak{A}$-invariant subgroup of $\mathfrak{Q}_1$ of minimal order subject to $\gamma \mathfrak{Q}_2\mathfrak{A} \neq \langle 1\rangle$. Let $\mathfrak{R}_2$ be an $\mathfrak{A}$-invariant subgroup of $\mathfrak{R}_1$ of minimal order subject to $\gamma \mathfrak{R}_2\mathfrak{A} \neq \langle 1\rangle$. Let $\mathfrak{A}_1 = \ker (\mathfrak{A} \to \operatorname{Aut} \mathfrak{Q}_2)$, $\mathfrak{A}_2 = \ker (\mathfrak{A} \to \operatorname{Aut} \mathfrak{R}_2)$. Since $\mathfrak{A}$ acts irreducibly on $\mathfrak{Q}_2 / D(\mathfrak{Q}_2)$ and on $\mathfrak{R}_2 / D(\mathfrak{R}_2)$, it follows that $\mathfrak{A} / \mathfrak{A}_i$ is cyclic, $i = 1, 2$. Since $\mathfrak{A} \in \mathcal{S}\mathcal{C}\mathcal{N}_3(\mathfrak{P})$, $\mathfrak{A}_1 \cap \mathfrak{A}_2 =$

$\mathfrak{A}_3 \neq \langle 1 \rangle$. An $\mathfrak{A}$-invariant $S_{q,r}$-subgroup of $\langle \mathfrak{A}, \mathfrak{D}_2, \mathfrak{R}_2 \rangle \subseteq C(\mathfrak{A}_3)$ satisfies the conditions defining $\mathcal{K}$, by Lemma 17.6 and $D_{p,q,r}$ in $\langle \mathfrak{A}, \mathfrak{D}_2, \mathfrak{R}_2 \rangle$.

Let $\mathfrak{R}$ be a maximal element of $\mathcal{K}$, with Sylow system $\mathfrak{R}_q$, $\mathfrak{R}_{rr}$, chosen so that $\mathfrak{A}$ normalizes both $\mathfrak{R}_q$ and $\mathfrak{R}_r$, $\mathfrak{R}_q$ being a $S_q$-subgroup of $\mathfrak{R}$.

LEMMA 22.3. $\mathfrak{R}_q$ is cyclic and $O_q(\mathfrak{R}) = \langle 1\rangle$.

Proof. Suppose $\mathfrak{R}_q$ is non cyclic. Then Lemma 22.1 yields $\mathfrak{R}_q \triangleleft \mathfrak{R}$. The maximal nature of $\mathfrak{R}$, together with Lemma 17.6, imply that $\mathfrak{R}_q$ is a maximal element of $\mathcal{N}(\mathfrak{A}; q)$, so is conjugate to $\mathfrak{D}_1$.

By Lemma 17.3, $N(\mathfrak{R}_q) = \mathfrak{N} = O^p(\mathfrak{N})$. Since $q \not\sim r$, $N(\mathfrak{R}_q)$ does not possess an elementary subgroup of order $r^3$. Now $\mathfrak{N} = O^p(\mathfrak{N})$ and Lemma 8.13 imply that $\gamma \mathfrak{R}_r\mathfrak{A} = \langle 1\rangle$, contrary to construction. Hence, $\mathfrak{R}_q$ is cyclic.

If $O_{q}(\mathfrak{R}) \neq \langle 1 \rangle$, then $\Omega_{1}(O_{q}(\mathfrak{R})) = \Omega_{1}(\mathfrak{R}_{q}) \triangleleft \mathfrak{R}$. The maximal nature of $\mathfrak{R}$ now conflicts with Lemma 17.6 and Theorem 22.1 proving this lemma.

We choose $C$ in $C(\mathfrak{A})$ so that $\mathfrak{R}_{r}^{\sigma} \subseteq \mathfrak{R}_{1}$; since $\mathfrak{R}^{\sigma}$ is also a maximal element of $\mathcal{K}$, we assume without loss of generality that $\mathfrak{R}_{r} \subseteq \mathfrak{R}_{1}$.

LEMMA 22.4.

(i) $\mathfrak{R}_r$ is non abelian.

(ii) No non identity weakly closed subgroup of $\mathfrak{R}_r$ is contained in $O_r(\mathfrak{R})$.

(iii) $O_{r}(\mathfrak{R})$ contains an element of $\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{R})$, $\mathfrak{R}$ being any $S_{r}$-subgroup of $\mathfrak{G}$ containing a $S_{r}$-subgroup $\mathfrak{R}^{*}$ of $N(\mathfrak{R}_{1})$.

Proof. We first prove (ii). Suppose $\mathfrak{T} \neq \langle 1 \rangle$, $\mathfrak{T}$ is weakly closed in $\mathfrak{R}_r$, and $\mathfrak{T} \subseteq O_r(\mathfrak{R})$. Then $\mathfrak{T} \triangleleft \mathfrak{R}\mathfrak{A}$, so the maximal nature of $\mathfrak{R}$ together with Lemma 17.6 imply that $\mathfrak{R}_r = \mathfrak{R}_1$.

$$
N (\mathfrak {R} _ {1}) = O ^ {p} (N (\mathfrak {R} _ {1}))
$$

$$
N (\mathfrak {T}) = O ^ {p} (N (\mathfrak {T}))
$$

$$
q \not \sim r,
$$

$$
\gamma \mathfrak {A} \mathfrak {R} _ {q} = \langle 1 \rangle
$$

If $\mathfrak{R}_r$ were abelian, then $O_q(\mathfrak{R}) = \langle 1\rangle$ and Lemma 1.2.3 of [21] imply that $\mathfrak{R}_r = O_r(\mathfrak{R})$, in violation of (ii). This proves (i).

Suppose $r \in \pi_3$. In this case, $C_{p,r}^*$ implies $\mathfrak{R}^* = \mathfrak{R}$, and since $\mathfrak{R}_1' \neq \langle 1 \rangle$, it is clear that $\mathfrak{R}_1$ contains an element $\mathfrak{U}$ of $\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{R})$. Since $\mathfrak{R}_r = N(O_r(\mathfrak{R})) \cap \mathfrak{R}_1$, it follows that $\mathfrak{U} \cap Z(\mathfrak{R}) \subseteq \mathfrak{R}_r$ and so by (B), $\mathfrak{U} \cap Z(\mathfrak{R}) \subseteq O_r(\mathfrak{R})$. It now follows that $\mathfrak{U} \subseteq \mathfrak{R}_r$, and so $\mathfrak{U} \subseteq O_r(\mathfrak{R})$, again by (B). Next, suppose that $r \in \pi_4$. In this case, since $\mathfrak{R}_1' \neq \langle 1 \rangle$, $\mathfrak{R}^*$ contains an element $\mathfrak{B}$ of $\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{R})$, $\mathfrak{R}^*$ being a $S_r$-subgroup of $N(\mathfrak{R}_1')$. Since $\mathfrak{B}$ centralizes $O_p(\mathfrak{P}\mathfrak{R}^*)$, by Lemma 19.1, we have $\mathfrak{B} \subseteq \mathfrak{R}_1$. Since $\mathfrak{B} \subseteq \mathfrak{R}_1$, $\mathfrak{B} \cap Z(\mathfrak{R}) \subseteq \mathfrak{R}_r$ and so by (B), $\mathfrak{B} \cap Z(\mathfrak{R}) \subseteq O_r(\mathfrak{R})$. It follows that $\mathfrak{B} \subseteq O_r(\mathfrak{R})$. This proves (iii).

To prove Theorem 22.2 we will now show that $\mathfrak{R}_q$ centralizes $Z(O_r(\mathfrak{R})) = 3$. Suppose by way of contradiction that this is not the case. We can choose $\mathfrak{C} \in \mathcal{U}(r)$ such that $\mathfrak{C} \subseteq \mathfrak{R}_r$ but $\mathfrak{C} \not\subseteq O_r(\mathfrak{R})$. Since $\mathfrak{R}_q$ is cyclic $\mathfrak{C}_1 = \mathfrak{C} \cap O_r(\mathfrak{R})$ is of order $r$. From (B), we then have $\gamma^{r-1}3\mathfrak{C}^{r-1} \neq \langle 1\rangle$.

If $r \geq 5$, we apply Lemma 16.2 and conclude that $\gamma^4\mathfrak{Z}\mathfrak{C}^4 = \langle 1 \rangle$, contrary to the above statement. Hence $r = 3$, and by Lemma 16.3 we have $\gamma^2\mathfrak{Z}\mathfrak{C}^2 = \mathfrak{C}_1$; in particular, $\mathfrak{C}_1 \subseteq 3$. Now apply Lemma 16.3 again, this time with $O_3(\mathfrak{R})$ in the role of $\mathfrak{F}$, and conclude that $\gamma^2O_3(\mathfrak{R})\mathfrak{C}^2 = \mathfrak{C}_1$.

Let $\mathfrak{T} = \gamma O_3(\mathfrak{R})\mathfrak{R}_q$. By Lemma 8.11, we have $\mathfrak{T} = \gamma \mathfrak{T}\mathfrak{R}_q$, and so $\mathfrak{C}_1 \subseteq \Omega_1(Z(\mathfrak{T}))$. Hence by (B), $\mathfrak{R}_q$ acts trivially on $\mathfrak{T}/\Omega_1(Z(\mathfrak{T}))$, and this implies that $\mathfrak{T} = \Omega_1(Z(\mathfrak{T}))$, so that $\mathfrak{T}$ is elementary.

The equality $\gamma^{2}\mathfrak{T}\mathfrak{C}^{2}=\mathfrak{C}_{1}$ and (B) imply that an element of $\mathfrak{C}-\mathfrak{C}_{1}$ induces an automorphism of $\mathfrak{T}$ with matrix $J_{3}$. Since $|\mathfrak{R}_{q}|$ divides $3^{3}-1$, we have $|\mathfrak{R}_{q}|=13$.

By definition of $\mathcal{K}$ we have $p / 12 = |\operatorname{Aut} \mathfrak{R}_{13}|$. Since $p \neq r = 3$, we have a contradiction, completing the proof that $\mathfrak{R}_q$ centralizes 3 in all cases.

Now $Z(\mathfrak{R}_1)$ centralizes $O_r(\mathfrak{R})$, so by maximality of $\mathfrak{R}$, we have $Z(\mathfrak{R}_1) \subseteq \mathfrak{R}_r$ and (B) implies that $Z(\mathfrak{R}_1) \subseteq Z(O_r(\mathfrak{R}))$. Hence, $\mathfrak{R} \subseteq N(Z(\mathfrak{R}_1)) = \mathfrak{R}_1$. But $\mathfrak{R}_1 = O^p(\mathfrak{R}_1)$ and since $q \neq r$, $\mathfrak{R}_1$ does not possess an elementary subgroup of order $q^3$. Lemma 8.13 implies that $\gamma \mathfrak{R}_q\mathfrak{A} = \langle 1\rangle$, contrary to construction, completing the proof of Theorem 22.2.

For $p$ in $\pi_3 \cup \pi_4$, let $\mathcal{W}(p)$ be the set of all subgroups $\mathfrak{B}$ of $\mathfrak{G}$ of type $(p, p)$ such that every element $W$ of $\mathfrak{B}$ centralizes an element $\mathfrak{B}$ of $\mathcal{U}(p)$. We allow $\mathfrak{B}$ to depend on $W$.

Hypothesis 22.3.

(i)  $p \in \pi_{3}, q \in \pi(\mathfrak{G})$ .

(ii)  $p \neq q.$

THEOREM 22.3. Under Hypothesis 22.3, if $\mathfrak{R}$ is a $p$, $q$-subgroup of $\mathfrak{G}$ and if $\mathfrak{R}$ contains an element of $\mathcal{W}(p)$, then a $S_p$-subgroup of $\mathfrak{R}$ is normal in $\mathfrak{R}$.

Proof. Let $\mathcal{K}$ be the set of subgroups of $\mathfrak{G}$ satisfying the hypotheses but not the conclusion of this theorem and let $\mathcal{K}_1$ be the subset of all $\mathfrak{R}$ in $\mathcal{K}$ which contain at least one element of $\mathcal{U}(p)$.

We first show that $\mathcal{K}_1$ is empty. Suppose false and $\mathfrak{R}$ in $\mathcal{K}_1$ is chosen to maximize $|\mathfrak{R}|_p$. Let $\mathfrak{R}_p$ be a $S_p$-subgroup of $\mathfrak{R}$, and let $\mathfrak{B} = V(ccl_{\mathfrak{B}}(\mathfrak{B});\mathfrak{R}_p)$ where $\mathfrak{B} \in \mathcal{U}(p)$ and $\mathfrak{B} \subseteq \mathfrak{R}_p$.

Since $p \not\sim q$, Hypothesis 22.1 does not hold. Hence, Hypothesis

19.1 holds. Apply Lemma 19.1 and conclude that $\mathfrak{V}$ centralizes $O_{q}(\mathfrak{R})$. Suppose $\mathfrak{R}_{p}$ is a $S_{p}$-subgroup of $\mathfrak{G}$. Then $\mathfrak{R}_{p}$ centralizes $O_{q}(\mathfrak{R})$. By Lemma 17.5 and Hypothesis 22.3 (i), if $\mathfrak{A} \in \mathcal{SCN}_{3}(\mathfrak{R}_{p})$, and $\mathfrak{V}_{1} = V(ccl_{\mathfrak{B}}(\mathfrak{A}); \mathfrak{R}_{p})$, then $\mathfrak{V}_{1} \subseteq O_{q,p}(\mathfrak{R})$. Since $\mathfrak{R}_{p}$ centralizes $O_{q}(\mathfrak{R})$, it follows that $\mathfrak{V}_{1} \subseteq O_{p}(\mathfrak{R})$, and so $\mathfrak{V}_{1} \triangleleft \mathfrak{R}$. By Lemma 17.2, $N(Z(\mathfrak{V}_{1})) = O^{p}(N(Z(\mathfrak{V}_{1})))$. Since $p \nrightarrow q$, $N(Z(\mathfrak{V}_{1}))$ does not possess an elementary subgroup of order $q^{3}$, so Lemma 8.13 implies that $\mathfrak{R}_{p} \triangleleft \mathfrak{R}$, contrary to the definition of $\mathscr{K}_{1}$. Hence, in showing that $\mathscr{K}_{1}$ is empty, we can suppose that $\mathfrak{R}_{p}$ is not a $S_{p}$-subgroup of $\mathfrak{G}$.

Since $\mathfrak{V}$ centralizes $O_q(\mathfrak{R})$, we have $\mathfrak{R}_p \cdot O_q(\mathfrak{R}) \subseteq N(\mathfrak{V})$. Since $\mathfrak{V}$ is weakly closed in $\mathfrak{R}_p$ and $\mathfrak{R}_p$ is not a $S_p$-subgroup of $\mathfrak{G}$, $\mathfrak{R}_p$ is not a $S_p$-subgroup of $N(\mathfrak{V})$. Maximality of $|\mathfrak{R}|_p$ implies that $\mathfrak{R}_p \triangleleft \mathfrak{R}_p \cdot O_q(\mathfrak{R})$, and so $O_p(\mathfrak{R})$ is a $S_p$-subgroup of $O_q(p(\mathfrak{R})$.

Let $\mathfrak{P}$ be a $S_{p}$-subgroup of $\mathfrak{G}$ containing $\mathfrak{R}_{p}$, and let $\mathfrak{A} \in \mathcal{SCN}_{3}(\mathfrak{P})$. Since $O_{p}(\mathfrak{R})$ is a $S_{p}$-subgroup of $O_{q,p}(\mathfrak{R})$, it follows from (B) that $\mathfrak{A} \cap \mathfrak{R}_{p} = \mathfrak{A} \cap O_{p}(\mathfrak{R})$. By maximality of $|\mathfrak{R}|_{p}$, $\mathfrak{R}_{p}$ is a $S_{p}$-subgroup of $N(O_{p}(\mathfrak{R}))$ and it follows readily that $\mathfrak{A} \subseteq O_{p}(\mathfrak{R})$. But in this case, $\mathfrak{V}_{2} = V(ccl_{\mathfrak{G}}(\mathfrak{A}); \mathfrak{R}_{p}) \triangleleft \mathfrak{R}$, by Lemma 17.5. Since $\mathfrak{R}_{p}$ is not a $S_{p}$-subgroup of $\mathfrak{G}$, it is not a $S_{p}$-subgroup of $N(\mathfrak{V}_{2})$, and the maximality of $\mathfrak{R}_{p}$ is violated in a $S_{p,q}$-subgroup of $N(\mathfrak{V}_{2})$. This contradiction shows that $\mathcal{K}_{1}$ is empty.

Now let $\mathfrak{K}$ be in $\mathcal{K}$ with $|\mathfrak{K}|_p$ maximal. Let $\mathfrak{W} \subseteq \mathfrak{K}_p$, $\mathfrak{W} \in \mathcal{W}(p)$. If $\gamma \mathfrak{W} O_q(\mathfrak{K}) \neq \langle 1 \rangle$, then $\mathfrak{W}$ does not centralize $C(W) \cap O_q(\mathfrak{K})$ for suitable $W$ in $\mathfrak{W}^*$. But in this case a $S_{p,q}$-subgroup of $C(W)$ contains an element of $\mathcal{U}(p)$ and also contains non normal $S_p$-subgroups, and $\mathcal{K}_1$ is non empty. Since this is not the case, $\mathfrak{W}$ centralizes $O_q(\mathfrak{K})$, and so $\mathfrak{W}_1 = V(ccl_{\mathfrak{G}}(\mathfrak{W}); \mathfrak{K}_p)$ centralizes $O_q(\mathfrak{K})$, $\mathfrak{W}$ being an arbitrary element of $\mathcal{W}(p)$ contained in $\mathfrak{K}_p$. Since $\mathfrak{K}_p$ is not a $S_p$-subgroup of $\mathfrak{G}$, it is not a $S_p$-subgroup of $N(\mathfrak{W}_1)$, so maximality of $|\mathfrak{K}|_p$ implies that $\mathfrak{K}_p$ centralizes $O_q(\mathfrak{K})$. Hence, $O_p(\mathfrak{K})$ is a $S_p$-subgroup of $O_{q,p}(\mathfrak{K})$. Since $\mathfrak{K}_p$ is a $S_p$-subgroup of $N(O_p(\mathfrak{K}))$ in this case, $Z(\mathfrak{P}) \subseteq O_p(\mathfrak{K})$ for every $S_p$-subgroup $\mathfrak{P}$ of $\mathfrak{G}$ which contains $\mathfrak{K}_p$. It follows that $\mathfrak{K}_p$ contains an element of $\mathcal{U}(p)$. This contradiction completes the proof of this theorem.

If $p \in \pi_3 \cup \pi_4$, we define $\pi(p)$ to be the set of primes $q$ such that $p \sim q$, and we set $\pi_3(p) = \pi(p) \cap \pi_3$.

THEOREM 22.4. If $p, q \in \pi_3$ and $p \sim q$, then $\pi_3(p) = \pi_3(q)$.

Proof. We only need to show that if $r \in \pi_3$ and $p \sim r$, then $r \sim q$. Apply Theorem 21.1, let $\mathfrak{R}$ be a $S_{p,q}$-subgroup of $\mathfrak{G}$ with Sylow system $\mathfrak{P}, \mathfrak{D}$, and let $\mathfrak{L}$ be a $S_p$-subgroup of $\mathfrak{G}$ with Sylow system $\mathfrak{P}, \mathfrak{R}$.

If Hypothesis 22.2 is satisfied, Theorem 22.2 applies and yields this theorem. Hence, we suppose without loss of generality that $\mathfrak{P}$ centralizes $O_{q}(\mathfrak{R})$.

Let $\mathfrak{A} \in \mathcal{SCN}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\mathfrak{P})$, $\mathfrak{B} = V(ccl_{\mathfrak{G}}(\mathfrak{A}); \mathfrak{P})$. Apply Lemma 17.5 and conclude that $\mathfrak{B} \triangleleft \mathfrak{R}$.

If $\mathfrak{P}$ also centralizes $O_r(\mathfrak{L})$, then we also have $\mathfrak{V} \triangleleft \mathfrak{L}$, and $q \sim r$ follows from consideration of $N(\mathfrak{V})$. We can suppose that $\mathfrak{P}$ does not centralize $O_r(\mathfrak{L})$.

Suppose we are able to show that $N(O_r(\mathfrak{L}))$ contains a $S_q$-subgroup of $C(\mathfrak{P})$. Apply Lemma 17.3 and conclude that $N(Z(\mathfrak{V})) = \mathfrak{N}_1 = O_{p'}(\mathfrak{N}_1) \cdot \mathfrak{N}_1 \cap \mathfrak{N}$, where $\mathfrak{N} = N(O_r(\mathfrak{L}))$. Let $\mathfrak{D}_1$ be a $S_q$-subgroup of $C(\mathfrak{P})$ which is contained in $\mathfrak{N}$. Since $\mathfrak{P}$ centralizes $O_q(\mathfrak{R})$, it follows that $\mathfrak{D}_1$ is a $S_q$-subgroup of $O_{p'}(\mathfrak{N}_1)$. Let $\mathfrak{N}_1^*$ be a $S_{q'}$-subgroup of $O_{p'}(\mathfrak{N}_1)$, so that $O_{p'}(\mathfrak{N}_1) = \mathfrak{N}_1^*\mathfrak{D}_1$. Hence,

$$
\mathfrak {N} _ {1} = \boldsymbol {O} _ {p ^ {\prime}} (\mathfrak {N} _ {1}) \cdot \mathfrak {N} _ {1} \cap \mathfrak {N} = \mathfrak {N} _ {1} ^ {*} \mathfrak {Q} _ {1} \cdot \mathfrak {N} _ {1} \cap \mathfrak {N} = \mathfrak {N} _ {1} ^ {*} \cdot \mathfrak {N} _ {1} \cap \mathfrak {N},
$$

since $\mathfrak{Q}_1 \subseteq \mathfrak{N}_1 \cap \mathfrak{N}$. Since $\mathfrak{N}_1$ contains a $S_q$-subgroup of $\mathfrak{G}$, so does $\mathfrak{N}_1 \cap \mathfrak{N}$. But $\mathfrak{N}$ contains a $S_r$-subgroup of $\mathfrak{G}$ as well, and so $q \sim r$.

Thus, in proving this theorem, it suffices to show that $N(O_r(\mathfrak{B}))$ contains a $S_q$-subgroup of $C(\mathfrak{P})$.

We wish to show first that some element $A$ of $\mathfrak{A}^*$ centralizes a subgroup $\mathfrak{W}$ of $\mathscr{W}(r)$ with $\mathfrak{W} \subseteq O_r(\mathfrak{V})$. If $D(O_r(\mathfrak{V})) = \mathfrak{D}$ is non cyclic, then every subgroup of $\mathfrak{D}$ of type $(r, r)$ is in $\mathscr{W}(r)$ and since $\mathfrak{A}$ possesses an elementary subgroup of order $p^3$, an element $A$ is available. Suppose then that $\mathfrak{D}$ is cyclic. If $\mathfrak{D} = \langle 1 \rangle$, then of course $\mathfrak{P}$ centralizes $\mathfrak{D}$. If $\mathfrak{D} \neq \langle 1 \rangle$, then $N(\mathfrak{D}) = O^p(N(\mathfrak{D}))$ and once again $\mathfrak{P}$ centralizes $\mathfrak{D}$. It now follows that $\mathfrak{A}^*$ contains an element $A$ whose fixed-point set on $\Omega_1(O_r(\mathfrak{V})) / \Omega_1(\mathfrak{D})$ is non cyclic, and this implies that $C(\mathfrak{A}) \cap O_r(\mathfrak{V})$ contains an element of $\mathscr{W}(r)$.

For such an element $A$, let $\mathfrak{H}$ be a $S_{q,r}$-subgroup of $O_{p'}(C(A))$ which is $\mathfrak{A}$-invariant and contains $O_q(\mathfrak{R})$. Then Lemma 17.5 implies that $\mathfrak{H}$ contains an element of $\mathscr{W}(r)$. Apply Theorem 22.3 and conclude that $\mathfrak{H}_r \triangleleft \mathfrak{H}, \mathfrak{H}_r$ being a $S_r$-subgroup of $\mathfrak{H}$. If $\mathfrak{H}^*$ is a maximal element of $\mathfrak{U}(\mathfrak{A}; q, r)$ containing $\mathfrak{H}$, then Theorem 22.3 implies that $\mathfrak{H}_r^* \triangleleft \mathfrak{H}^*$, $\mathfrak{H}_r^*$ being a $S_r$-subgroup of $\mathfrak{H}^*$. By maximality of $\mathfrak{H}^*$, $\mathfrak{H}_r^*$ is a maximal element of $\mathfrak{U}(\mathfrak{A}; r)$. Since $\mathfrak{H}$ contains a maximal element of $\mathfrak{U}(\mathfrak{A}; q)$, namely, $O_q(\mathfrak{R})$, so does $\mathfrak{H}^*$. It follows that $N(O_r(\mathfrak{L}))$ contains a maximal element $\mathfrak{D}^*$ of $\mathfrak{U}(\mathfrak{P}^*; q)$ where $\mathfrak{P}^*$ is a suitable $S_p$-subgroup of $N(O_r(\mathfrak{L}))$. But $\mathfrak{P} \subseteq N(O_r(\mathfrak{L}))$, and so $\mathfrak{P} = \mathfrak{P}^{*N}$ for some $N$ in $N(O_r(\mathfrak{L}))$, and so $\mathfrak{Q}^{*N} = \mathfrak{Q}_2$ is a maximal element of $\mathfrak{U}(\mathfrak{P}; q)$ normalizing $O_r(\mathfrak{V})$. By Lemma 17.4, $\mathfrak{Q}_2$ is a maximal element of $\mathfrak{U}(\mathfrak{A}; q)$.

Now $\mathfrak{P}$ centralizes $O_{q}(\mathfrak{R})$, and $O_{q}(\mathfrak{R})$ is a maximal element of $\mathcal{N}(\mathfrak{P}; q)$. It follows that $N(O_{q}(\mathfrak{R})) / C(O_{q}(\mathfrak{R}))$ is a $p'$-group. Since $\mathfrak{D}_2$ and $O_{q}(\mathfrak{R})$

are conjugate by Theorem 17.1, it follows that $N(\mathfrak{D}_2) / C(\mathfrak{D}_2)$ is a $p'$-group, and so $\mathfrak{P}$ centralizes $\mathfrak{D}_2$. By $C_{p,q}^*$, it follows that $\mathfrak{D}_2$ is a $S_q$-subgroup of $C(\mathfrak{P})$, completing the proof of this theorem.

THEOREM 22.5. If $p \in \pi_3$, then $\mathfrak{G}$ satisfies $C_{\pi_3(p)}$.

Proof. By Theorem 22.4, if $q, r \in \pi_3(p)$, then $q \sim r$. By Theorem 20.1, $\mathfrak{G}$ satisfies $E_q$ for $q, r \in \pi_3(p)$. By Theorem 21.2, $\mathfrak{G}$ satisfies $C_{\pi_3(p)}$.

Hypothesis 22.4.

(i) $p \in \pi_{3}, q \in \pi_{3} \cup \pi_{4}$.

(ii) If $\mathfrak{P}$ is a $S_{p}$-subgroup of $\mathfrak{G}$, then $\mathfrak{P}$ contains a normal subgroup $\mathfrak{G}$ of type $(p, p)$ which centralizes at least one maximal element of $\mathsf{U}(\mathfrak{P}; q)$.

LEMMA 22.5. Under Hypothesis 22.4, $\mathfrak{F}$ centralizes every element of $\mathcal{N}(\mathfrak{G}; q)$.

Proof. Suppose false and $\mathfrak{D}$ is an element of $\mathcal{N}(\mathfrak{E};q)$ minimal with respect to $\gamma\mathfrak{D}\mathfrak{E}\neq\langle1\rangle$. Then $\mathfrak{D}=\gamma\mathfrak{D}\mathfrak{E}$ and $\mathfrak{G}_{0}=C_{\mathfrak{E}}(\mathfrak{D})\neq\langle1\rangle$. Let $\mathfrak{D}=C(\mathfrak{G}_{0})$. Then $\mathfrak{D}$ contains an element $\mathfrak{A}$ of $\mathscr{SCN}_{3}(\mathfrak{P})$ with $\mathfrak{G}\subseteq\mathfrak{A}$. By Lemma 17.5, $\mathfrak{A}\subseteq O_{p^{\prime},p}(\mathfrak{G})$, and so $\mathfrak{Q}=\gamma\mathfrak{D}\mathfrak{E}$ is contained in $O_{p^{\prime}}(\mathfrak{G})$. If $\mathfrak{Q}^{*}$ is an $\mathfrak{A}$-invariant $S_{q}$-subgroup of $O_{p^{\prime}}(\mathfrak{G})$, it follows readily that $\gamma\mathfrak{Q}^{*}\mathfrak{E}\neq\langle1\rangle$. If $\tilde{\mathfrak{Q}}$ is a maximal element of $\mathcal{N}(\mathfrak{A};q)$ containing $\mathfrak{Q}^{*}$, then $\mathfrak{G}$ does not centralize $\tilde{\mathfrak{Q}}$. Let $\mathfrak{Q}_{0}$ be a maximal element of $\mathcal{N}(\mathfrak{P};q)$ centralizing $\mathfrak{G}$. Since $\mathfrak{Q}_{0}$ is also a maximal element of $\mathcal{N}(\mathfrak{A};q)$, we have $\mathfrak{Q}_{0}=\tilde{\mathfrak{Q}}^{g}$ for suitable $C$ in $C(\mathfrak{A})\subseteq C(\mathfrak{G})$. Since $\mathfrak{G}$ does not centralize $\tilde{\mathfrak{Q}}$, $\mathfrak{G}^{g}=\mathfrak{G}$ does not centralize $\mathfrak{Q}_{0}$. This contradiction completes the proof of this lemma.

The next theorem is fairly delicate and brings  $\pi_{4}$  into play explicitly for the first time.

Hypothesis 22.5.

(i) $p \in \pi_3, q \in \pi_4$.

(ii)  $p \sim q.$

THEOREM 22.6. Under Hypothesis 22.5, if $\mathfrak{P}$ is a $S_{p}$-subgroup of $\mathfrak{G}$ and $\mathfrak{D}_1$ is a maximal element of $\mathsf{N}(\mathfrak{P};q)$, then $\mathfrak{D}_1\neq\langle1\rangle$. If $\mathfrak{D}_2$ is a $S_{q}$-subgroup of $N(\mathfrak{D}_{1})$ permutable with $\mathfrak{P}$ and $\mathfrak{D}_3$ is a $S_{q}$-subgroup of $\mathfrak{G}$ containing $\mathfrak{D}_2$, then $\mathfrak{D}_1$ contains every element of $\mathcal{SCN}(\mathfrak{D}_3)$. Furthermore, $O_{p}(\mathfrak{P}\mathfrak{D}_{2})=\langle1\rangle$.

Proof. By Theorem 19.1, $\mathfrak{P}$ does not centralize $\mathfrak{D}_1$, so in particular $\mathfrak{D}_1 \neq \langle 1 \rangle$.

Suppose that $\mathfrak{Q}_2$ contains an element $\mathfrak{B}$ of $\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{Q}_3)$. By Lemma 19.1, $\mathfrak{B}$ centralizes $O_p(\mathfrak{P}\mathfrak{Q}_2)$ and since $\mathfrak{B}$ is a normal abelian subgroup of $\mathfrak{Q}_2$, (B) implies that $\mathfrak{B} \subseteq O_q(\mathfrak{P}\mathfrak{Q}_2)$. Let $\mathfrak{A}$ be an element of $\mathcal{S}\mathcal{C}\mathcal{N}_3(\mathfrak{Q}_3)$ containing $\mathfrak{B}$. Let $\mathfrak{H} = N(\mathfrak{B}) \cong \langle \mathfrak{Q}_3, O_p(\mathfrak{P}\mathfrak{Q}_2) \rangle$. Since $q \in \pi_4$, $O_{q'}(\mathfrak{H}) = \langle 1 \rangle$, and (B) implies that $\mathfrak{A} \subseteq O_q(\mathfrak{H})$. Hence, $[\mathfrak{A} \cap \mathfrak{Q}_2, O_p(\mathfrak{P}\mathfrak{Q}_2)] \subseteq O_q(\mathfrak{H}) \cap O_p(\mathfrak{P}\mathfrak{Q}_2) = \langle 1 \rangle$, and by (B), $\mathfrak{A} \cap \mathfrak{Q}_2 \subseteq O_{p,q}(\mathfrak{P}\mathfrak{Q}_2)$, and so $\mathfrak{A} \cap \mathfrak{Q}_2 \subseteq O_q(\mathfrak{P}\mathfrak{Q}_2)$, that is, $\mathfrak{A} \cap \mathfrak{Q}_2 = \mathfrak{A} \cap \mathfrak{Q}_1$. If $\mathfrak{A} \cap \mathfrak{Q}_1 \subset \mathfrak{A}$, then $\mathfrak{A} \cap \mathfrak{Q}_1 \subset N_{\mathfrak{A}}(\mathfrak{Q}_1)$, contrary to $N_{\mathfrak{A}}(\mathfrak{Q}_1) \subseteq \mathfrak{A} \cap \mathfrak{Q}_2 = \mathfrak{A} \cap \mathfrak{Q}_1$. Hence, $\mathfrak{A} \subseteq \mathfrak{Q}_1$. Since $q \in \pi_4$, Corollary 17.3 implies that $\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{A}; p)$ is trivial, so $O_p(\mathfrak{P}\mathfrak{Q}_2) = \langle 1 \rangle$. By Lemma 7.9, it follows that $\mathfrak{Q}_1$ contains every element of $\mathcal{S}\mathcal{C}\mathcal{N}(\mathfrak{Q}_3)$, and not merely $\mathfrak{A}$. This proves the theorem in this case.

We can now assume that  $D_{2}$  does not contain any element of  $\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{D}_{3})$ , and try to derive a contradiction.

Since $\mathfrak{D}_2$ is a $S_q$-subgroup of the normalizer of every non identity normal subgroup of $\mathfrak{P}\mathfrak{D}_2$, if $D(\mathfrak{D}_1) \neq \langle 1 \rangle$, then $N_{\mathfrak{D}_3}(D(\mathfrak{D}_1))$ contains an element of $\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{D}_3)$, and since $N_{\mathfrak{D}_3}(D(\mathfrak{D}_1)) = \mathfrak{D}_2$ in this case, $\mathfrak{D}_2$ contains an element of $\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{D}_3)$, contrary to assumption. Hence, $D(\mathfrak{D}_1) = \langle 1 \rangle$.

Let $\mathfrak{Q}_1^* = O_{p,q}(\mathfrak{P}\mathfrak{Q}_2) \cap \mathfrak{Q}_2$. Since $[\mathfrak{Q}_1^*, \mathfrak{Q}_1] = [O_{p,q}(\mathfrak{P}\mathfrak{Q}_2), \mathfrak{Q}_1] \triangleleft \mathfrak{P}\mathfrak{Q}_2$, and since every element of $\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{Q}_3)$ normalizes $[\mathfrak{Q}_1^*, \mathfrak{Q}_1]$, we conclude that $\mathfrak{Q}_1 \subseteq Z(\mathfrak{Q}_1^*)$. Since $D(\mathfrak{Q}_1^*) \cap \mathfrak{Q}_1$ is normalized by every element of $\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{Q}_3)$ and also by $\langle O_p(\mathfrak{P}\mathfrak{Q}_2), \mathfrak{P}\mathfrak{Q}_2 \cap N(\mathfrak{Q}_1^*) \rangle = \mathfrak{P}\mathfrak{Q}_2$, we have $D(\mathfrak{Q}_1^*) \cap \mathfrak{Q}_1 = \langle 1 \rangle$. This implies that $\mathfrak{Q}_1^* = \mathfrak{Q}_1 \times \mathfrak{F}$ for a suitable subgroup $\mathfrak{F}$ of $\mathfrak{Q}_1^*$.

Since $Z(\mathfrak{Q}_3) \subseteq \mathfrak{Q}_2$, we have $Z(\mathfrak{Q}_3) \subseteq \mathfrak{Q}_1^*$, by (B). Since $\mathfrak{Q}_2$ contains no element of $\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{Q}_3)$, $Z(\mathfrak{Q}_3)$ is cyclic. For the same reason, $Z(\mathfrak{Q}_3) \cap \mathfrak{Q}_1 = \langle 1 \rangle$, since otherwise, $\Omega_1(Z(\mathfrak{Q}_3)) \subseteq \mathfrak{Q}_1$ and every element of $\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{Q}_3)$ normalizes $\mathfrak{Q}_1$. In particular, $\mathfrak{Q}_1$ is a proper subgroup of $\mathfrak{Q}_1^*$. This implies that $O_p(\mathfrak{P}\mathfrak{Q}_2) \neq \langle 1 \rangle$. More exactly, $\mathfrak{Q}_1 = C_{\mathfrak{Q}_1^*}(O_p(\mathfrak{P}\mathfrak{Q}_2))$.

Let $\mathfrak{B} \in \mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{Q}_3)$ and let $\widetilde{\mathfrak{Q}}_1 = C_{\mathfrak{Q}_1}(\mathfrak{B})$, so that $|\mathfrak{Q}_1: \widetilde{\mathfrak{Q}}_1| = q$.

Suppose $O_{p}(\mathfrak{P}\mathfrak{Q}_{2})$ is non cyclic. In this case, a basic property of $p$-groups implies that $O_{p}(\mathfrak{P}\mathfrak{Q}_{2})$ contains a subgroup $\mathfrak{G}$ of type $(p, p)$ which is normal in $\mathfrak{P}$. Since $\mathfrak{Q}_{1}$ is a maximal element of $\mathfrak{N}(\mathfrak{P}; q)$, Hypothesis 22.4 is satisfied. Since $\widetilde{\mathfrak{Q}}_{1}$ is of index $q$ in $\mathfrak{Q}_{1}$, Theorem 22.1 implies that $\widetilde{\mathfrak{Q}}_{1} \neq \langle 1 \rangle$. Hence, $\langle \mathfrak{B}, \mathfrak{G} \rangle$ is a proper subgroup of $\mathfrak{G}$ centralizing $\widetilde{\mathfrak{Q}}_{1}$. Choose $\mathfrak{B}_{1} \in ccl_{\mathfrak{G}}(\mathfrak{B})$ and $\mathfrak{C}_{1} \in ccl_{\mathfrak{G}}(\mathfrak{G})$ so that $\mathfrak{R} = \langle \mathfrak{B}_{1}, \mathfrak{C}_{1} \rangle$ is minimal. By $D_{p,q}$ in $\mathfrak{R}$, it follows that $\mathfrak{R}$ is a $p, q$-group. By Lemma 19.1, $\mathfrak{B}_{1}^{\mathfrak{R}}$ centralizes $O_{p}(\mathfrak{R})$ and by Lemma 22.5, $\mathfrak{C}_{1}^{\mathfrak{R}}$ centralizes $O_{q}(\mathfrak{R})$. It follows that $\mathfrak{R} = \mathfrak{B}_{1} \times \mathfrak{C}_{1}$. Let $\mathfrak{R} = N(\mathfrak{B}_{1})$. Since $q \in \pi_{4}, F(\mathfrak{R})$ is a $q$-group. By Lemma 22.5, $\mathfrak{C}_{1}$ centralizes $F(\mathfrak{R})$ so 3.3 is violated. This contradiction shows that $O_{p}(\mathfrak{P}\mathfrak{Q}_{2})$ is cyclic.

Since $\mathfrak{Q}_1 = C_{\mathfrak{Q}_1^*}(O_p(\mathfrak{P}\mathfrak{Q}_2))$, it follows that $\mathfrak{F}$ is cyclic of an order -dividing $p - 1$.

Let $\mathfrak{B} = \Omega_1(\mathfrak{D}_1^*) = \mathfrak{D}_1 \times \Omega_1(\mathfrak{F})$, and let $\mathfrak{D}_1 = N_{\mathfrak{P}\mathfrak{D}_2}(\mathfrak{B})$. We see that $\mathfrak{P}\mathfrak{D}_2 = \mathfrak{D}_1O_p(\mathfrak{P}\mathfrak{D}_2)$, $\mathfrak{D}_1 \cap O_p(\mathfrak{P}\mathfrak{D}_2) = \langle 1 \rangle$. Let $\mathfrak{M} = N(\mathfrak{B})$, $\mathfrak{M}_1 = C(\mathfrak{B})$. It is clear that $\mathfrak{M}_1 \cap \mathfrak{P}\mathfrak{D}_2 = \mathfrak{D}_1^*$, since $\mathfrak{M}_1 \cap O_p(\mathfrak{P}\mathfrak{D}_2) = \langle 1 \rangle$, and since $\mathfrak{D}_1^*$ is a $S_q$-subgroup of $O_p_q(\mathfrak{P}\mathfrak{D}_2)$.

Let $\mathfrak{L} = O_q(\mathfrak{M} \bmod \mathfrak{M}_1)$. We see that $\mathfrak{L} \cap \mathfrak{P}\mathfrak{D}_2 = \mathfrak{Q}_1^*$, again since $\mathfrak{Q}_1^*$ is a $S_q$-subgroup of $O_{p,q}(\mathfrak{P}\mathfrak{D}_2)$. We observe that since $\mathfrak{Q}_1^*$ contains $Z(\mathfrak{Q}_3)$, $\mathfrak{M}$ contains every element of $\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{Q}_3)$, and so contains $\mathfrak{B}$. By Lemma 7.1, $\mathfrak{L}$ contains $\mathfrak{B}$. Hence, $\mathfrak{L} \supset \mathfrak{M}_1$.

We next show that $\mathfrak{L}/\mathfrak{M}_{1}=\overline{\mathfrak{L}}$ is elementary. If $D(\overline{\mathfrak{L}})\neq\langle1\rangle$, then by a basic property of $q$-groups, $C_{\mathfrak{B}}(D(\overline{\mathfrak{L}}))$ is of order at least $q^{2}$. Hence, $\hat{\mathfrak{Q}}_{1}=C_{\mathfrak{B}}(D(\overline{\mathfrak{L}}))\cap\mathfrak{Q}_{1}\neq\langle1\rangle$. But in this case, $\hat{\mathfrak{Q}}_{1}$ is normalized by $\langle O_{p}(\mathfrak{P}\mathfrak{Q}_{2}),\mathfrak{H}_{1}\rangle=\mathfrak{P}\mathfrak{Q}_{2}$, and is centralized by $D(\mathfrak{L}\bmod\mathfrak{M}_{1})$, and so $\mathfrak{Q}_{2}$ is not a $S_{q}$-subgroup of $N(\mathfrak{Q}_{1})$. This is not possible, so $D(\overline{\mathfrak{L}})=\langle1\rangle$. We have in fact shown that if $\mathfrak{L}_{1}\triangleleft\mathfrak{M}$, and $\mathfrak{M}_{1}\subset\mathfrak{L}_{1}\subseteq\mathfrak{L}$, then $C_{\mathfrak{B}}(\mathfrak{L}_{1})$ is of order $q$.

Since $\overline{\mathfrak{V}}$ is abelian, $\mathfrak{V}$ normalizes $[\mathfrak{V},\mathfrak{B}] = \Omega_1(Z(\mathfrak{D}_3))$. It follows that $C_{\mathfrak{B}}(\mathfrak{V}) = \Omega_1(Z(\mathfrak{D}_3))$.

Let $\mathfrak{L}_1 = \langle \mathfrak{B}^{\mathfrak{M}}, \mathfrak{M}_1 \rangle$, and let $\mathfrak{B}_1 = \mathfrak{B}^{\mathfrak{M}}$, $M \in \mathfrak{M}$. Since $\mathfrak{B}$ and $\mathfrak{B}_1$ are conjugate in $\mathfrak{M}$, $[\mathfrak{B}, \mathfrak{B}_1]$ is of order $q$ and is centralized by $\mathfrak{L}$. It follows that $[\mathfrak{B}, \mathfrak{B}_1] = \Omega_1(Z(\mathfrak{D}_3))$. Since $\bar{\mathfrak{L}}$ is abelian, and since $\mathfrak{B}^{\mathfrak{M}}$ covers $\mathfrak{L}_1 / \mathfrak{M}_1 = \bar{\mathfrak{L}}_1$, it follows that $[\mathfrak{B}, \mathfrak{L}_1] = \Omega_1(Z(\mathfrak{D}_3))$. Let $|\mathfrak{D}_1| = q^n$, and $|\mathfrak{L}_1 : \mathfrak{M}_1| = q^m$. Since each element of $\bar{\mathfrak{L}}_1^\sharp$ determines a non trivial homomorphism of $\mathfrak{B} / \Omega_1(Z(\mathfrak{D}_3))$ into $\Omega_1(Z(\mathfrak{D}_3))$, it follows that $m \leq n$. Since $C_{\mathfrak{B}}(\mathfrak{L}_1) = \Omega_1(Z(\mathfrak{D}_3))$, it also follows that $m \geq n$. Hence, $m = n$. This implies that $\mathfrak{L}_1 = \mathfrak{L}$, since any $q$-element of Aut $\mathfrak{B}$ which centralizes $\bar{\mathfrak{L}}_1$ is in $\bar{\mathfrak{L}}_1$, by 3.10. Here we are invoking the well known fact that $\bar{\mathfrak{L}}_1$ is normal in a $S_q$-subgroup $\mathfrak{Q}$ of Aut $\mathfrak{B}$ and is in fact in $\mathcal{H}CN(\mathfrak{Q})$. (This appeal to the “enormous” group Aut $\mathfrak{B}$ is somewhat curious.)

Returning to $\mathfrak{L}$, let $\mathfrak{V}^*$ be a $S_q$-subgroup of $\mathfrak{L}$, and let $\mathfrak{W} = \Omega_1(\mathfrak{V}^*)$. Since $\mathcal{O}^1(\mathfrak{V}^*) \subseteq Z(\mathfrak{V}^*)$, and $Z(\mathfrak{V}^*)$ is cyclic, it is easy to see that $\Omega_1(Z(\mathfrak{W})) = \Omega_1(Z(\mathfrak{Q}_3))$, and that $\mathfrak{W}/\Omega_1(Z(\mathfrak{W}))$ is abelian. Hence, $\mathfrak{W}$ is an extra special group of order $q^{2n+1}$ and exponent $q$.

We next show that $\mathfrak{M}_1$ is a $p'$-group. Since $\mathfrak{M}_1 \subseteq C(\mathfrak{Q}_1)$, it suffices to show that no non identity $p$-element of $N(\mathfrak{Q}_1)$ centralizes $\mathfrak{B}$. This is clear by $D_{p,q}$ in $N(\mathfrak{Q}_1)$, together with the fact that no non identity $p$-element of $\mathfrak{P}\mathfrak{Q}_2$ centralizes $\mathfrak{B}$.

Since $\mathfrak{M}_1$ is a $p'$-group, so is $\mathfrak{L}$. Since $\mathfrak{L} \triangleleft \mathfrak{M}$, we assume without loss of generality that $N_{\mathfrak{B}}(\mathfrak{V})$ normalizes $\mathfrak{V}^*$.

Let $\mathfrak{C} \in \mathcal{SCN}_{3}(\mathfrak{P})$, and set $\mathfrak{C}_1 = \mathfrak{C} \cap N_{\mathfrak{P}}(\mathfrak{V})$. Since $\mathfrak{P} = O_p(\mathfrak{P}\mathfrak{D}_2) \cdot \mathfrak{N}_{\mathfrak{P}}(\mathfrak{V})$ and $O_p(\mathfrak{P}\mathfrak{D}_2)$ is cyclic, $\mathfrak{C}_1$ is non cyclic. Since $\mathfrak{C}_1$ is faithfully represented on $\mathfrak{V}$, it is faithfully represented on $\mathfrak{W} = \Omega_1(\mathfrak{V}^*)$. Since $p > q$, $\mathfrak{C}_1$ centralizes $\Omega_1(Z(\mathfrak{W}))$.

We can now choose $C$ in $\mathfrak{C}_1^*$ so that $\mathfrak{C}_1$ does not centralize $\mathfrak{W}_1 = C_{\mathfrak{W}}(C)$. Let $\mathfrak{W}_2 = [\mathfrak{W}_1, \mathfrak{C}_1]$. We will show that $\mathfrak{W}_2$ is non abelian. To do this, we first show that $\mathfrak{W}_1$ is extra special. Let $W \in \mathfrak{W}_1 - \Omega_1(Z(\mathfrak{W}))$. Since $C$ centralizes $W$, $C$ normalizes $C_{\mathfrak{W}}(W)$. Since $p > q$, $C$ acts trivially on $\mathfrak{W} / C_{\mathfrak{W}}(W)$, and so $C$ centralizes some element of $\mathfrak{W} - C_{\mathfrak{W}}(W)$. It follows that $Z(\mathfrak{W}_1) = Z(\mathfrak{W})$, so that $\mathfrak{W}_1$ is extra special. We can now find $\mathfrak{W}_3 \subseteq \mathfrak{W}_1$ so that $\mathfrak{W}_1 = \mathfrak{W}_2\mathfrak{W}_3$ and $\mathfrak{W}_3 \cap \mathfrak{W}_3 \subseteq Z(\mathfrak{W})$; in fact, we take $\mathfrak{W}_3 = C_{\mathfrak{W}_1}(\mathfrak{W}_2)$. By the argument just given, $\mathfrak{W}_3$ is extra special. Since $\mathfrak{W}_1$ is, too, it follows that $\mathfrak{W}_2$ is extra special, hence is non abelian.

For such an element $C$, let $\mathfrak{T} = C(C) \cong \langle \mathfrak{C}, \mathfrak{W}_2 \rangle$. By Lemma 17.5, $\mathfrak{C} \subseteq O_{p', p}(\mathfrak{T})$. Since $\mathfrak{W}_2 = [\mathfrak{W}_2, \mathfrak{C}_1]$, by Lemma 8.11, it follows that $\mathfrak{W}_2 \subseteq O_{p'}(\mathfrak{T})$. It follows now that $\mathcal{N}(\mathfrak{C}; q)$ contains a non abelian group. But now Theorem 17.1 implies that the maximal elements of $\mathcal{N}(\mathfrak{C}; q)$ are non abelian. Since $\mathfrak{D}_1$ is a maximal element of $\mathcal{N}(\mathfrak{C}; q)$ and $\mathfrak{D}_1$ is elementary, we have a contradiction, completing the proof of this theorem.

THEOREM 22.7. If $p, q \in \pi_3$, and $p \sim q$, then $\pi(p) = \pi(q)$.

Proof. Suppose $p \sim r$. By Theorem 22.4, we can suppose that $r \in \pi_4$. Proceeding by way of contradiction, we can assume that a $S_q$-subgroup $\mathfrak{Q}$ of $\mathfrak{G}$ centralizes every element of $\mathfrak{U}(\mathfrak{Q}; r)$, by Theorem 22.1. By Theorem 19.1, a $S_p$-subgroup $\mathfrak{P}$ of $\mathfrak{G}$ does not centralize every element of $\mathfrak{U}(\mathfrak{P}; r)$. Applying Theorem 22.2, we can suppose that $\mathfrak{P}$ centralizes every element of $\mathfrak{U}(\mathfrak{P}; q)$.

Let $\mathfrak{Q}_1$ be a maximal element of $U(\mathfrak{P}; q)$ and let $\mathfrak{R}_1$ be a maximal element of $U(\mathfrak{P}; r)$. Let $\mathfrak{R}_2$ be a $S_r$-subgroup of $N(\mathfrak{R}_1)$ permutable with $\mathfrak{P}$ and let $\mathfrak{R}_3$ be a $S_r$-subgroup of $\mathfrak{G}$ containing $\mathfrak{R}_2$. Let $\mathfrak{A} \in \mathcal{SCN}_3(\mathfrak{P})$. By Theorem 22.6, $O_p(\mathfrak{P}\mathfrak{R}_2) = \langle 1 \rangle$, so $\mathfrak{A}$ does not centralize $\mathfrak{R}_1$. We can then find $A$ in $\mathfrak{A}^*$ such that $\mathfrak{R}_1^* = [C_{\mathfrak{R}_1}(A), \mathfrak{A}] \neq \langle 1 \rangle$.

Suppose $\mathfrak{Q}_1$ is non cyclic. Then by $C_{p,q}^*$, $\mathfrak{Q}_1$ contains an element of $\mathscr{W}(q)$. Let $\mathfrak{Q} = C(A) \cong \langle \mathfrak{A}, \mathfrak{R}_1^*, \mathfrak{Q}_1 \rangle = \mathfrak{T}$, and let $\mathfrak{K}$ be an $\mathfrak{A}$-invariant $S_q$-subgroup of $O_p(\mathfrak{T})$ with Sylow system $\mathfrak{R}_r$, $\mathfrak{Q}_1$. By Theorem 22.3, $\mathfrak{Q}_1 \triangleleft \mathfrak{K}$. Since $N(\mathfrak{Q}_1) = O^p(N(\mathfrak{Q}_1))$, it follows that $\gamma \mathfrak{A} \mathfrak{R}_r = \langle 1 \rangle$ by Lemma 8.11 and the fact that $N(\mathfrak{Q}_1)$ does not contain an elementary subgroup of order $r^3$. This violates the fact that $\mathfrak{R}_1^* = \gamma \mathfrak{R}_1^* \mathfrak{A} \neq \langle 1 \rangle$, by $D_p$ in $\mathfrak{Q}$. Hence, $\mathfrak{Q}_1$ is cyclic.

Since $\gamma\mathfrak{D}_1\mathfrak{P}=\langle 1\rangle$, $\mathfrak{P}_1=O_p(\mathfrak{P}\mathfrak{D}_2)\neq\langle 1\rangle$, where $\mathfrak{D}_2$ is a $S_q$-subgroup of $\mathfrak{G}$ permutable with $\mathfrak{P}$ and containing $\mathfrak{D}_1$, which exists by $C_{p,q}^*$. Since $N(\mathfrak{P}_1)=O^q(N(\mathfrak{P}_1))$, it follows that $\mathfrak{D}_1\subseteq Z(\mathfrak{D}_2)$, $\mathfrak{D}_1$ being a $S_q$-subgroup of $O_{p'}(N(\mathfrak{P}_1))$.

Let $\mathfrak{B} = V(ccl_{\mathfrak{B}}(\mathfrak{A});\mathfrak{B})$, and $\mathfrak{N}_1 = N(Z(\mathfrak{B}))$. By Lemma 17.3, $\mathfrak{N}_1 =$

$O_{p'}(\mathfrak{N}_1) \cdot \mathfrak{N}_1 \cap N(\mathfrak{N}_1)$. Since $\mathfrak{D}_1$ is a $S_q$-subgroup of $O_{p'}(\mathfrak{N}_1)$, it follows readily that $\mathfrak{N}_1 \cap N(\mathfrak{N}_1)$ contains an element of $\mathscr{W}(q)$. In particular, $N(\mathfrak{R}_1)$ contains an element of $\mathscr{W}(q)$. If $\mathfrak{L}$ is a $S_q_r$-subgroup of $N(\mathfrak{R}_1)$ with Sylow system $\mathfrak{L}_q, \mathfrak{L}_r$, then $\mathfrak{L}_q \triangleleft \mathfrak{L}$, by Theorem 22.3. By Theorem 22.6, $\mathfrak{R}_1$ contains an element $\mathfrak{C}$ of $\mathscr{SCN}_3(\mathfrak{R}_3)$. By Corollary 17.3, $\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{C})$ is trivial. Since $\mathfrak{L}_q \in \mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{C})$, we have a contradiction, completing the proof of this theorem.

## 23. Preliminary Results about the Maximal Subgroups of $\mathfrak{G}$

Hypothesis 23.1.

(i) $\varpi$ is a non empty subset of $\pi_{3}$.

(ii) For at least one $p$ in $\varpi$, $\varpi = \pi(p)$.

We remark that by Theorem 22.7, Hypothesis 23.1 (ii) is equivalent to

(ii)' $\pi(p) = \varpi$ for all $p$ in $\varpi$.

Under Hypothesis 23.1, Theorem 22.5 implies that $\mathfrak{G}$ contains a $S_{\varpi}$-subgroup $\mathfrak{G}$. Since $\mathfrak{G}$ also satisfies $E_{\varpi_1}$ for all subsets $\varpi_1$ of $\varpi$, $\mathfrak{G}$ is a proper subgroup of $\mathfrak{G}$ by P. Hall's characterization of solvable groups [15]. This section is devoted to a study of $\mathfrak{G}$ and its normalizer $\mathfrak{M} = N(\mathfrak{G})$. All results of this section assume that Hypothesis 23.1 holds. Let $\varpi = \{p_1, \cdots, p_n\}$, $n \geq 1$, and let $\mathfrak{P}_1, \cdots, \mathfrak{P}_n$ be a Sylow system for $\mathfrak{G}$.

LEMMA 23.1. M is a maximal subgroup of G and is the unique maximal subgroup of G containing H.

Proof. Let $\mathfrak{R}$ be any proper subgroup of $\mathfrak{G}$ containing $\mathfrak{H}$. We must show that $\mathfrak{R} \subseteq \mathfrak{M}$. Since $\mathfrak{R}$ is solvable we assume without loss of generality that $\mathfrak{R}$ is a $\varpi$, $q$-group for some $q \notin \varpi$. Let $\mathfrak{P}_1, \ldots, \mathfrak{P}_n$, $\mathfrak{Q}$ be a Sylow system for $\mathfrak{R}$. It suffices to show that $\mathfrak{P}_1 \triangleleft \mathfrak{P}_1\mathfrak{Q}$.

Since $q \notin \mathfrak{w}$, $p_1 \neq q$. Theorem 22.1 implies that $\mathfrak{P}_1$ centralizes $O_q(\mathfrak{P}_1\mathfrak{Q})$. By Lemma 17.5, $\mathfrak{B} \triangleleft \mathfrak{P}_1\mathfrak{Q}$, where $\mathfrak{B} = V(ccl_{\mathfrak{G}}(\mathfrak{A}); \mathfrak{P}_1)$ and $\mathfrak{A} \in \mathcal{SCN}_3(\mathfrak{P}_1)$. By Lemma 17.2, $\mathfrak{N}_1 = N(Z(\mathfrak{B})) = O^{p_1}(\mathfrak{N}_1)$. Since $\mathfrak{N}_1$ does not contain an elementary subgroup of order $q^s$, Lemma 8.13 implies that $\mathfrak{P}_1$ centralizes every $q$-factor of $\mathfrak{P}_1\mathfrak{Q}$ and so $\mathfrak{P}_1 \triangleleft \mathfrak{P}_1\mathfrak{Q}$, completing the proof of this lemma.

LEMMA 23.2. If $p_i \in \pi(F(\mathfrak{H}))$, and $\mathfrak{A}_i \in \mathscr{SCN}_3(\mathfrak{P}_i)$, then $C(\mathfrak{A}_i) \subseteq \mathfrak{M}$.

Proof. We can assume that i=1. By  $C_{p_{1},p_{j}}^{*}$  & contains a  $S_{p_{j}}$ -subgroup of  $C(\mathfrak{A}_{1})$  for each  $j=2,\cdots,n$ . Thus, it suffices to show that if  $q\notin\varpi$ , and D is a  $S_{q}$ -subgroup of  $C(\mathfrak{A}_{1})$  permutable with  $P_{1}$ ,

then $\mathfrak{Q} \subseteq \mathfrak{M}$.

By the preceding argument, $\mathfrak{P}_1 \triangleleft \mathfrak{P}_1\mathfrak{D}$. Since $\mathfrak{P}_1$ normalizes $C(\mathfrak{A}_1) = \mathfrak{A}_1 \times \mathfrak{D}$, $\mathfrak{D}$ being a $p_1'$-group, it follows that $\mathfrak{P}_1\mathfrak{D} = \mathfrak{P}_1 \times \mathfrak{D}$.

Since $F(\mathfrak{H}) \cap \mathfrak{P}_1 \neq \langle 1 \rangle$, it follows that $\mathfrak{M} = N(F(\mathfrak{H}) \cap \mathfrak{P}_1)$, since $F(\mathfrak{H}) \cap \mathfrak{P}_1$ char $\mathfrak{H} \triangleleft \mathfrak{M}$ and $\mathfrak{M}$ is the unique maximal subgroup of $\mathfrak{G}$ containing $\mathfrak{H}$. The lemma follows since $N(F(\mathfrak{H}) \cap \mathfrak{P}_1) \supseteq C(F(\mathfrak{H}) \cap \mathfrak{P}_1) \supseteq \mathfrak{D}$.

LEMMA 23.3. Let $1 \leq i \leq n$, and let $\mathfrak{A}_i \in \mathcal{SCN}_3(\mathfrak{P}_i)$, $\mathfrak{B}_i = V(ccl_{\mathfrak{G}}(\mathfrak{A}_i); \mathfrak{P}_i)$. If $C(\mathfrak{A}_i) \subseteq \mathfrak{M}$, then $N(\mathfrak{B}_i) \subseteq \mathfrak{M}$.

Proof. We can assume that $i=1$. If $F(\mathfrak{H})$ is a $p_{1}$-group, then Lemma 17.5 implies that $\mathfrak{B}_{1} \triangleleft \mathfrak{H}$ and so $\mathfrak{B}_{1} \triangleleft \mathfrak{M}$, since $\mathfrak{B}_{1}$ is weakly closed in $F(\mathfrak{H}) \cap \mathfrak{P}_{1}$. In this case, $N(\mathfrak{B}_{1}) = \mathfrak{M}$ and we are done.

We can suppose that $F(\mathfrak{H})$ is not a $p_1$-group, and so $\mathfrak{T} = O_{p'}(\mathfrak{H}) \neq \langle 1 \rangle$. Let $\mathfrak{T}_2, \cdots, \mathfrak{T}_n$ be a $\mathfrak{P}_1$-invariant Sylow system for $\mathfrak{T}$, where $\mathfrak{T}_i$ is a $S_{p_i}$-subgroup of $\mathfrak{T}$ and we allow $\mathfrak{T}_i = \langle 1 \rangle$. By $C_{p_1, p_i}^*, \mathfrak{T}_i$ is a maximal element of $\mathcal{N}(\mathfrak{P}_1; p_i)$.

Let $N \in N(\mathfrak{B}_1)$. Then by Theorem 17.1, $\mathfrak{T}_i^N = \mathfrak{T}_i^{o_i}$ where $C_2, \cdots, C_n$ are in $C(\mathfrak{A}_i) \subseteq \mathfrak{M}$. Since $\mathfrak{T}$ char $\mathfrak{H} \triangleleft \mathfrak{M}$, each $\mathfrak{T}_i^{o_i}$ is contained in $\mathfrak{T}$ and so $\mathfrak{T}^N = \mathfrak{T}$. Since $\mathfrak{T} \neq \langle 1 \rangle$, $\mathfrak{M} = N(\mathfrak{T}) \supseteq N(\mathfrak{B}_1)$, as required.

LEMMA 23.4. Let $1 \leq i \leq n$, $\mathfrak{A}_i \in \mathcal{PCN}_s(\mathfrak{P}_i)$, $\mathfrak{B}_i = V(ccl_{\mathfrak{G}}(\mathfrak{A}_i); \mathfrak{P}_i)$. If $\langle C(\mathfrak{A}_i), N(\mathfrak{B}_i) \rangle \subseteq \mathfrak{M}$, then $\mathfrak{M}$ is the unique maximal subgroup of $\mathfrak{G}$ containing $\mathfrak{P}_i$.

Proof. We can assume that $i=1$. Let $\mathfrak{D}$ be a $q$-subgroup of $\mathfrak{G}$ permutable with $\mathfrak{P}_{1}$. It suffices to show that $\mathfrak{D} \subseteq \mathfrak{M}$.

Since $\mathfrak{Q} = O_{q}(\mathfrak{P}_{1}\mathfrak{Q})\cdot N_{\mathfrak{Q}}(\mathfrak{P}_{1})$, it suffices to show that $\mathfrak{Q}_1 = O_q(\mathfrak{P}_1\mathfrak{Q})\subseteq$$\mathfrak{M}$. If $\mathfrak{Q}$ is centralized by $\mathfrak{P}_1$, then by hypothesis $\mathfrak{Q}\subseteq \mathfrak{M}$. Otherwise we apply Theorem 22.1 and conclude that $q\in \varpi$. By Theorem 17.1, $\mathfrak{Q}_1^{\sigma}\subseteq \mathfrak{H}$ for suitable $C\in C(\mathfrak{A}_1)\subseteq \mathfrak{M}$, and the lemma follows.

LEMMA 23.5. For each $i=1,\cdots,n$, if $\mathfrak{A}_{i}\in\mathcal{SCN}_{s}(\mathfrak{P}_{i})$, then $\boldsymbol{C}(\mathfrak{A}_{i})\subseteq\mathfrak{M}$, and $\mathfrak{M}$ is the unique maximal subgroup of $\mathfrak{G}$ containing $\mathfrak{P}_{i}$.

Proof. First, suppose $p_i \in \pi(F(\mathfrak{Q}))$. Then $C(\mathfrak{A}_i) \subseteq \mathfrak{M}$, by Lemma 23.2. Then by Lemma 23.3, $N(\mathfrak{B}_i) \subseteq \mathfrak{M}$, $\mathfrak{B}_i = V(ccl_{\mathfrak{G}}(\mathfrak{A}_i); \mathfrak{P}_i)$, and then by Lemma 23.4, this lemma follows. We can suppose that $p_i \notin \pi(F(\mathfrak{Q}))$.

We assume that $i=1$. Let $C(\mathfrak{A}_{1})=\mathfrak{A}_{1}\times\mathfrak{D}$, where $\mathfrak{D}$ is a $p_{1}^{\prime}$-group. It suffices to show that for each $q$ in $\pi(\mathfrak{D})$, $\mathfrak{M}$ contains a $S_{q}$-subgroup $\mathfrak{D}$ of $\mathfrak{D}$. If $q\in\varpi$, this is the case by $C_{p_{1},q}^{*}$, so we can suppose that $q\notin\varpi$.

Since $p_1 \notin \pi(F(\mathfrak{H}))$, $\mathfrak{A}_1$ does not centralize $F(\mathfrak{H})$. If $F(\mathfrak{H})$ were cyclic, and $p = \max\{p_1, \cdots, p_n\}$, then a $S_p$-subgroup of $\mathfrak{H}$ would be contained in $F(\mathfrak{H})$ and so be cyclic. Since this is not the case, $F(\mathfrak{H})$ is non cyclic,

so we can assume that $F(\mathfrak{H}) \cap \mathfrak{P}_2$ is non cyclic. We can then find $A$ in $\mathfrak{A}_1^*$ so that $C(A) \cap F(\mathfrak{H}) \cap \mathfrak{P}_2$ contains an element of $\mathscr{W}(p_2)$, say $\mathfrak{W}$. Let $\mathfrak{L}^* = \langle \mathfrak{D}, \mathfrak{W}, \mathfrak{A}_1 \rangle \subseteq C(A)$, and let $\mathfrak{L}$ be a $S_{p_1, p_2, q}$-subgroup of $\mathfrak{L}^*$ with Sylow system $\mathfrak{L}_{p_1}, \mathfrak{L}_{p_2}, \mathfrak{L}_q$, where $\mathfrak{A}_1 \subseteq \mathfrak{L}_{p_1}$ and $\mathfrak{Q} \subseteq \mathfrak{L}_q$. Since $\mathfrak{A}_1 \subseteq O_{p_i', p_1}(\mathfrak{L}^*)$ by Lemma 17.5, it follows that $\mathfrak{A}_1 O_{p_i'}(\mathfrak{L}^*) / O_{p_i'}(\mathfrak{L}^*)$ is a central factor of $\mathfrak{L}^*$. Hence, $\mathfrak{A}_1$ is a $S_{p_1}$-subgroup of $\mathfrak{L}^*$ and so $\mathfrak{L}^* = \mathfrak{A}_1 \cdot O_{p_i'}(\mathfrak{L}^*)$.

We apply Theorem 22.3 and conclude that $\mathfrak{L}_{p_2} \triangleleft \mathfrak{L}$. If $\widetilde{\mathfrak{L}}$ is a maximal element of $\mathcal{N}(\mathfrak{A}_1; p_2, q)$ containing $\mathfrak{L}_{p_2} \cdot \mathfrak{L}_q$, it follows that $\widetilde{\mathfrak{L}}_{p_2} \triangleleft \widetilde{\mathfrak{L}}$, where $\widetilde{\mathfrak{L}}_{p_2}$ is a maximal element of $\mathcal{N}(\mathfrak{A}_1; p_2)$. By construction, $\widetilde{\mathfrak{L}}$ contains $\mathfrak{Q}$. By Theorem 17.1, there is an element $C$ in $C(\mathfrak{A}_1)$ such that $\widetilde{\mathfrak{L}}_{p_2}^{\sigma} = O_{p_2}(\mathfrak{P}_1\mathfrak{P}_2)$. Since $\mathfrak{Q}^{\sigma}$ normalizes $\widetilde{\mathfrak{L}}_{p_2}^{\sigma}$, it follows that $N(O_{p_2}(\mathfrak{P}_1\mathfrak{P}_2))$ contains a $S_q$-subgroup of $C(\mathfrak{A}_1)$. But $p_2 \in \pi(F(\mathfrak{Q}))$, so by what is already proved, we have $N(O_{p_2}(\mathfrak{P}_1\mathfrak{P}_2)) \subseteq \mathfrak{M}$, and so $\mathfrak{M}$ contains $C(\mathfrak{A}_1)$. We apply Lemmas 23.3 and 23.4 and complete the proof of this lemma.

## 24. Further Linking Theorems

LEMMA 24.1. If $p \in \pi_4, q \in \pi_3 \cup \pi_4$ and $q \sim p$, then $\pi(q) \subseteq \pi(p)$.

Proof. If q = p, there is nothing to prove, so suppose  $q \neq p$ . Corollary 19.1 implies that  $q \in \pi_{3}$ . Let  $r \sim q$ ,  $r \neq q$ ,  $r \neq p$ . We must show that  $r \sim p$ .

If $r \in \pi_4$ and $\mathfrak{D}$ is a $S_q$-subgroup of $\mathfrak{G}$, then $\mathfrak{D}$ does not centralize every element of $\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{D}; p)$ and $\mathfrak{D}$ does not centralize every element of $\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{D}; r)$. By Theorem 22.2, we have $p \sim r$.

If $r \in \pi_3$, then since also $q \in \pi_3$, we have $r \sim p$, by Theorem 22.7. This completes the proof of this lemma.

If $p \in \pi_4$ and $p_1 \in \pi(p)$, $p_1 \neq p$, let $\pi(p_1) = \{p, p_1, \cdots, p_n\}$. By Theorem 22.7 and Lemma 24.1, $\pi(p_i) = \pi(p_j)$, $1 \leq i, j \leq n$. It follows from $p \in \pi_4$ that $p_i \in \pi_3$, $1 \leq i \leq n$. By Theorem 22.5, $\mathfrak{G}$ satisfies $C_{\pi_3(p_1)}$. Let $\mathfrak{G}$ be a $S_{\pi_3(p_1)}$-subgroup of $\mathfrak{G}$. Clearly, $\mathfrak{G} \subset \mathfrak{G}$ since $p \notin \pi_3(p_1)$.

It is easy to see that $F(\mathfrak{H})$ is non cyclic. Choose $i$ so that the $S_{p_i}$-subgroup of $F(\mathfrak{H})$ is non cyclic. Let $\mathfrak{P}_1, \cdots, \mathfrak{P}_n$ be a Sylow system for $\mathfrak{H}, \mathfrak{P}_j$ being a $S_{p_j}$-subgroup of $\mathfrak{H}$. Thus, $\mathfrak{P}_i \cap F(\mathfrak{H})$ is non cyclic, so that $\mathfrak{P}_i \cap F(\mathfrak{H})$ contains a subgroup $\mathfrak{B}$ of type $(p, p)$ which is normal in $\mathfrak{P}_i$. Let $\mathfrak{A}$ be an element of $\mathcal{SCN}_3(\mathfrak{P}_i)$ which contains $\mathfrak{B}$. Let $\mathfrak{P}_0$ be a maximal element of $\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{A}; p)$. By Lemma 24.1 and Theorem 22.6, $\mathfrak{P}_0 \neq \langle 1 \rangle$. Let $C(\mathfrak{A}) = \mathfrak{A} \times \mathfrak{D}, \mathfrak{D}$ being a $p_i'$-group.

THEOREM 24.1. $\langle \mathfrak{P}_0, O_{p_i'}(\mathfrak{Q}), \mathfrak{D} \rangle$ is a $p_i'$-group.

Proof. Let $\mathcal{P}$ be the set of $\mathfrak{A}$-invariant subgroups $\widetilde{\mathfrak{P}}_0$ of $\mathfrak{P}_0$ such

that $\langle \tilde{\mathfrak{P}}_0, O_{p_i}(\mathfrak{D}), \mathfrak{D} \rangle \subset \mathfrak{G}$. Since $\langle O_{p_i}(\mathfrak{D}), \mathfrak{D} \rangle \subseteq C(\mathfrak{B})$, it follows that $\langle 1 \rangle \in \mathcal{P}$.

Suppose $\widetilde{\mathfrak{P}}_0\in \mathcal{P}$, and $\mathfrak{T} = \langle \widetilde{\mathfrak{P}}_0,O_{p_i}(\mathfrak{V}),\mathfrak{D}\rangle$. Since $\mathfrak{A}$ normalizes $\mathfrak{T},\langle \mathfrak{A},\mathfrak{T}\rangle = \mathfrak{A}\mathfrak{T} = \mathfrak{V}\subset \mathfrak{G}$. By Lemma 17.6, $\mathfrak{A}\subseteq O_{p_i',p_k}(\mathfrak{V})$.

Let $\mathfrak{A}$ be the image of $\mathfrak{A}$ under the projection of $O_{p_i', p_i'}(\mathfrak{V})$ onto $O_{p_i', p_i'}(\mathfrak{V}) / O_{p_i'}(\mathfrak{V})$. Since $\mathfrak{A} \cong \mathfrak{A}$, we see that $\mathfrak{A}$ is a self centralizing subgroup of $O_{p_i', p_i'}(\mathfrak{V}) / O_{p_i'}(\mathfrak{V})$, and it follows readily that $O_{p_i', p_i'}(\mathfrak{V}) / O_{p_i'}(\mathfrak{V})$ is centralized by $\widetilde{\mathfrak{P}}_0, O_{p_i'}(\mathfrak{V})$ and $\mathfrak{D}$. By Lemma 1.2.3 of [21], we have $\langle \widetilde{\mathfrak{P}}_0, O_{p_i'}(\mathfrak{V}), \mathfrak{D} \rangle \subseteq O_{p_i'}(\mathfrak{V})$ and hence $\mathfrak{T} = O_{p_i'}(\mathfrak{V})$ is a $p_i'$-group.

Let $\mathfrak{T}_1, \cdots, \mathfrak{T}_m$ be an $\mathfrak{A}$-invariant Sylow system of $\mathfrak{T}, \mathfrak{T}_j$ being a $S_{q_j}$-subgroup of $\mathfrak{T}$. If $q_j \in \{p_1, \cdots, p_n\}$, it follows from $C_{p_i,q_j}^*$ that $\mathfrak{T}_j$ is a maximal element of $\mathcal{N}(\mathfrak{A}; q_j)$. Since $\mathfrak{D} \subseteq \mathfrak{T}$, this implies that $O_{p_i}(\mathfrak{Q})$ is a $S$-subgroup of $\mathfrak{T}$. If $q_j \neq p, q_j \notin \{p_1, \cdots, p_n\}$, then Theorem 22.1 implies that $\mathfrak{A}$ centralizes $\mathfrak{T}_j$, so that $\mathfrak{T}_j \subseteq \mathfrak{D}$. Finally, if $q_j = p$, then there is an element $D$ of $\mathfrak{D}$ such that $\mathfrak{T}_j^D \subseteq \mathfrak{P}_0$, by Theorem 17.1.

Let $\mathfrak{R}$ be a fixed $S_{p'}$-subgroup of $\langle \mathfrak{D}, O_{p_i}(\mathfrak{H}) \rangle$. By the preceding paragraph, $\mathfrak{R}$ is a $S_{p'}$-subgroup of $\mathfrak{T}$, and $\mathfrak{P}_0 \cap \mathfrak{T}$ is a $S_p$-subgroup of $\mathfrak{T}$. Since $\widetilde{\mathfrak{P}}_0 \subseteq \mathfrak{P}_0 \cap \mathfrak{T}$, it follows that $\langle \widetilde{\mathfrak{P}}_0 | \widetilde{\mathfrak{P}}_0 \in \mathcal{P} \rangle = \mathfrak{P}^*$ is permutable with $\mathfrak{R}$ so that $\mathfrak{P}^*\mathfrak{R}$ is a proper $p_i'$-subgroup of $\mathfrak{G}$. This means that $\mathcal{P}$ contains a unique maximal element. Since $C_{\mathfrak{P}_0}(B)$ is $\mathfrak{A}$-invariant for each $B \in \mathfrak{B}'$, since $\mathfrak{P}_0 = \langle C_{\mathfrak{P}_0}(B) | B \in \mathfrak{B}' \rangle$, and since $\langle C_{\mathfrak{P}_0}(B), O_{p_i'}(\mathfrak{H}), \mathfrak{D} \rangle \subseteq C(B) \subset \mathfrak{G}$, the theorem follows.

THEOREM 24.2. Let $\mathfrak{R} = \langle \mathfrak{P}_0, O_{p_i}(\mathfrak{H}), \mathfrak{D} \rangle$, and $\mathfrak{M} = N(\mathfrak{R})$. Then $\mathfrak{M}$ contains $\mathfrak{H}, \mathfrak{M}$ is a maximal subgroup of $\mathfrak{G}$ and $\mathfrak{M}$ is the only maximal subgroup of $\mathfrak{G}$ containing $\mathfrak{P}_i$.

Proof. Since $\mathfrak{P}_0 \neq \langle 1 \rangle$, $\mathfrak{M}$ is a proper subgroup of $\mathfrak{G}$. We first show that $\mathfrak{M}$ contains $\mathfrak{P}_i$. Let $\mathfrak{D}$ be an $\mathfrak{A}$-invariant $S_q$-subgroup of $\mathfrak{R}$, so that $\mathfrak{D}$ is a maximal element of $\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{A}; q)$, either by virtue of $q \in \pi(p_i)$, or by virtue of $q \notin \pi(p_i)$ so that $\mathfrak{A}$ centralizes $\mathfrak{D}$. For $P$ in $\mathfrak{P}_i$, $\mathfrak{D}^P = \mathfrak{D}^D$ for some $D$ in $\mathfrak{D}$ by Theorem 17.1 together with $C(\mathfrak{A}) = \mathfrak{A} \times \mathfrak{D}$. Since $\mathfrak{D} \subseteq \mathfrak{R}$, $\mathfrak{D}^P$ is a $S_q$-subgroup of $\mathfrak{R}$. Hence, $\mathfrak{R}^P \subseteq \mathfrak{R}$, and so $\mathfrak{R}^P = \mathfrak{R}$. Thus, $\mathfrak{P}_i \subseteq \mathfrak{M}$.

To show that $\mathfrak{H} \subseteq \mathfrak{M}$, we use the fact that $\mathfrak{H} = O_{p_i}(\mathfrak{H}) \cdot N_{\mathfrak{H}}(\mathfrak{V})$, where $\mathfrak{V} = V(ccl_{\mathfrak{H}}(\mathfrak{A}); \mathfrak{P}_i)$. Since $O_{p_i}(\mathfrak{H}) \subseteq \mathfrak{R}$, it suffices to show that $N_{\mathfrak{H}}(\mathfrak{V}) \subseteq \mathfrak{M}$. We will in fact show that $N(\mathfrak{V}) \subseteq \mathfrak{M}$. Let $\mathfrak{Q}$ be a $\mathfrak{V}$-invariant $S_q$-subgroup of $\mathfrak{R}$. If $N \in N(\mathfrak{V})$, then $\mathfrak{A}^{N-1} \subseteq \mathfrak{V}$, so that $\mathfrak{A}^{N-1}$ normalizes $\mathfrak{Q}$. Hence, $\mathfrak{A}$ normalizes $\mathfrak{Q}^N = \mathfrak{Q}^D$, $D \in \mathfrak{D}$, and we see that $\mathfrak{R}^N = \mathfrak{R}$. Thus, $\mathfrak{M}$ contains $\mathfrak{H}$ and $N(\mathfrak{V})$.

Let $\mathfrak{M}_1$ be a maximal subgroup of $\mathfrak{G}$ containing $\mathfrak{M}$. It is easy to see that $\Re = O_{p_i}(\mathfrak{M}_1)$ by Lemma 7.3, so that $\mathfrak{M}_1 \subseteq \mathfrak{M}$, and $\mathfrak{M}$ is a maximal subgroup of $\mathfrak{G}$.

Let $\mathfrak{R}$ be any proper subgroup of $\mathfrak{G}$ containing $\mathfrak{P}_i$. To show that $\mathfrak{R} \subseteq \mathfrak{M}$, it suffices to treat the case that $\mathfrak{R}$ is a $q, p_i$-group. Let $\mathfrak{R}_q$ be a $S_q$-subgroup of $\mathfrak{R}$ permutable with $\mathfrak{P}_i$. Since $N(\mathfrak{B}) \subseteq \mathfrak{M}$, it suffices to show that $O_{p_i}(\mathfrak{R}) \subseteq \mathfrak{M}$. This is clear by $C_{p_i,p}^*$ if $q \in \{p_1, \cdots, p_n\}$. If $q = p$, this is also clear, by Theorem 17.1, since $C(\mathfrak{A}) \subseteq \mathfrak{M}$ and $\mathfrak{P}_0 \subseteq \mathfrak{M}$. If $q \notin \{p, p_1, \cdots, p_n\}$, then $\mathfrak{P}_i$ centralizes $O_q(\mathfrak{P}_i\mathfrak{R}_q)$ by Theorem 22.1, and we are done, since $C(\mathfrak{A}) \subseteq \mathfrak{M}$.

If $q \in \pi_3 \cup \pi_4$, and $\mathfrak{D}$ is a $S_q$-subgroup of $\mathfrak{G}$, we define

$\mathcal{A}_1(\mathfrak{Q}) = \{\mathfrak{Q}_0 | \mathfrak{Q}_0 \subseteq \mathfrak{Q}, \mathfrak{Q}_0 \text{ contains some element of } \mathcal{SCN}_3(\mathfrak{Q})\}$,

$\mathcal{A}_i(\mathfrak{D}) = \{\mathfrak{D}_0\mid \mathfrak{D}_0\subseteq \mathfrak{D},\mathfrak{D}_0$ contains a subgroup $\mathfrak{D}_1$ of type $(q,q)$

such that $C_{\mathfrak{Q}}(Q) \in \mathcal{A}_{i-1}(\mathfrak{Q})$ for each $Q$ in $\mathfrak{Q}_1\}$, $i = 2, 3, 4$.

LEMMA 24.2. If $q \in \pi_3 \cup \pi_4$ and $\mathfrak{D}$ is a $S_q$-subgroup of $\mathfrak{G}$, then every subgroup $\mathfrak{D}_0$ of $\mathfrak{D}$ which contains a subgroup of type $(q, q, q)$ is in $\mathscr{A}_3(\mathfrak{D})$.

Proof. Let $\mathfrak{B} \in \mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{D})$, $\mathfrak{D}_j^* = C_{\mathfrak{D}_0}(\mathfrak{B})$, so that $\mathfrak{D}_j^*$ is non cyclic. Let $\mathfrak{D}_1$ be a subgroup of $\mathfrak{D}_j^*$ of type $(q, q)$. If $Q \in \mathfrak{D}_1$, then $C_{\mathfrak{D}}(Q) \cong \mathfrak{B}$. Since $\mathfrak{B}$ is contained in an element of $\mathscr{SCN}_3(\mathfrak{D})$, it follows that $C_{\mathfrak{D}}(Q)$ is in $\mathscr{A}_2(\mathfrak{D})$.

THEOREM 24.3. If $q \in \pi_3, \mathfrak{D}$ is a $S_q$-subgroup of $\mathfrak{G}$, and $\mathfrak{D}$ is contained in a unique maximal subgroup of $\mathfrak{G}$, then each element of $\mathscr{A}_1(\mathfrak{D})$ is contained in a unique maximal subgroup of $\mathfrak{G}$.

Proof. Let $\mathfrak{M}$ be the unique maximal subgroup of $\mathfrak{G}$ containing $\mathfrak{Q}$. We remark that if this theorem is proved for the pair $(\mathfrak{Q}, \mathfrak{M})$, then it will also be proved for all pairs $(\mathfrak{Q}^{\mathfrak{M}}, \mathfrak{M})$ where $M \in \mathfrak{M}$. This prompts the following definition: $\mathcal{A}_1^*(\mathfrak{Q})$ is the set of all subgroups $\mathfrak{Q}_0$ of $\mathfrak{Q}$ such that $\mathfrak{Q}_0$ contains $\mathfrak{C}^{\mathfrak{M}}$ for some $\mathfrak{C}$ in $\mathcal{SCN}_3(\mathfrak{Q})$ and some $M \in \mathfrak{M}$. Clearly $\mathcal{A}_1(\mathfrak{Q}) \subseteq \mathcal{A}_1^*(\mathfrak{Q})$.

Suppose some element of $\mathcal{A}_1^*(\mathfrak{D})$ is contained in a maximal subgroup of $\mathfrak{G}$ different from $\mathfrak{M}$. Among all such elements $\mathfrak{D}_0$ of $\mathcal{A}_1^*(\mathfrak{D})$, let $|\mathfrak{D}_0|$ be maximal. By hypothesis, $\mathfrak{D}_0 \subset \mathfrak{D}$. Let $\mathfrak{M}_1$ be a maximal subgroup of $\mathfrak{G}$ different from $\mathfrak{M}$ which contains $\mathfrak{D}_0$ and let $\mathfrak{D}_0^*$ be a $S_q$-subgroup of $\mathfrak{M}_1$ which contains $\mathfrak{D}_0$. If $\mathfrak{D}_0 \subset \mathfrak{D}_0^*$, then $\mathfrak{D}_0 \subset N_{\mathfrak{D}_0^*}(\mathfrak{D}_0)$. Since $\mathfrak{D}_0 \subset N_{\mathfrak{D}}(\mathfrak{D}_0)$, maximality of $|\mathfrak{D}_0|$ implies that $N_{\mathfrak{G}}(\mathfrak{D}_0) \subseteq \mathfrak{M}$, so that $N_{\mathfrak{D}_0^*}(\mathfrak{D}_0) \subseteq \mathfrak{D}^M$ for some $M$ in $\mathfrak{M}$. Since $\mathfrak{M}_1 \neq \mathfrak{M}$, so also $\mathfrak{M}_1^{M-1} \neq \mathfrak{M}$. But $N_{\mathfrak{D}_0^*}(\mathfrak{D}_0)^{M-1} \in \mathcal{A}_1^*(\mathfrak{D})$, and maximality of $|\mathfrak{D}_0|$ is violated. Hence, $\mathfrak{D}_0$ is a $S_q$-subgroup of $\mathfrak{M}_1$.

Let $\mathfrak{C} \in \mathcal{SCN}_{3}(\mathfrak{D})$ be chosen so that $\mathfrak{C}^{M} \subseteq \mathfrak{D}_{0}$ for some $M \in \mathfrak{M}$. Since every element of $\mathfrak{H}(\mathfrak{C})$ is contained in $\mathfrak{M}$, every element of

$\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{C}^{\mathfrak{M}})$ is contained in $\mathfrak{M}^{\mathfrak{M}} = \mathfrak{M}$. Hence $O_{q'}(\mathfrak{M}_1) \subseteq \mathfrak{M}$. If $\mathfrak{B} = V(ccl_{\mathfrak{B}}(\mathfrak{C}); \mathfrak{Q}_0)$, then $\mathfrak{Q}_0 \subset N_{\mathfrak{D}}(\mathfrak{B})$, so $N_{\mathfrak{M}_1(\mathfrak{B})} \subseteq \mathfrak{M}$, by maximality of $|\mathfrak{Q}_0|$. Since $\mathfrak{M}_1 = O_{q'}(\mathfrak{M}_1) \cdot N_{\mathfrak{M}_1(\mathfrak{B})}$ by Lemma 17.6, we find that $\mathfrak{M}_1 \subseteq \mathfrak{M}$, contrary to assumption. The theorem is proved.

THEOREM 24.4. Let $q \in \pi_3 \cup \pi_4$, and let $\mathfrak{D}$ be a $S_q$-subgroup of $\mathfrak{G}$. If each element of $\mathcal{A}_1(\mathfrak{D})$ is contained in a unique maximal subgroup $\mathfrak{M}$ of $\mathfrak{G}$, then for each $i = 2, 3, 4$, and each element $\mathfrak{D}_0$ of $\mathcal{A}_i(\mathfrak{D})$, $\mathfrak{M}$ is the unique maximal subgroup of $\mathfrak{G}$ containing $\mathfrak{D}_0$.

Proof. For $i=2,3,4$, let $\mathscr{A}_{i}^{*}(\mathfrak{Q})$ be the set of subgroups $\mathfrak{Q}_{0}$ of $\mathfrak{Q}$ such that $\mathfrak{Q}_{0}$ contains a subgroup $\mathfrak{Q}_{1}$ of type $(q,q)$ such that $C_{\mathfrak{M}}(Q)$ contains an element of $\mathscr{A}_{i-1}^{*}(\mathfrak{Q}^{M})$ for some $M\in\mathfrak{M}$ and all $Q\in\mathfrak{Q}_{1}$. Here $\mathscr{A}_{i}^{*}(\mathfrak{Q}^{M})$ denotes the set of $\mathfrak{Q}_{0}^{M},\mathfrak{Q}_{0}\in\mathscr{A}_{i}^{*}(\mathfrak{Q})$. Suppose $i=2,3$, or 4 is minimal with the property that some element of $\mathscr{A}_{i}^{*}(\mathfrak{Q})$ is contained in at least two maximal subgroups of $\mathfrak{G}$. This implies that $\mathscr{A}_{i-1}^{*}(\mathfrak{Q}^{M})$ does not contain any elements which are contained in two maximal subgroups of $\mathfrak{G},M$ being an arbitrary element of $\mathfrak{M}$. Choose $\mathfrak{Q}_{0}$ in $\mathscr{A}_{i}^{*}(\mathfrak{Q})$ with $|\mathfrak{Q}_{0}|$ maximal subject to the condition that $\mathfrak{Q}_{0}$ is contained in a maximal subgroup $\mathfrak{M}_{1}$ of $\mathfrak{G}$ with $\mathfrak{M}_{1}\neq\mathfrak{M}$. We see that $\mathfrak{Q}_{0}$ is a $S_{q}$-subgroup of $\mathfrak{M}_{1}$. Let $\mathfrak{Q}_{1}$ be a subgroup of $\mathfrak{Q}_{0}$ of type $(q,q)$ with the property that $C_{\mathfrak{M}}(Q)$ contains an element of $\mathscr{A}_{i-1}^{*}(\mathfrak{Q}^{M})$ for suitable $M$ in $\mathfrak{M}$, and each $Q$ in $\mathfrak{Q}_{1}$. (We allow $M$ to depend on $Q$.) Since $O_{q^{\prime}}(\mathfrak{M}_{1})$ is generated by its subgroups $O_{q^{\prime}}(\mathfrak{M}_{1})\cap C(Q),Q\in\mathfrak{Q}_{1}^{\sharp}$, it follows that $O_{q^{\prime}}(\mathfrak{M}_{1})\subseteq\mathfrak{M}$.

Let $\mathfrak{C}$ be an element of $\mathcal{S}\mathcal{E}\mathcal{N}_{3}(\mathfrak{Q})$. Then $\mathfrak{C} \not\subseteq \mathfrak{Q}_0$, or we are done. Let $\widetilde{\mathfrak{Q}}_0 = \mathfrak{Q}_0 \cap O_{q'}_q(\mathfrak{M}_1)$. Since $\widetilde{\mathfrak{Q}}_0 \cap \mathfrak{C} = \mathfrak{Q}_0 \cap \mathfrak{C}$ by (B), it follows that $N_{\mathfrak{Q}}(\widetilde{\mathfrak{Q}}_0) \supset \mathfrak{Q}_0$. Hence, $N(\widetilde{\mathfrak{Q}}_0) \subseteq \mathfrak{M}$, by maximality of $|\mathfrak{Q}_0|$. Since $\mathfrak{M}_1 = O_{q'}(\mathfrak{M}_1) \cdot N_{\mathfrak{M}_1}(\widetilde{\mathfrak{Q}}_0)$, we have $\mathfrak{M}_1 \subseteq \mathfrak{M}$, completing the proof of this theorem, since $\mathcal{A}_i(\mathfrak{Q}) \subseteq \mathcal{A}_i^*(\mathfrak{Q})$, $i = 2, 3, 4$.

THEOREM 24.5. If $q \in \pi_3$ and $\mathfrak{D}$ is a $S_q$-subgroup of $\mathfrak{G}$, then $\mathfrak{D}$ is contained in a unique maximal subgroup of $\mathfrak{G}$.

Proof. If  $\pi(q) \subseteq \pi_{3}$ , this theorem follows from Lemma 23.5. Suppose  $p \in \pi(q) \cap \pi_{4}$ . Let  $\pi(q) = \{p, p_{1}, \cdots, p_{n}\}$ , where  $q = p_{1}$ , and let  $\mathfrak{D}$  be a  $S_{\pi_{3}(p_{1})}$ -subgroup of G containing Q. If  $\mathfrak{D} \cap F(\mathfrak{H})$  is non cyclic, we are done by Theorem 24.2, so we suppose that  $\mathfrak{D} \cap F(\mathfrak{H})$  is cyclic.

Let $\mathfrak{M}$ be the unique maximal subgroup of $\mathfrak{G}$ containing $\mathfrak{H}$. Suppose we are able to show that $C(\mathfrak{G}) \subseteq \mathfrak{M}$ for some $\mathfrak{G}$ in $\mathcal{SCH}_{\mathfrak{g}}(\mathfrak{Q})$. Since $F(\mathfrak{H}) \cap \mathfrak{Q}$ is cyclic, $F(\mathfrak{M}) \cap \mathfrak{Q}$ is also cyclic. Hence, $O_{q}(\mathfrak{M}) \neq 1$. If $\mathfrak{B} = V(ccl_{\mathfrak{G}}(\mathfrak{G}); \mathfrak{Q})$, then $N(\mathfrak{B})$ normalizes $O_{q'}(\mathfrak{M})$, by Theorem 17.1,

together with $C(\mathfrak{C}) \subseteq \mathfrak{M}$. Since $\mathfrak{R} = O_{q'}(\mathfrak{R}) \cdot N_{\mathfrak{R}}(\mathfrak{B})$ for every proper subgroup $\mathfrak{R}$ of $\mathfrak{G}$ which contains $\mathfrak{Q}$, it suffices to show that every element of $\mathcal{H}(\mathfrak{Q})$ is contained in $\mathfrak{M}$. This follows readily by $C_{q,p_j}^*$, Theorem 22.1 and $C(\mathfrak{C}) \subseteq \mathfrak{M}$.

Thus, it suffices to show that $C(\mathfrak{C}) \subseteq \mathfrak{M}$. Choose $i$ such that $\mathfrak{F}_i$, a $S_{p_i}$-subgroup of $F(\mathfrak{G})$, is non cyclic, and let $\mathfrak{P}_i$ be a $S_{p_i}$-subgroup of $\mathfrak{G}$ permutable with $\mathfrak{D}$. It suffices to show that $C_{\mathfrak{F}_i}(C) \in \mathcal{A}_i(\mathfrak{P}_i)$ for some $C \in \mathfrak{C}^*$, by Theorems 24.3 and 24.4 together with the fact that $\mathfrak{M}$ is the unique maximal subgroup of $\mathfrak{G}$ containing $\mathfrak{P}_i$.

Let $\mathfrak{F}_i^* = O_{p_i}(\mathfrak{Q}\mathfrak{P}_i)$, so that $\mathfrak{F}_i^*$ is a maximal element of $\mathcal{N}(\mathfrak{Q}; p_i)$. By Lemma 17.3, $\mathfrak{Q} \subseteq N(\mathfrak{F}_i^*)'$. Since $\mathfrak{P}_i$ is contained in $\mathfrak{M}$ and no other maximal subgroup, $\mathfrak{Q} \subseteq \mathfrak{M}'$. Thus, if $\Omega_1(Z_2(\mathfrak{F}_i))$ is generated by two elements, then $\mathfrak{Q}$ centralizes $Z_2(\mathfrak{F}_i)$ and we are done. If $Z(\mathfrak{F}_i)$ is non cyclic, then every subgroup of $Z(\mathfrak{F}_i)$ of type $(p_i, p_i)$ is contained in $\mathscr{A}_4(\mathfrak{P}_i)$. Since $\mathfrak{C}$ contains a subgroup of type $(q, q, q)$, $C(C) \cap Z(\mathfrak{F}_i)$ is non cyclic for some $C$ in $\mathfrak{C}^*$, and we are done in this case. There remains the possibility that $Z(\mathfrak{F}_i)$ is cyclic, while $\Omega_1(Z_2(\mathfrak{F}_i))$ is not generated by two elements. Since every subgroup of $\Omega_1(Z_2(\mathfrak{F}_i))$ of type $(p_i, p_i)$ which contains $\Omega_1(Z(\mathfrak{F}_i))$ is contained in $\mathscr{A}_4(\mathfrak{P}_i)$, by Lemma 24.2, and since $C(C)$ contains such a subgroup for some $C$ in $\mathfrak{C}^*$, we are done.

The preceding theorems give precise information regarding the  $S_{q}$ -subgroups of the maximal subgroups of G for q in  $\pi_{s}$ .

THEOREM 24.6. Let $q \in \pi_3$ and let $\mathfrak{M}$ be a maximal subgroup of $\mathfrak{G}$. If $\mathfrak{D}$ is a $S_q$-subgroup of $\mathfrak{M}$ and $\mathfrak{Q}$ is not a $S_q$-subgroup of $\mathfrak{G}$, then $\mathfrak{Q}$ contains a cyclic subgroup of index at most $q$.

Proof. Let $\mathfrak{Q}^*$ be a $S_q$-subgroup of $\mathfrak{G}$ containing $\mathfrak{Q}$, let $\mathfrak{B} \in \mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{Q}^*)$ and let $\mathfrak{Q}_0 = C_{\mathfrak{Q}}(\mathfrak{B})$ so that $|\mathfrak{Q}: \mathfrak{Q}_0| = 1$ or $q$. If $\mathfrak{Q}_0$ is non cyclic, then $\mathfrak{Q}_0 \in \mathcal{A}_4(\mathfrak{Q}^*)$, and so $\mathfrak{Q}_0$ is contained in a unique maximal subgroup of $\mathfrak{G}$, which must be $\mathfrak{M}$, since $\mathfrak{Q}_0 \subseteq \mathfrak{M}$. But $\mathfrak{Q}^* \not\subseteq \mathfrak{M}$, a contradiction, so $\mathfrak{Q}_0$ is cyclic, as required.

Theorem 24.6 is of interest in its own right, and plays an important role in the study of  $\pi_{4}$ , to which all the preceding results are now turned.

<div class="docvortex-algorithm" style="white-space: pre-wrap; font-family:monospace;">
Hypothesis 24.1.
1.  $3 \in \pi_{4}$ .
2. P is a  $S_{s}$ -subgroup of G.
3. R is a proper subgroup of G such that
(i)  $P \subseteq R$ .
(ii) If  $\mathfrak{H} = O_{\mathfrak{s}}(\mathfrak{R})$ , there is a subgroup C of H chosen in
</div>

accordance with Lemma 8.2 such that  $Z(\mathfrak{C})$  is generated by two elements.

THEOREM 24.7. Under Hypothesis 24.1, $\mathfrak{P}$ is contained in a unique maximal subgroup $\mathfrak{M}$ of $\mathfrak{G}$, and $\mathfrak{M}$ centralizes $Z(\mathfrak{P})$.

Proof. Let $\mathfrak{L}$ be any proper subgroup of $\mathfrak{G}$ containing $\mathfrak{P}$. We must show that $\mathfrak{L}$ centralizes $Z(\mathfrak{P})$.

By Lemma 8.2, $\ker (\mathfrak{R}\to \operatorname {Aut}\mathfrak{C})$ is a 3-group, so is contained in $\mathfrak{H}$. It follows that $C_{\mathfrak{R}}(\mathfrak{C}) = Z(\mathfrak{C})$ and in particular $C_{\mathfrak{R}}(\mathfrak{C}) = Z(\mathfrak{C})$.

Suppose $\mathfrak{C} \subseteq O_{3}(\mathfrak{V})$. Then $Z(O_{3}(\mathfrak{V})) \subseteq C_{\mathfrak{P}}(\mathfrak{C}) \subseteq Z(\mathfrak{C})$, so $Z(O_{3}(\mathfrak{V}))$ is generated by two elements. Since $|\mathfrak{V}|$ is odd, a $S_{3}$-subgroup of $\mathfrak{V}$ centralizes $Z(O_{3}(\mathfrak{V}))$, so centralizes $Z(\mathfrak{P})$. Since $\mathfrak{P}$ also centralizes $Z(\mathfrak{P})$, we have $\mathfrak{V} \subseteq C(Z(\mathfrak{P}))$.

Suppose $\mathfrak{C} \not\subseteq O_{3}(\mathfrak{L})$. Since $Z(\mathfrak{C})$ is a normal abelian subgroup of $\mathfrak{P}$ we have $Z(\mathfrak{C}) \subseteq O_{3}(\mathfrak{L})$. Since $\gamma^{2}\mathfrak{P}\mathfrak{C}^{2} \subseteq Z(\mathfrak{C})$, we conclude that $\mathfrak{C} \subseteq O_{3,3',3}(\mathfrak{L})$. By the preceding paragraph, $N(\mathfrak{P} \cap O_{3,3',3}(\mathfrak{L}))$ centralizes $Z(\mathfrak{P})$. Thus, it suffices to show that $\mathfrak{P}O_{3,3'}(\mathfrak{L}) = \mathfrak{L}_{1}$ centralizes $Z(\mathfrak{P})$. Since $\mathfrak{L}_{1} = N_{\mathfrak{L}_{1}}(O_{3}(\mathfrak{L})\mathfrak{C}) \cdot [O_{3,3'}(\mathfrak{L}), \mathfrak{C}]$, and since $\mathfrak{P}$ normalizes $O_{3}(\mathfrak{L})\mathfrak{C}$, it suffices to show that $[O_{3,3'}(\mathfrak{L}), \mathfrak{C}]$ centralizes $Z(\mathfrak{P})$. Let $3 = Z(O_{3}(\mathfrak{L}))$, so that $3$ contains $Z(\mathfrak{P})$. Since $3$ is a normal abelian subgroup of $\mathfrak{P}$, (B) implies that $3 \subseteq O_{3}(\mathfrak{R})$. Hence, $\gamma^{2}\mathfrak{B}\mathfrak{C}^{2} = 1$, which implies that $[O_{3,3'}(\mathfrak{L}), \mathfrak{C}]$ induces only 3-automorphisms on $3$, and suffices to complete the proof.

Hypothesis 24.2.

1. $3 \in \pi_{4}$.

2. $\mathfrak{P}$ is a $S_{3}$-subgroup of $\mathfrak{G}$.

3. If $\mathfrak{R}$ is any proper subgroup of $\mathfrak{G}$ containing $\mathfrak{P}$, and if $\mathfrak{H} = O_{\mathfrak{s}}(\mathfrak{R})$, then every subgroup $\mathfrak{C}$ of $\mathfrak{H}$ chosen in accordance with Lemma 8.2 satisfies $m(Z(\mathfrak{C})) \geq 3$.

REMARK. If  $3 \in \pi_{4}$ , then Hypothesis 24.1 and Hypothesis 24.2 exhaust all possibilities.

LEMMA 24.3. Under Hypothesis 24.2, $\mathfrak{P}$ contains an element $\mathfrak{B}$ of $\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{P})$ such that the normal closure of $\mathfrak{B}$ in $C(\Omega_1(Z(\mathfrak{P})))$ is abelian.

Proof. If $Z(\mathfrak{P})$ is non cyclic, every element of $\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{P})$ satisfies this lemma.
